Start here1,080 words · README.md

Course: ITSY 1377, Agentic AI and Security Operations Institution: Northeast Lakeview College, Cyber Defense program Prepared: 2026-09-04 Audience: the program manager. Nothing here assumes you are building the course.

This is the design for an eight-week online course in which students build one working security tool, a week at a time. The tool is a phishing email triage assistant. By week 7 it reads a reported email, gathers facts, decides what to check next, writes a note another analyst can act on, and is measured for accuracy over twenty emails.

The corpus this course runs on was approved on 2026-09-03. This blueprint is the second of three stages. Building the course follows approval of this one.

What to read, depending on how much time you have

Ten minutes. This page, then the coverage memo, which answers how the course covers the eight outcomes you circulated.

An hour. Add the alignment matrix, the week 5 model test, and the assumptions and gaps.

Everything. The sixteen documents listed below, about 83,000 words.

The nine things you asked for

# Item Where it is
1 Alignment matrix and the Canvas Outcomes plan alignment-matrix.md
2 Survey content plan for the four breadth topics, with both discussion prompts in full survey-content-plan.md
3 A specification per week week-01-spec.md through week-07-spec.md
4 The week 5 sidebar and lab, written in full week-05-spec.md, sections 5 and 15
5 Week 5 model test results and a recommendation ../week5-model-test.md
6 Week 0 outline and its self-assessment week-00-outline.md
7 Week 8 exam blueprint exam-blueprint.md
8 Every conflict between the institutional template and the build brief template-conflicts.md
9 Every assumption and every gap assumptions-and-gaps.md

Three supporting documents carry numbers that belong to the whole course rather than to one week. code-ledger.md fixes what code exists by the end of each week. grade-distribution-and-tokens.md sets points and token budgets. And coverage-memo.md is addressed to you.

The seven decisions worth your attention

1. One capability a week, and the agent works every week. Week 1 calls the model from code. Week 2 gets a strict answer out of it. Week 3 stops sending the raw email and starts sending extracted facts. Week 4 gives it three checks it can run. Week 5 lets it choose which check to run next.

Week 6 defends it against an email written to fool it. Week 7 makes it write a useful note and measures how often it is right. Nothing is rebuilt. A student who could not finish week 4 can still start week 5.

2. The stop condition lives in the student's code, not in the model. This was measured, not assumed. Left to decide for itself, the model picks the right first check almost every time and then keeps going after it already has the answer. Moving the stop into six lines the student writes closed that gap completely, and it is also the better lesson.

3. Granite 4.2 3B, with its reasoning mode switched off. In the design above it produced the exact expected sequence of checks in 29 runs out of 30. Reasoning mode costs 83 seconds and about 7,900 tokens for one email, against 4.1 seconds and 3,813 tokens with it off. Granite 4.1 3B is the documented fallback.

4. Every lab is worth 100 points, and the rubrics are gradeable from three things. A screenshot, one exported file, and three to five sentences. No instructor ever runs a student's notebook.

5. The breadth topics are carried by reading, discussion and the exam, not by labs. Four topics the course must cover are wider than the phishing tool. They are taught in the week 1 and week 7 readings, assessed in discussions, self-checks and the final exam, and they get no learning objective and no Canvas Outcome. The coverage memo says plainly which of your eight outcomes this applies to.

6. Week 0 is optional, ungraded, and open all term. Students arrive from a non-technical prerequisite with no Python. Week 0 teaches exactly what weeks 1 to 7 need and nothing else, using everyday examples rather than security ones. It opens with a self-assessment that tells a student plainly whether to work through it or skip it.

7. Cost is reported as a break-even price, because per-token cost is not yet known. One clean pass through all seven labs is about 388,000 tokens per student. At the advisory figure of $5 per student, that fits if the model costs under $12.89 per million tokens. If every lab is rerun once, which is the case to plan for, the price has to be under $6.44.

What is not settled

assumptions-and-gaps.md holds all of it, in four groups: what this build is proceeding on, what needs a decision from you, what needs a decision from the college, and what needs a measurement that something is currently blocking.

The four that matter most:

  • Late work and resubmission policy. Not set. It was outside the brief.
  • Three institutional template requirements conflict with the rule that this course uses no external services and needs no installs. They are identity verification, a lockdown browser, and an orientation course requirement. The college decides these, not this build.
  • Forty-four of the 56 corpus emails are not yet rendered. Twelve are. The rest are specified and generate from the same manifest.
  • The readings are planned but not written, so their citations are not yet verified to resolve and to be openly licensed.

How the work was checked

Three independent audits ran against this blueprint. One checked coherence across weeks. One recomputed every number. One measured every page for reading level and checked that every term was defined before use.

They found 62 problems and all 62 were fixed. Among them: seven different lab point totals, a missing grade distribution, seven sidebars sharing three subjects, and the week 5 lab having been described rather than written out.

The model test was run twice. The first run was thrown away because an authoring job regenerated four of the six test emails while the measurement was reading them. The measurement records a hash of every file it reads, which is how that was caught rather than reported as a result.

Coverage memo, addressed to you1,225 words · coverage-memo.md

To: Program manager, Cyber Defense program Course: Agentic AI and Security Operations (ITSY 1377), Northeast Lakeview College Date: 2026-09-03

Why this memo exists

You circulated eight suggested learning outcomes. They are not adopted and are not the course's objectives. The course's objectives are the six course learning objectives (CLOs) in the build prompt, and every rubric and Canvas Outcome is built on those six.

You asked to see how the course covers your list anyway. For each of your eight: where it is taught, how it is assessed, and how deep the coverage goes. This memo reports; it proposes no changes.

The depth words used here

  • Built and assessed in a lab. A rubric criterion linked to a Canvas Outcome scores it.
  • Practiced in a lab but assessed indirectly. The scored criterion measures a nearby judgment.
  • Carried by survey reading and discussion only. No lab. A discussion, self-check, or the final exam scores it.
  • Stated as policy, not assessed. Nothing scores it.

The eight

1. Role of AI, generative AI, and agentic workflows

Taught: week 1 survey reading, part two. It defines three kinds of AI, with two examples of each from IT or security work outside phishing. Breadth topic B1.

Assessed: the week 1 graded discussion, where each student names a place they met AI at work and says which kind it was. Also two week 1 self-check items and the final exam. No rubric criterion here links to a Canvas Outcome.

Depth: carried by survey reading and discussion only.

2. Common AI-assisted security use cases

Taught: week 7 survey reading, part three. One paragraph each on alert triage, log review, phishing analysis, documentation, scripting help, vulnerability research, and incident response support. Breadth topic B2.

Assessed: the week 7 graded discussion, where each student picks a use case other than phishing and names one change their own agent would need. Also four self-check items and the final exam.

Depth: split. The course builds one of your seven. Phishing analysis is the whole build arc, weeks 1 to 7, built and assessed in labs. The other six are carried by survey reading and discussion only.

3. Security operations components

Taught: week 1 survey reading, part one. It defines assets, users, networks, endpoints, logs, alerts, tickets, incidents, escalation, and documentation. A diagram shows a reported email becoming an alert, then a ticket, then an incident. Breadth topic B3.

Assessed: three week 1 self-check items, part of the week 1 discussion, and the final exam.

Depth: carried by survey reading and discussion only.

4. Safe prompting, data minimization, and responsible use

Four things at four depths, reported apart.

Using AI tools for security tasks. Every lab, weeks 1 to 7. Built and assessed in a lab.

Data minimization. Taught in the week 3 reading, "What you put in the prompt is what you expose." Students choose which facts go into the prompt. Week 3 rubric criterion 4 scores a written answer naming one fact the student left out, and why the verdict did not need it. It links to the CLO1 Outcome. Built and assessed in a lab.

Safe prompting. Taught in week 2 as schema validation with a retry, and in week 6 as delimiting, treating body text as data, and checking output against facts. The scored criteria measure the validation record and the judgment about the guardrails, not the prompt wording. Practiced in a lab but assessed indirectly.

Responsible use. It sits in the AI use policy and syllabus, which permit AI use with disclosure. The syllabus acknowledgment is ungraded, and no rubric criterion scores disclosure. Stated as policy, not assessed.

5. Extract and organize technical facts

Taught: the week 3 lab. Students build a facts block from parsed headers, the three authentication results, display name against sender domain, and link hosts.

Assessed: week 3 rubric criterion 2, "Fact extraction," the primary evidence for CLO1. Criteria 3 and 4 support it.

Depth: for emails and URLs, built and assessed in a lab. Your outcome also names logs, alerts, system messages, and tickets. The week 3 reading names the same pattern for those, and one self-check item asks about a log line. Students never extract from a log or a ticket, so that part is survey reading and a self-check only.

6. Evaluate AI-generated recommendations

Taught: week 2, checking output against a schema. Week 6, checking it against extracted facts and escalating below a confidence threshold. Week 7, scoring twenty results against ground truth.

Assessed: three Outcome-linked criteria, all primary evidence: week 2 criterion 2 for CLO2, week 6 criterion 2 for CLO4, week 7 criterion 3 for CLO6.

Depth: built and assessed in labs, the best covered item on your list. Accuracy, completeness, and safety each have a scored home. Relevance does not; it is discussed, never scored.

7. Common AI risks in security operations

Taught: prompt injection is the week 6 lab, with payloads in six positions, a word-for-word control email, and a benign decoy. Hallucination, data exposure, privacy, automation bias, and overreliance are the week 6 second reading page, breadth topic B4. Each is tied to an email students ran.

Assessed: injection through week 6 rubric criterion 2, which links to the CLO4 Outcome. Of the other five risks, one is carried by a single week 6 self-check item and one by the week 6 discussion. All five are carried by the final exam, which gives breadth topic B4 five of its fifteen breadth items, the largest share of any topic.

Depth: injection is built and assessed in a lab. The other five risks are carried by survey reading, a self-check, and the final exam.

8. Document and communicate for both audiences

Taught: week 7. The agent writes a triage note in a fixed format: verdict, confidence, indicators, recommended action, and escalate yes or no. The note also carries a two-sentence plain-language summary for a nontechnical reader, such as the employee who reported the email.

Assessed: week 7 rubric criterion 2, "Triage note," the primary evidence for CLO5. Its wording requires the summary to be readable by someone with no security background, which is where the nontechnical audience is scored.

Depth: built and assessed in a lab. One limit: the course produces one note format, not tickets or reports.

Summary table

Your outcome Carried by Depth
1. Role of AI and agentic workflows B1 Survey only
2. Common AI use cases B2; CLOs for phishing One of seven in labs; six survey only
3. Security operations components B3 Survey only
4. Prompting, minimization, responsible use CLO1, CLO2, CLO4; nothing for responsible use Mixed; see below
5. Extract technical facts CLO1 Emails and URLs in a lab; other sources survey only
6. Evaluate AI recommendations CLO2, CLO4, CLO6 Labs
7. Common AI risks CLO4 for injection, B4 for the rest Injection in a lab; five risks survey only
8. Document and communicate CLO5 Lab

The one gap. Responsible use, inside your outcome 4, has no CLO and no breadth topic. It lives in the AI use policy, and nothing scores it.

Week 5 model test and recommendation1,565 words · week5-model-test.md

Course: Agentic AI and Security Operations (ITSY 1377), Northeast Lakeview College, Cyber Defense program
Phase 1 blueprint, item 5. Prepared 2026-09-04.
Audience: the program manager. Nothing here assumes you are building the course.

Week 5 is the week this course is most likely to fail. The agent has to choose its next check from what it just learned, stop when it has enough, and never run away. That is the least certain behaviour on a small model. It is also the hardest week for students, who arrive with no programming background. So it was measured before the lab was written.

What was measured

294 complete agent runs. Each run is one email triaged from start to finish, so a run is several model calls, not one. The six emails are the six the week 5 lab uses, one per loop shape. Every run was scored against the corpus record for that email: the tools it should call, the order, the reason the run should end, and the verdict.

Four lab designs were tested, because the point of the exercise was to find one that works rather than to grade a single guess.

Design What the model decides
free the model picks any tool and decides for itself when to stop
guided the sender check is run for the student, the model picks at most one more tool and decides when to stop
coded the model picks the next tool, the student's code decides when to stop
coded anchored as coded, plus one line telling the model to start with the sender check

The world, asserted before measuring

  • Serving stack: ollama version is 0.32.15, OpenAI-compatible endpoint at http://localhost:11434/v1.
  • Model granite4.1:3b, blob digest 6fd349357287c7ff.
  • Model granite4.2:3b, blob digest 40577dc168a3a9ad.
  • Step cap: 4. One model request at a time, never two.
  • Tool results: Tool results come from spec/corpus-spec.json tool_expectations, which lab/tools/check_tools.py asserts the shipped tools reproduce exactly.
  • Every email file still carries the exact digest recorded when it was read. The measurement is against the files that ship.

That last check is not decoration. An earlier sweep of 234 runs was thrown away because an authoring job regenerated four of the six emails while the measurement was reading them. The digests caught it.

The result

Configuration Model Design Right tool path Right stop reason Right verdict Valid answer Tokens per run Seconds per run
granite4.1-free granite4.1:3b free 15/30 not applicable 25/30 30/30 4,791 2.9
granite4.1-guided granite4.1:3b guided 15/30 not applicable 25/30 30/30 2,508 1.9
granite4.2-noreason-free granite4.2:3b reasoning off free 10/30 not applicable 21/30 30/30 6,508 4.8
granite4.2-noreason-guided granite4.2:3b reasoning off guided 20/30 not applicable 21/30 30/30 2,246 2.2
granite4.2-reason-free granite4.2:3b reasoning on, 512 token budget free 1/18 not applicable 0/18 0/18 2,224 10.7
granite4.1-coded granite4.1:3b coded 0/30 30/30 20/30 30/30 3,432 2.7
granite4.2-noreason-coded granite4.2:3b reasoning off coded 29/30 30/30 28/30 29/30 3,813 4.1
granite4.1-coded-anchored granite4.1:3b coded anchored 15/30 30/30 25/30 30/30 3,268 2.4
granite4.2-noreason-coded-anchored granite4.2:3b reasoning off coded anchored 27/30 28/30 28/30 28/30 3,774 3.8
granite4.2-reason-free-4096 granite4.2:3b reasoning on, 4096 token budget free 9/18 not applicable 12/18 12/18 8,108 81.0
granite4.2-reason-coded-4096 granite4.2:3b reasoning on, 4096 token budget coded 13/18 18/18 16/18 17/18 7,639 84.6

Right tool path means the exact tools in the exact order the corpus record expects. Right stop reason applies only to the designs where code owns the stop, and means the run ended for the reason the corpus records. Valid answer means the final reply parsed as the JSON the lab asks for.

What the numbers say

1. The models choose tools well and stop badly

Across the runs where the model was free to choose, it picked the correct first tool 25/30 times but produced the correct whole path only 15/30 times. The gap is the model not stopping once it had enough. On the email whose sender is already known bad, it ran the link check anyway, reached the right verdict, and spent an extra step doing it.

2. Moving the stop condition into code fixes it

When a deterministic rule in the student's own code decides when to stop, the run ends for the right reason 136/138 times across every model and every email. The model's remaining job, choosing the next check, is the part it is already good at. This is the design the week 5 spec already describes, and the measurement supports it.

3. One configuration is clearly the one to ship

granite4.2-noreason-coded: granite4.2:3b with reasoning off, in the design where code owns the stop. It produced the exact expected tool path 29/30, the right stop reason 30/30, and the right verdict 28/30, at 3,813 tokens and 4.1 seconds per triage.

4. Reasoning mode is not usable for this course

At the 512-token answer budget the labs use, Granite 4.2 in its default reasoning mode spent the whole budget thinking and returned empty content 18/18 times. That is a configuration incompatibility, not a limit of the model, so it was re-tested at eight times the budget.

At 4,096 tokens it works, and it still should not be used. Across 36 runs it averaged 7,874 tokens and 83 seconds for a single triage, against 3,813 tokens and 4.1 seconds for the recommended configuration. A student waiting a minute and a half per email will assume the notebook has hung.

Per email

Email g4.1-free g4.1-guided g4.2-free g4.2-guided g4.2-reason-free g4.1-coded g4.2-coded g4.1-coded-anchored g4.2-coded-anchored g4.2-reason-free-4096 g4.2-reason-coded-4096
004-cfo-quick-task 5/5 5/5 3/5 0/5 0/3 0/5 5/5 0/5 5/5 0/3 3/3
005-slatemoor-past-due-statement 5/5 5/5 0/5 5/5 0/3 0/5 5/5 5/5 5/5 3/3 2/3
007-larkseal-contract-signature 0/5 0/5 0/5 5/5 0/3 0/5 5/5 0/5 5/5 0/3 3/3
010-ferncrate-rfq-share 5/5 5/5 1/5 5/5 1/3 0/5 5/5 5/5 4/5 2/3 1/3
011-po-20431-thread-reply 0/5 0/5 1/5 5/5 0/3 0/5 5/5 5/5 4/5 3/3 3/3
012-elmhollow-badge-access 0/5 0/5 5/5 0/5 0/3 0/5 4/5 0/5 4/5 1/3 1/3

Cells are right tool paths out of trials.

Two findings about the corpus, not the models

Email 012 splits the configurations, and only the recommended one gets it right. It is a real vendor on a new domain, with a link host on neither list, asking for names. Most configurations read that as an attack. The recommended configuration answered correctly 4/5; every other configuration together answered correctly 9/44, and Granite 4.1 called it malicious in all 20 of its runs. So this is a genuine false positive for the weaker designs and a case the shipped design usually survives. Week 5 still grades the loop rather than the verdict on it, because the verdict is not stable across models, and week 7 should expect it in the false positive column.

Email 012's recorded stop reason was wrong and is now fixed. The corpus said the run should end at the step cap. Only three tools exist under a cap of four, so a well-behaved agent runs out of tools first. Every run confirms it. The corpus record, its ground truth entry and the week 5 spec now all read tools_exhausted. The step cap is still taught, through the deliberately uncapped run the lab already includes.

Recommendation

Standardise on granite4.2:3b with reasoning switched off, and keep the week 5 design in which the student's code owns the stop condition. Set reasoning_effort to none in the notebook configuration cell, gated by model name, because Granite 4.1 rejects that field with an HTTP 400.

Keep Granite 4.1 3B as the fallback, with one caveat stated in the instructor guide. In the same design it ends the run for the right reason every time, but it chooses a different tool order, so a rubric that grades tool order would fail students for the model's behaviour. The week 5 rubric grades the stop reason and the trace, not the order, which is what makes either model safe to teach on.

Re-run this measurement every term. The command is python3 builder/lab/eval/week5_loop_test.py --all. It asserts the endpoint, the model digests and the hash of every email before it measures, and writes its results after every run.

Alignment matrix and Canvas Outcomes4,835 words · alignment-matrix.md

Course: Agentic AI and Security Operations (ITSY 1377), Northeast Lakeview College, Cyber Defense program Phase 1 blueprint, item 1. Prepared 2026-09-03. Audience: the program manager. No part of this document assumes you are building the course.

This file holds two deliverables. Part 1 is the alignment matrix: the six course learning objectives, the national framework tasks each one maps to, the week that teaches it, and the graded work that proves it. Part 2 is the Canvas Outcomes plan: what gets created in Canvas, on what scale, and how scores reach the program's reports.

1. How to read this document

Nine terms carry most of the weight here.

  • A course learning objective (CLO) is a skill the course promises a student will have at the end. This course has six. They are fixed by the build prompt.
  • A Bloom level names how demanding a thinking task is. The levels used here run Apply, Analyze, Evaluate, Create, from least to most demanding. The level decides what kind of assessment can prove the objective.
  • The NICE Framework is a national list of cybersecurity work roles. NIST publishes it. Each role carries a list of tasks and skills.
  • A work role is one job description in that list. This course aligns to PD-WRL-001, Defensive Cybersecurity.
  • A Canvas Outcome is a record in Canvas that stores how each student did on one objective. The program pulls its outcome reports from these records.
  • A rubric criterion is one scored line on a grading rubric. Linking a criterion to an Outcome is what moves a score into the outcome report.
  • A mastery scale is the set of rating levels an Outcome is scored on. It also names the level that counts as mastery.
  • The roll-up is the single number the program's report shows for one objective, gathered from every rubric criterion linked to it.
  • Classic Quizzes is one of the two quiz engines Canvas offers. A Classic Quiz has no rubric, so it cannot post a score to an Outcome.

2. Framework citation and verification status

The matrix cites this source:

NICE Framework Components v2.2.0 (NIST, April 28, 2026), implementing NIST SP 800-181 Rev. 1 (November 2020); work role PD-WRL-001 Defensive Cybersecurity, Protection and Defense; retrieved 2026-09-03 from niccs.cisa.gov and nist.gov.

Date caveat. The NIST Current Versions heading reads "2.2.0 (April 28, 2025)". The change log dates 2.2.0 to 28 April 2026 and 2.1.0 to 3 December 2025, and the release note URL sits under /2026/04/. The heading year is a typo. This document cites the change log.

Verification result. Every id in the matrix was checked on 2026-09-03. All 21 exist in v2.2.0 and all 21 are linked to PD-WRL-001. None is missing. None sits outside the role. No id was substituted for another. The full record, including per-id source URLs and the disagreements that were settled, is at builder/docs/nice-verification.md.

Each id has its own page on niccs.cisa.gov. Tasks use the path /tools/nice-framework/task/<ID> and skills use /tools/nice-framework/skill/<ID>. Statements in section 4 are quoted from those pages and from the components JSON, never from a role-page summary, because the summaries were seen to swap and truncate statements.

Primary evidence placement is fixed by the build prompt, quoted here as the contract the matrix implements:

CLO6 Measure agent triage accuracy across a batch of emails and recommend where human review stays required. Evaluate CLO6: Measure triage accuracy T0164, Perform cyber defense trend analysis and reporting T1020, Determine the operational and safety impacts of cybersecurity lapses; T1290, Communicate daily network event and activity reports; S0892, Skill in performing trend analysis 7 (primary) Week 7 lab: the exported batch evaluation report over the 20-email batch, with accuracy and with false positives and false negatives reported separately Week 7 rubric, criterion 3: Accuracy and human review

Framework citation for the matrix: NICE Framework Components v2.2.0 (NIST, April 28, 2026), implementing NIST SP 800-181 Rev. 1 (November 2020); work role PD-WRL-001 Defensive Cybersecurity, Protection and Defense; retrieved 2026-09-03.

Four notes on reading the table.

  1. Ids beginning with T are tasks. Ids beginning with S are skills, not tasks. The build prompt lists both under "supporting", so both appear in the supporting column.
  2. Every lab submits the same three items: a screenshot of the working output, one file the notebook exports with a single pre-written cell, and a written answer of 3 to 5 sentences. The artifact column names the exported file and the answer, because those two carry the evidence.
  3. Week 7 has one rubric that carries two objectives. CLO5 takes criterion 2 and CLO6 takes criterion 3.
  4. Criterion 1 in every weekly rubric is submission completeness. It is a non-CLO expectation and carries no Outcome link. Section 10 states that rule.

4. The NICE statements behind the matrix

Every statement below is the exact wording from the verified source.
