What only the college can answer

These are carried into the blueprint's gaps section. The first three are the ones that change what gets built.

  1. Is identity verification required in every online section, and must this course carry it? The build leaves it out. The exam is open book and unproctored, so nothing here depends on it.
  2. Is the locked testing browser module required in every online section? The build leaves it out. Some students have no rights to install software on the machine they use.
  3. Does the college collect Critical Thinking outcome data from every course? If it does, which item carries the zero-point rubric, given that Canvas allows one rubric per assignment?
  4. May a course authorize AI use with disclosure under the district AI procedure, and will AI detection software be run on a course that requires students to use AI?
  5. What is the late work penalty, and does the college require anything to lock after a deadline? The build locks nothing.
  6. Is the external syllabus system the record of truth? If it is, does the Canvas syllabus page repeat it or replace it?
  7. Must the Orientation module gate access to later modules? The template's home page says it does; the template's own export does not implement it.
  8. Is the free online-learning orientation mini-course still required this term, and does the instructor still check the post-test result in week 1?
  9. Does the college's production Canvas instance have the DesignPLUS theme installed, and which institution tools sit in its course menu?
  10. When an institution page is kept verbatim, does that mean the words or the HTML? Fixing the template's accessibility defects changes the HTML.
  11. Is the course built through the API from scratch, which is the current plan, or by importing the template cartridge and editing it?
  12. Who owns this course, and under what license does it ship?
  13. Is the bookstore statement required on a course that assigns no textbook?
  14. What are the term dates for the 8 week Flex session, so module titles can carry dates the way the template asks?

8. Amendments recorded in Phase 2

Phase 2 built the Orientation module, Student Resources, the Q & A board and Module 0. Eight resolutions above changed while that was built, or were found to describe something the build had not done. Each one is recorded here rather than corrected in place, so the two versions can be read side by side.

A. Conflict 1. The weekly module holds eight items, not six

The two notebooks are module items. The reason and the cost are in the rewritten paragraph in section 1 above. Nothing about the template's page set changes: Overview, Content, the three assessment items and Wrap-up all exist and are filled. content/pages/how-to-navigate-canvas.md tells students the same eight. Module 0 carries four items on the same rule, which the amendment to conflict 27 already records. Decision: Build.

B. Conflict 2. Modules take the template's names. Item titles do not

content/course.yaml had named the modules for the week, which contradicted this item, week-00-outline.md and assumption A12. The modules are renamed to the template's pattern: Module 0: Programming On-Ramp through Module 8: Final Exam, with Orientation Module, Student Resources and Q & A Community named as the template names them.

Page and item titles inside a module do not take the template's Module N Content pattern. They say what the item is: "Week 1 reading", "Week 0 self-assessment", "Syllabus, policies and grades".

The reason is that everything else in this course speaks in weeks. Every due date, every schedule row and every discussion prompt does. A student should not have to turn a module number into a week number to find what is due.

The mapping is stated twice, where a student meets it. The course schedule page and the navigation page both say that Module 1 is week 1. Decision stays PM. This is the half of the item the program manager should look at hardest.

C. Conflict 3. Student Resources and Q & A now exist in the spine

Both modules were resolved as kept and neither was built. Both are now in content/course.yaml, in the template's order: Orientation Module, Student Resources, Q & A Community, Module 0, Modules 1 to 8.

Four of the template's seven Student Resources pages are authored for this course. They move out of the Orientation module and into this one. They are How to Navigate Canvas as a Student (built as "Finding your way around"), Communicating Online, Academic Integrity, and Canvas and Tech Support.

The other three are institution pages this build does not author. They are Using Zoom as a Student, Academic & Student Support Resources, and Uploading a Paper to Turnitin Using Canvas. Item 19 keeps them as they ship. They are not in the spine, and that gap is in the open questions below.

D. Conflict 11. The Q & A board is built

content/discussions/qa-board.md, ungraded and pinned, in the Q & A Community module. The template's "type your introduction" wording is corrected to "type your question". The builder gained support for an ungraded discussion, which carries no assignment, no group and no points. An ungraded board also does not require an initial post, because a student reading the board to find an answer should not have to post one first.

E. Conflicts 12 and 23. Both institution pages are built

content/pages/olrn-0001.md and content/pages/course-material.md exist. Each keeps its institution statement word for word in a block quote, and the rest of each page is written at the course reading level.

  • The bookstore statement is kept word for word on the Course Material page. The course material page says plainly that there is no textbook and nothing to buy, and lists what students do need.
  • The OLRN page keeps the enrollment requirement, the "1 to 2 hours" and the "80 percent or higher on the post-test" figures, and the two college contacts.

Four links in those two template files were checked. Three resolve: the OLRN student page, the district registration page, and the college bookstore page. The bookstore's separate online store answered the check with HTTP 403.

One of the four is carried, the OLRN student page. It is the only one a student here has to open. Neither page needs the other three. This course assigns no textbook, and the two college phone numbers reach a person faster than a registration tutorial does.

F. Conflict 16 and template item 5. Course Evaluation is folded into the syllabus

The template's Course Evaluation page is not created. Conformance section 4.6 says that page is where the template expects the grading breakdown. In this build the grading breakdown is on the syllabus page, content/pages/course-policies.md, which already carries the four weighted groups, the points per item and the letter scale. Two pages holding one grade table is how the two numbers drift apart. Decision: Build.

G. Conflicts 4, 31 and 34. The home page is built

content/pages/home.md is built and set as the course front page, and course[default_view] is wiki, which is what the template sets. It carries the welcome, the catalog description, the delivery paragraph for a course with no meetings, the instructor contact block, the template's four Getting Started steps and the fifth step this build adds for the week 0 self-assessment.

Two template sentences are not carried, as those items resolved: the sentence sending students to Zoom for attending class remotely (item 31), and the sentence saying the Orientation module must be completed before the next module opens (item 5). The syllabus step points at the Course Syllabus page rather than an external tool that the target instance does not have (item 34).

content/pages/meet-your-instructor.md is built at the same time, as the template's second Orientation item. It carries the contact channel, the reply time, the feedback turnaround and what the instructor does each week. It carries no instructor name, email address, photo or contact hours, because the build does not know them and a placeholder is not allowed. That is in the open questions below.

H. Conflict 13. The letter scheme and the course description are applied

course.yaml declared default_view, description_page and the A/B/C/D/F scheme. The builder read none of the three. They looked applied in the repository and were absent in Canvas.

build_course.py now creates the grading standard at 90, 80, 70 and 60 and sets it on the course. It also sets default_view, sets the front page, sets the catalog description as public_description, and renders the syllabus page into syllabus_body.

The letter table students read on the syllabus page is now the table Canvas applies. --verify checks all of it and fails if any of it is missing.

The catalog description states the enforced prerequisite, which the build prompt requires the course description to carry.

Open questions this amendment adds

  1. Who is the instructor, and what are the contact hours? The Meet Your Instructor page and the home page instructor card both want a name, an email address and contact hours. The build has none of the three and wrote none of them. They go in when the section is assigned.
  2. Are the three unbuilt Student Resources pages copied in? Using Zoom as a Student, Academic & Student Support Resources, and Uploading a Paper to Turnitin Using Canvas. None is used by any graded activity here. The Zoom page conflicts with item 31 and the Turnitin page describes a submission type this course does not use.
  3. Does the program manager accept week-named items inside module-named modules? Amendment B.
Survey content for the breadth topics7,389 words · survey-content-plan.md

Course: Agentic AI and Security Operations (ITSY 1377), Northeast Lakeview College, Cyber Defense program Phase 1 blueprint, item 2. Prepared 2026-09-03. Audience: the program manager. Nothing here assumes you are building the course.

Field Value
Topics covered B1, B2, B3, B4
Weeks that carry them 1, 6, 7
Course objectives created None. These are not objectives
Canvas Outcomes created None
Labs created None
Added student time None. Survey reading replaces reading, it does not add reading

A note on terms used below. Survey content is reading that widens what a student knows without asking them to build anything. A breadth topic is a required subject this course carries by reading, discussion and quiz rather than by lab work. A self-check is a low stakes quiz a student may retake.

Three more terms appear later. Prompt injection is attacker text that a model reads as an instruction. A false positive is a safe message the agent calls malicious. A false negative is a malicious message it calls safe.

1. What survey content is, and what it is not

The course build goes deep on one narrow thing: a phishing triage agent. Four subjects the program needs are wider than that agent. They are carried by reading, discussion, self-checks and the final exam.

They get no course learning objective number. They get no Canvas Outcome. They get no lab. The build arc is not widened to reach any of them.

Topic Subject Where the reading sits Also assessed by
B1 AI and agentic workflows in IT and security operations broadly Week 1 reading, part two Week 1 discussion, week 1 self-check, final exam
B2 The range of AI-assisted security use cases Week 7 reading, part three Week 7 discussion, week 7 self-check, final exam
B3 Security operations components Week 1 reading, part one Week 1 self-check, week 1 discussion, final exam
B4 The wider AI risk set Week 6 reading, page two Week 6 self-check, week 6 discussion in part, final exam

Two placement choices are worth stating plainly.

B1 and B3 sit in week 1 because a student cannot judge a phishing agent without knowing what a security team is and what AI already does in one. B3 also does a second job. The prerequisite course is a non-technical AI literacy course, so no earlier course in the chain explains what a security operations center holds. Week 1 supplies that orientation.

B2 sits in week 7 for the opposite reason. A student can only generalize from something they have already built. In week 7 they have a working agent and their own accuracy numbers, so the question "where else does this go" has an anchor.

2. How reading time is priced

The weekly reading budget is 40 to 50 minutes. Survey content lives inside that budget. It never adds to it.

This section holds the one reading rate the whole course uses. Every one of the seven weekly specs prices its reading from the rule below, and none of them names a different rate.

Two numbers are reported for every reading below, because they answer two different questions.

One pass at 200 words a minute. This is a normal adult silent reading rate for plain prose. It answers "how long does it take to read the words once." Every week uses this rate and no other.

The minutes priced in the week. This is what the week's time table actually charges the student. It is higher, and the difference is study time rather than reading time. These pages carry first-time definitions, a labeled diagram and worked examples, and students go back over those.

So the priced minutes are a study pace, not a reading pace. Each week spec shows its own split: one pass at 200 words a minute, then one named line for each thing the page asks beyond reading the words. The two parts add to the priced total.

Both numbers appear in every section. The gap between them is visible on purpose. The build prompt asks for the weekly time budget to be checked against the real week 1 and week 2 builds, and that check is what should test the study pace. The rate for one pass is not what that check is testing.

Week Reading priced Budget Total words Survey words Survey share of priced minutes
1 40 minutes 40 to 50 1,350 1,350 40 of 40
6 45 minutes 40 to 50 2,300 1,150 22 of 45
7 42 minutes 40 to 50 1,250 390 10 of 42

The replacement rule in one sentence. In week 6, the injection page runs to 1,150 words rather than 2,300 because B4 takes the other half of the same 45 minutes.

3. B1, AI and agentic workflows in IT and security operations

Where it lives: week 1 reading, part two. Also: the week 1 discussion, part 2.

B1 covers AI across IT and security work, not only phishing. The reading separates three things students otherwise blur together, then shows each one at work outside email.

3.1 What the reading covers, section by section

# Section Words What it does
1 Three kinds of AI, and why the difference matters 120 One plain sentence for each kind. Scoring AI sorts or rates something. Generative AI writes new text or code. An agentic workflow picks its own next step and then acts
2 Scoring AI at work 130 Two examples outside phishing. A mail gateway rating every message. An endpoint tool scoring a file as risky. What it hands back is a number or a label
3 Generative AI at work 130 Two examples outside phishing. Drafting a change request for a firewall rule. Explaining an unfamiliar log line in plain words. What it hands back is new text
4 Agentic workflows at work 130 Two examples outside phishing. A password reset flow that checks the ticket, the account and the device before acting. A patch check that reads an inventory, looks up a version, and opens a ticket. What it hands back is a decision plus an action, inside limits
5 What stays human 60 All three hand their output to a person. Names the escalation point that part one defined, so B1 and B3 close together
Total 570

Every example is set at Pellgrave Mechanical Group, the fictional company the corpus supplies. No real company, product or brand is named.

Section 4 is the one that earns its place. The course claims that an agent is a loop around a model call, not magic. A student who has seen a password reset flow described as a loop of checks reads week 5 differently.

3.2 Word count and reading time

Measure Value
Words 570
One pass at 200 words a minute 2.9 minutes
Priced in the week 1 time table 10 minutes

3.3 How it fits the week 1 reading budget

Week 1 has no separate technical reading page. The week's technical material, the model call and temperature and token counts, is carried by the two short videos and by the notebook's own markdown cells. Those are priced in the video band and the lab band, not in the reading band.

So the whole reading band in week 1 is survey content, and it is shared by B1 and B3.

Part of the week 1 reading Words Minutes priced
Part one, B3, the parts of a security operations center 780 20
Part two, B1, AI in IT and security operations 570 10
Opening and skimming one of the four cited sources 10
Total 1,350 40

The arithmetic: 780 plus 570 is 1,350 words. Twenty plus 10 plus 10 is 40 minutes. Forty minutes is the floor of the 40 to 50 minute budget, not the ceiling. At 200 words a minute the whole page reads once in 6.8 minutes, so the survey content is nowhere near what would push this week over its budget.

3.4 Sources it will cite

Week 1 cites four sources for both B1 and B3 together. B1 draws on these two.

Source Publisher and identifier What it grounds
Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework 1.0 NIST, report AI 100-1, January 2023, on nist.gov and the AI Resource Center What counts as an AI system, and the place of human oversight
Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework: Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile NIST, report AI 600-1, July 2024, on nist.gov What generative AI is, and how it differs from a scoring model

A fifth source was considered and cut to hold the count at four: the MITRE ATT&CK entry for phishing, technique T1566. It is available if the program manager wants it added.

Every citation is checked before publication. The check has four parts: the document is fetched, the link is confirmed to resolve, the licence is confirmed to permit use, and an expert subagent review confirms the claim the citation is attached to. Any claim the review cannot ground is removed or flagged, never quietly kept.

3.5 How the self-check and the final exam assess it

Week 1 self-check. Two of its six multiple choice items sit on B1. Each describes a tool in plain words and asks which of the three kinds it is. Unlimited attempts, highest score kept.

Final exam. B1 items ask a student to place a described workflow in one of the three kinds, or to say what changes when a workflow becomes agentic. Section 10 gives the item share. A sample stem:

The Pellgrave help desk runs a tool on new password tickets. It reads the ticket, checks the user's device record, resets the password, and closes the ticket. Which kind of AI work is this?

  • A. scoring AI
  • B. generative AI
  • C. an agentic workflow
  • D. none of these

4. B2, the range of AI-assisted security use cases

Where it lives: week 7 reading, part three. Also: the week 7 discussion.

B2 is the answer to "what else is this good for," asked at the only point in the course where a student has their own results to answer it with.

4.1 What the reading covers, section by section

Part three of the week 7 reading walks seven use cases. A use case is one job people give an AI tool. Each gets one short paragraph and answers the same three questions: what facts does the tool need first, what does it hand back, and who checks it.

# Section Words What it does
1 What a use case is, and the three questions 40 Sets the pattern the seven paragraphs follow
2 Alert triage 50 Sorting a queue of alerts by what deserves a person first. Needs the alert, the asset it names, and what normal looks like
3 Log review 50 Reading a large log for the lines that matter. Needs the log lines and a baseline
4 Phishing analysis 50 The one students built. Placed here so they see their own work as one of seven, not as the whole field
5 Documentation 50 Drafting a ticket note or an incident summary. Needs the facts already recorded, never new ones
6 Scripting help 50 Writing a small script to pull or reshape data. Needs the data shape and a person who reads the script before running it
7 Vulnerability research 50 Explaining what a published weakness means for one specific system. Needs the software inventory and versions
8 Incident response support 50 Drafting a timeline or a next-step list during an incident. Needs the evidence already collected
Total 390

Section 4 is deliberate. Students who have spent seven weeks on phishing can leave believing phishing triage is what AI in security means. One paragraph of the same shape as the other six fixes that.

Each paragraph names what would have to change in the agent students built. That is what the discussion then asks them to do for one use case of their own choosing.

4.2 Word count and reading time

Measure Value
Words 390
One pass at 200 words a minute 2.0 minutes
Priced in the week 7 time table 10 minutes

4.3 How it fits the week 7 reading budget

Week 7 has real technical reading. Parts one and two teach the triage note format and how to measure the agent, and both carry a worked example that students read line by line. B2 is part three and carries no worked example, so it is priced lower.

Part of the week 7 reading Words Minutes priced
1. The note, with one worked triage note 480 17
2. Measuring, with one worked score block 380 15
3. Where else this goes, B2 390 10
Total 1,250 42

The arithmetic: 480 plus 380 plus 390 is 1,250 words, inside the 1,400 word ceiling the writing standard sets for a weekly reading. Seventeen plus 15 plus 10 is 42 minutes, inside the 40 to 50 minute budget with 8 minutes of headroom. At 200 words a minute the whole page reads once in 6.3 minutes.

B2 takes 10 of the 42 minutes. Without it, parts one and two would be written longer to fill the same band. That is what "replaces reading time" means here.

4.4 Sources it will cite

Source Publisher and identifier What it grounds

Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework 1.0 NIST, report AI 100-1, January 2023, on nist.gov Measurement and the human oversight point
Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework: Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile NIST, report AI 600-1, July 2024, on nist.gov What a generative tool can and cannot be trusted to produce

The revision in force is pinned at fetch time. NIST revises these documents, so the writer records the revision number and the retrieval date rather than trusting the number printed here. Every citation goes through the same four-part check described in section 3.4.

4.5 How the self-check and the final exam assess it

Week 7 self-check. Four of its eight items sit on B2. Each gives a short description of a security job, and asks which use case it is and one fact the tool would need first. Two attempts, highest score kept.

Week 7 discussion. Question 2 asks each student to pick one use case other than phishing. Question 3 asks what they would change in the agent they built. Section 8 gives the prompt in full.

Final exam. B2 items give a described job and ask which use case it is, or ask which fact the tool needs before it can judge anything. A sample stem:

Devin wants an AI tool to help with log review instead of phishing. Which fact does the tool need before it can judge anything?

  • A. the raw log lines and what normal looks like for that host
  • B. the model temperature
  • C. the token count
  • D. the sender's display name

5. B3, security operations components

Where it lives: week 1 reading, part one. Also: the week 1 self-check.

B3 does two jobs at once. It is a required breadth topic, and it is the security operations orientation that no earlier course in the chain supplies.

5.1 What the reading covers, section by section

# Section Words What it does
1 What a security operations center is 70 A plain definition. Says that at a company the size of Pellgrave it is one analyst, not a room of them
2 The things you protect: assets, users, networks, endpoints 200 One plain definition each, one Pellgrave example each. A laptop is an asset and an endpoint at the same time, and the reading says why that is not a contradiction
3 The things that tell you something happened: logs, alerts 140 A log is a record that something occurred. An alert is a record that something decided was worth a person's time. The difference is the whole job
4 The things you do about it: tickets, incidents, escalation, documentation 230 One plain definition each. Names who receives an escalated ticket at Pellgrave, and what documentation has to contain for the next person to act on it
5 The picture, and where your agent sits 140 A labeled diagram. A reported email becomes an alert, then a ticket, then either a closed ticket or an incident. A pin on that diagram marks where this course's agent sits
Total 780

Section 5 is the section the rest of the course leans on. The pin sits between the alert and the ticket. The agent reads the facts behind an alert and drafts the note that goes into a ticket. It does not close anything, and it does not decide that something is an incident.

A student who holds that picture reads two later weeks differently. The week 6 escalation threshold and the week 7 human review question become the same question, asked twice.

The diagram ships with alt text that describes the same path in words, so the meaning does not depend on seeing the image. That is a Web Content Accessibility Guidelines 2.2 AA requirement and it is checked before publication.

5.2 Word count and reading time

Measure Value
Words 780
One pass at 200 words a minute 3.9 minutes
Priced in the week 1 time table 20 minutes

Ten definitions and a diagram are the slowest 780 words in the course, which is why this part is priced at more than half of the week 1 reading band.

5.3 How it fits the week 1 reading budget

The week 1 table in section 3.3 covers both topics and is not repeated here. The arithmetic for B3: 780 words of the 1,350 on the page, and 20 minutes of the 40 priced. At 200 words a minute part one reads once in 3.9 minutes.

Week 1 is also the week the build prompt names for checking the time budget against a real build. This figure should be measured again after Phase 2 rather than trusted.

5.4 Sources it will cite

B3 draws on the other two of week 1's four sources.

Source Publisher and identifier What it grounds
The NIST Cybersecurity Framework 2.0 NIST, Cybersecurity White Paper 29, February 2024, on nist.gov and csrc.nist.gov Assets, the identify and detect functions, and why an inventory comes before anything else
Incident Response Recommendations and Considerations for Cybersecurity Risk Management NIST, Special Publication 800-61 Revision 3, April 2025, on csrc.nist.gov The difference between an event, an alert and an incident. Escalation and documentation as defined steps

The same four-part check in section 3.4 applies. One item needs the reviewer's attention: the terms ticket and escalation are operational rather than defined in either document. The reading will attribute the incident handling steps to Special Publication 800-61 Revision 3 and will describe ticket handling as common practice rather than as a cited requirement. That is the honest read and the reviewer is asked to confirm it.

5.5 How the self-check and the final exam assess it

Week 1 self-check. Three of its six items sit on B3. One gives a line and asks which part it belongs to. One asks where this course's agent sits in the picture. One asks who receives an escalated ticket.

Week 1 discussion. Part 2 asks each student to name one part of a security operations center that their own example touched, chosen from the ten the reading defined.

Final exam. B3 items give a short scene from the corpus and ask which component it names, or where the agent sits relative to it. A sample stem:

The mail gateway writes a record for every message it scans. One record is marked for a person to look at. What is that marked record called?

  • A. an asset
  • B. a log
  • C. an alert
  • D. an incident

6. B4, the wider AI risk set

Where it lives: week 6 reading, page two. Also: the week 6 self-check, and one question of the week 6 discussion.

Week 6 is the week students attack their own agent. The lab work is prompt injection. B4 is the rest of the risk set, and every risk on the page lands on a case the student just ran.

6.1 What the reading covers, section by section

# Section Words What it does
1 Five risks that are not injection 100 Says plainly that injection is one risk among several, and that the other five need different controls
2 Hallucination 210 A model stating something that is not in the facts it was given. The case: an indicator naming a host the extractor never found. The control: validate every output field against the extracted facts
3 Data exposure 210 Putting information somewhere it should not go. The case: email 023, whose forged header asks the agent to paste the recipient's report history into a summary written for the reporter. The control: an output validator that rejects any field the note format does not define
4 Privacy 200 The same case one step wider. What you put in a prompt is what you expose. Repeats the week 3 rule: send the minimum a sound judgment needs, and no more
5 Automation bias 200 Trusting a tool because it is a tool. The case: email 015, the benign message a keyword rule escalates. A person who trusts the tool stops looking at the message
6 Overreliance 180 Letting the tool decide what a person should decide. The case: the confidence floor, and the honest reason it exists
7 What to do about all five 50 A short list pointing at the four controls the lab already built, so the page ends in the student's own notebook
Total 1,150

Two things about this page are worth the program manager's attention.

First, it is not an abstract risk list. Each of the five names an email or a setting from the lab the student finished that same week. Automation bias is not a concept here, it is email 015 escalating when it should not.

Second, it teaches the difference between the risk and the control. Hallucination is caught by validating output against facts. Data exposure is caught by an output validator. Automation bias and overreliance are not caught by code at all, which the page says out loud.

6.2 Word count and reading time

Measure Value
Words 1,150
One pass at 200 words a minute 5.8 minutes
Priced in the week 6 time table 22 minutes

6.3 How it fits the week 6 reading budget

Week 6 carries two reading pages of equal length. Page one is technical and belongs to the lab. Page two is B4.

Page of the week 6 reading Words Minutes priced
1. Prompt injection in a triage agent 1,150 23
2. The wider AI risk set, B4 1,150 22
Total 2,300 45

The arithmetic: 1,150 plus 1,150 is 2,300 words. Twenty three plus 22 is 45 minutes, inside the 40 to 50 minute budget with 5 minutes of headroom. At 200 words a minute both pages read once in 11.5 minutes.

This is the clearest case of the replacement rule in the course. Week 6 spends 45 minutes on reading either way. B4 takes half of that, so the injection page is written to 1,150 words instead of 2,300. Nothing was added to the week.

Week 6 also carries the strongest lab in the course, so it has the least slack. If the real build shows this week over 6 hours, the build prompt says to cut reading first. The order of cuts on this page is stated here so it is not decided under pressure: cut section 4 into section 3, then shorten sections 5 and 6. Do not cut a risk entirely.

Source Publisher and identifier What it grounds
Adversarial Machine Learning: A Taxonomy and Terminology of Attacks and Mitigations NIST, report AI 100-2, on csrc.nist.gov Injection and data extraction as named attack classes, with the edition pinned at fetch
Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework: Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile NIST, report AI 600-1, July 2024, on nist.gov Hallucination, data privacy, and the human configuration risks behind automation bias

Two verification notes for the writer. The OWASP list is versioned by year and its numbering changes between versions, so the reading quotes the entry titles and pins the version in force at publication. The MITRE technique identifiers are confirmed against the live site at fetch time rather than written from memory. Every citation goes through the same four-part check described in section 3.4.

6.5 How the self-check and the final exam assess it

Week 6 self-check. One of its six items sits on B4. It asks which wider risk the forged header case in email 023 shows.

Week 6 discussion. The discussion is a course objective 4 activity and belongs to the injection work. One of its three questions also lands on B4. It asks the student to write the extra guardrail rule their own run needed, then to price that rule against email 015, the benign decoy, using a number from their own file. That is the overreliance and automation bias question in operational form.

A gap this plan reports rather than hides. B4 has the thinnest assessment of the four topics outside the exam: one self-check item and part of one discussion question. The other three each carry two or more graded touches. Two options are offered and neither is taken without approval. Raise the week 6 self-check from six items to eight, adding one item on hallucination and one on automation bias. Or leave it at six and accept that the exam carries most of B4. The second option is defensible, because B4 is the topic selected response suits best, and because the exam blueprint now gives B4 the largest share of the breadth half for this reason.

Final exam. B4 items give an agent output or a setting and ask which risk it shows. A sample stem:

Your triage note lists an indicator that names a host your extractor never found in the email. Which risk does this show?

  • A. hallucination
  • B. data exposure
  • C. automation bias
  • D. overreliance

7. The week 1 discussion prompt

Graded. One post and one substantive reply. Estimated student time 20 minutes, inside the 15 to 20 minute band the build prompt sets. The estimate appears in the week 1 Tasks table on the module overview page, which is where the institutional template puts activity times.

The prompt carries B1 and B3. Part 1 requires the student's own exported file, so a general opinion cannot answer it. Two students will not have the same six answers, because the model is not deterministic.

Reply requirement: one reply, to a classmate who named a different kind of AI than the poster did, in two or three sentences, pointing to a line in that classmate's post. A reply that only agrees does not count.

Written out below exactly as students will see it.

Where you have already met AI at work

Post by Thursday. Reply to one classmate by Sunday at 11:59 PM Central.

Write about 150 words in your post. Write about 60 words in your reply.

Part 1. Your own results.

Open your file week1_first_calls.txt. Find the six answers your notebook gave to the same email. Quote one short line from one answer. Say which temperature produced it. Then name one thing that answer got right. Name one thing it got wrong or left out. Use your own file. Do not use a classmate's.

Part 2. Where you have met this before.

Name one place you have already met AI in IT work or security work. It can be from a job, from school, from home, or from the news. Then say which of these three kinds it was.

  • Scoring AI, which sorts or rates something. A spam filter is one.
  • Generative AI, which writes new text or code. A chat assistant is one.
  • An agentic workflow, where a tool picks its own next step and then acts.

Then name one part of a security operations center that your example touched. Pick from this week's reading: assets, users, networks, endpoints, logs, alerts, tickets, incidents, escalation, or documentation.

Your reply.

Reply to one classmate who named a different kind than you did. In two or three sentences, say what would change in their example if the tool were agentic. Point to a line in their post. A reply that only agrees does not count.

You may use AI help on this post. If you do, add one line saying what you asked it.

Why this cannot be answered from opinion. Part 1 needs a file the student's own notebook wrote, a line quoted from it, and the temperature that produced it. Part 2 needs a real place the student has been. The reply needs a specific classmate's post and a specific line in it.

One facilitation note for the instructor guide. Students will name spam filters far more than anything else. The instructor's own reply should name an agentic example from IT work, so the third kind does not stay abstract.

8. The week 7 discussion prompt

Graded. One post and one substantive reply. Estimated student time 18 minutes, inside the 15 to 20 minute band. The estimate appears in the week 7 Tasks table.

The prompt carries B2. It requires three numbers from the student's own batch run, so it cannot be answered from opinion or from a classmate's post.

Reply requirement: one reply, to a classmate who chose a different use case, naming one thing that classmate's change would break or fix in the replier's own agent, and citing one number from the replier's own report. A reply that only agrees does not count.

Written out below exactly as students will see it.

Your numbers, and one other job for an agent

A use case is one job people give an AI tool.

Open your week 7 report. Find three numbers: your accuracy, your false positive count, and your false negative count.

Post by Thursday. Do three things.

  1. Give your three numbers. Name one email your agent got wrong. Say which error it was.
  2. Pick one use case from this week's reading. Do not pick phishing. Your choices are alert triage, log review, documentation, scripting help, vulnerability research, or incident response support.
  3. Name one part of your agent you would have to change for that use case. Say what the change is. Say why it is needed.

Then reply to one classmate by Sunday at 11:59 PM Central. Pick someone who chose a different use case. Say one thing their change would break or fix in your own agent. Use one number from your own report. A reply that only agrees does not count.

Keep your post near 150 words. Keep your reply near 60 words.

You may use AI help on this post. If you do, add one line saying what you asked it.

Why this cannot be answered from opinion. The three numbers come from a batch the student ran themselves, and no two students will have the same three. The wrong email has to be one their own run got wrong. Question 3 requires them to name a real part of the agent they built.

One facilitation note for the instructor guide. A student who picks documentation or scripting help will often say nothing would change. That is the teachable moment. The facts the tool needs first change completely, which is the point of every use case paragraph in the reading.

9. Reading level

Every student-facing block in this document was measured with the course readability tool. The tool excludes code, tables, file paths and headings, and measures what is left.

Block Grade Average sentence Longest sentence Limit
Week 1 discussion prompt, section 7 3.09 8.8 words 17 words grade 7, average 12
Week 7 discussion prompt, section 8 3.03 8.3 words 20 words grade 7, average 12
The four sample exam stems, sections 3.5, 4.5, 5.5 and 6.5 3.89 7.7 words 19 words grade 7, average 12
This document as a whole 6.30 14.0 words 37 words grade 9.5, average 18

How each block was measured. The two discussion prompts are the blockquoted text in sections 7 and 8. The stem row is the four blockquoted sample stems, taken together, with the leading > removed from each line so the tool reads them as prose rather than skipping them as a quotation. Each stem on its own is under the tool's 40 word floor or close to it, so the four are scored as one block. Scored one at a time the two that clear the floor read 3.06 and 2.53.

One earlier number corrected. An earlier draft of this table reported the stem row at grade 4.97 with an average sentence of 9.4 words. That draft wrote every option list as one inline sentence beginning "Options:", which pushed the B2 stem to an average of 15.7 words, over the 12 word limit for a student-facing stem. All four stems now write one option per list line, which is the form the week 8 exam blueprint already uses. The row above is what the tool returns on the rewritten stems.

Both discussion prompts pass at grade 7 or below with room to spare. Both hold an average sentence under 12 words. This document is a program manager document, so it is held to grade 9.5, and it passes with no paragraph over 70 words.

The readings themselves are measured when they are written, not now. Each one is held to the same 7th grade standard, and each measured score is reported per page in the final quality report.

10. What the final exam carries

The final exam is multiple choice and its primary job is these four topics. Roughly half its items sit on B1 through B4. The other half are scenario reads drawn from the course's own agent and corpus.

The exam blueprint is item 7 of this phase and it sets the item count. It sets a thirty item exam, fifteen of them breadth items. This plan sets what each topic is owed inside that breadth half, and the two documents carry the same numbers.

Topic Items Share of the breadth half
B1 3 20.0 percent
B2 4 26.7 percent
B3 3 20.0 percent
B4 5 33.3 percent
Total 15 of 30 100 percent

B4 gets the largest share for two reasons. It has the thinnest assessment elsewhere, as section 6.5 states. And it is the course's differentiator, so the program has the most reason to see a number on it.

B3 yields the item that B4 gains. B3 has the thickest coverage outside the exam: the whole first part of the week 1 reading, plus three of the six week 1 self-check items. It can afford three exam items where B4 cannot.

Two rules bind every breadth item. No item is answerable from general phishing knowledge, and every stem is drawn from this course's own corpus or its own agent. The four sample stems above meet both.

11. Citation verification, stated once

Every citation in every survey reading is verified before publication. The check has four parts.

  1. The document is fetched and read, not cited from memory.
  2. The link is confirmed to resolve, and its licence is confirmed to permit use in a course. Documents from NIST, CISA and MITRE are public. The OWASP material is openly licensed and the exact licence is recorded.
  3. An expert subagent review confirms that the claim the citation is attached to is actually supported by the document.
  4. Any claim the review cannot ground is removed or flagged in the risk register. It is never quietly kept.

The revision number and the retrieval date are recorded for each source, because these documents are revised. Where a revision printed in this plan turns out to be superseded, the reading cites the current one and the change is reported rather than silently made.

12. Assumptions

  1. Reading rate. The 200 words a minute figure is a standard adult silent reading rate for plain prose. No reading rate has been measured for this student population. The priced minutes are a study pace, and they are the numbers the week budgets use. Section 2 states the rate once for the whole course, and the seven week specs price from it rather than from a rate of their own.
  2. Word counts are targets, not measurements. These readings are not written yet. Each count is what the section is planned to run to, inside the 1,400 word ceiling the writing standard sets for a weekly reading.
  3. Week 1 has no separate technical reading page. The week's technical material reaches students through the two videos and the notebook markdown. If that changes in Phase 2, the week 1 reading band has to be re-split.
  4. This plan holds the only text of the week 1 and week 7 discussion prompts. The week 1 and week 7 specifications now carry the same words, copied from sections 7 and 8 of this document. Where the two ever differ again, this plan is the source.
  5. The Pellgrave organization supplies every example. No real company, product, person, domain or address appears in any survey reading.
  6. Escalation and ticket handling are described as common practice rather than attributed to a cited requirement, because neither cited document defines them.

13. Open questions for the program manager

  1. Reading rate. Settled, and recorded here rather than left open. An earlier draft priced week 1 at 100 words a minute, week 6 at 50, and this plan by section. All seven weeks now use one rate: 200 words a minute for one pass over the words, with everything a page asks beyond that priced as its own named line. The rate is stated once, in section 2 above, and every week spec points at it. Every weekly reading total is unchanged, because only the way each total is built has changed. One question is left for the program manager: should the rate and the named lines be shown to students on the module page, or held in the course documents?
  2. B4 assessment depth. Section 6.5 offers two options: raise the week 6 self-check from six items to eight, or leave it at six and let the exam carry most of B4. Neither is taken without a decision. The exam blueprint now gives B4 five of the fifteen breadth items, the largest share of any topic, which narrows the gap either way.
  3. The fifth week 1 source. The MITRE ATT&CK phishing entry was cut to hold week 1 to four citations. Add it or leave it out.
  4. Discussion due-day wording. This plan uses "post by Thursday, reply by Sunday at 11:59 PM Central" in both prompts. The week 6 prompt words it slightly differently. One wording should be chosen and used in all seven weekly discussions.
  5. Whether the week 1 reading should be split into two pages. It runs to 1,350 words, above the 900 to 1,050 word target the writing standard sets, because it carries two breadth topics. Splitting it into two pages would meet the target but would add a page to the module. This plan keeps one page.
Week 0, the programming on-ramp6,008 words · week-00-outline.md

Course: Agentic AI and Security Operations (ITSY 1377), Northeast Lakeview College, Cyber Defense program Phase 1 blueprint, item 6. Prepared 2026-09-03. Audience: the program manager. Nothing here assumes you are building the course.

Field Value
Module title Module 0: Programming On-Ramp
Status Optional, strongly recommended, ungraded
Open Day one, and for the whole term
Sits Outside the seven instructional weeks
Student time 4 hours 8 minutes at the fast end, 4 hours 57 minutes planned, 5 hours 56 minutes at the careful end
Delivery One runnable notebook, plus a Canvas practice quiz for the self-assessment
Sections 10, in the order the course needs them
Self-assessment 10 questions, one per section
Model calls None
Tokens spent Zero
API key needed None
CLO mapping None. Week 0 is not assessed and gets no Canvas Outcome.

A note on terms. A notebook is the web page where students run Python. A cell is one block in that notebook. A TODO cell is a cell marked for the student to fill in. Pre-written means the cell arrives finished and working. A traceback is the block of text Python prints when a line of code fails.

What week 0 is

Students arrive from a non-technical AI literacy course. That course supplies no Python. Every week from week 1 on asks them to write Python.

Week 0 closes that gap and nothing more. It teaches ten constructs. Every one of them is used by a lab cell a student has to write. Nothing else is taught.

Week 0 teaches Python, not the course. Every worked example uses everyday subject matter. No example mentions email, phishing, headers, alerts or verdicts. That rule holds in all ten sections and in all ten self-assessment questions.

The notebook runs offline. It makes no model calls, spends no tokens, and needs no API key. A student can finish all of week 0 before the key is issued.

What students are told about the time cost

The build prompt requires that the front-loading be stated plainly. Here is the text students read, on the Module 0 Overview page and again in the notebook's first cell.

About the time this takes

Week 0 is extra. It sits outside the seven course weeks. Those weeks each ask for under six hours of your time. Week 0 asks for four to six hours on top of that.

Most of that time lands before week 1 is due. Plan for it. Two or three sittings in the first week works well.

You can also come back later. Week 0 stays open all term. If week 4 breaks something loose, open week 0 again.

Week 0 is not graded. It is strongly recommended.

Where week 0 sits in Canvas

Template conflict 27 in template-conflicts.md covers this. The template's module anatomy assumes a Content page and graded work. Week 0 has neither.

Module 0 therefore uses four items, in the template's own order.

Item Type What it holds
Module 0 Overview Page What week 0 is, the time statement above, the out-of-scope list, and the link to the self-assessment
Week 0 Self-Assessment Classic quiz, ungraded practice The 10 questions below
Week 0 notebook: Python on-ramp Notebook file The ten sections, the section index, and the error card in its opening cells
Module 0 Wrap-up Page What you learned, what you did, what is next

Amended in Phase 2. There is no Module 0 Content page, and the notebook is a module item rather than a link inside one.

The template's Content page holds the week's reading. In Module 0 the reading is the notebook. A page whose only job is to link the notebook puts a click between a student and the thing they came for.

So the notebook is the module item. The overview page names it, the section index sits in the notebook's own opening cells, and the error card also ships as the Canvas page If something breaks. The amendment to template conflict 1 records the same rule for weeks 1 to 7, where the two notebooks are module items too.

Module 0 has no discussion and no assignment. The self-assessment also gets a Getting Started step on the course home page and a callout in the Orientation module, per template conflict 4.

The on-ramp, section by section

The ten sections follow the order the build prompt lists them in. That order is also the order the course needs them. Section numbers are load-bearing: the week 1 sidebars link to section 2 and section 5 by number, and the week 5 sidebar links to section 8.

# Section Learning point Everyday domain Minutes Week and lab construct it prepares
1 Variables and strings A name holds a value, and you can change what it holds. Text joins with + and measures with len(). A lunch order 20 Week 1 TODO 1 holds the prompt and the answer in variables. Week 3 TODO 2 format_facts() builds a labeled text block. Week 6 TODO 1 build_guarded_prompt() wraps text between two markers.
2 Calling functions and using return values A call runs code and hands a value back. Store it or lose it. A recipe scaled for guests 25 Week 1 TODO 1 ask_model() calls the pre-written send_request() and returns what came back. Week 1 sidebar 1A links here. Every later TODO cell calls pre-written helpers.
3 Lists, dictionaries and keys A list holds items in order. A dictionary holds values by name. You read by key, write by key, fold one dictionary into another, and reach inside a dictionary that holds a list. A library account 40 Week 1 TODO 1 builds the request dictionary, folds MODEL_EXTRAS into it, and reads the answer back out of a chain. Week 2 TODO 1 extract_json() returns a dictionary. Week 3 TODO 1 build_facts() reads values held inside parsed["auth"] and parsed["gateway"]. Week 4 TODO 1 run_tool() needs the safe lookup.
4 JSON as a dictionary in text form JSON is a dictionary written as text. You cannot read it by name until you convert it. A bus arrival board line 20 Week 2 TODO 1 extract_json() calls json.loads(). Week 2 sidebar 2B links here. Every week's export cell writes JSON.
5 f-strings Put an f in front of the quote and values drop into the sentence. A workshop name badge 20 Week 3 TODO 2 labels each fact. Week 7 TODO 1 write_note() fills a six-line template. Week 1 sidebar 1B and week 7 sidebar 7B link here.

Defaults are taught because classify_email(parsed, max_tries=3) and run_loop(parsed, prompt_text, max_steps=MAX_STEPS) both carry them.

8 Loops: for, then while with a counter Do the same thing to each item. Then run until a stop condition, with a cap that always ends it. Week 5 sidebar links here.

10 How to read an error message Read the last line first. Find your line. Print the value. A shopping basket 30 Every week. Named failures include week 1 NameError, week 2 TypeError on a None result, and KeyError in weeks 3, 4 and 7.

Section 1: Variables and strings

The worked example is a lunch order. Two variables hold a sandwich and a drink. A third joins them into one order line. Then the drink changes and the line is rebuilt, so the student sees that a name can be reassigned.

The "try changing this" cell asks the student to swap the drink. The "you try" cell asks for an order line for two different items, with the answer in the cell below it.

Section 2: Calling functions and using return values

A pre-written scale_recipe(cups, guests) arrives already working. The student calls it twice and stores both answers. The section then calls it once more and throws the answer away, so the difference is on screen.

This is the section week 1 leans on hardest. Week 1 TODO 1 is a function that calls another function and returns what it got back. A student who has not seen a return value used cannot write that cell.

Section 3: Lists, dictionaries and keys

Lists come first, because a dictionary in this course usually holds one. The example is a library account: a list of books checked out, and a dictionary with the card holder, the branch and a due day per book. The section runs in three parts, on that one example.

Part one reads. Read by position. Read by key. Read with a default when the key may be missing. Add to a list with .append().

Part two writes. Assign a key with square brackets on the left of an equals sign. Fold a second dictionary into the first one with .update().

Part three opens nested data. A dictionary holds a list, each item in that list is its own dictionary, and one chained expression reaches a field inside it.

The safe lookup is here for a specific reason. Two sender reputation records in the corpus match on an address and carry no domain key. Week 4 code that assumes the key will fail on those two.

Parts two and three are here because week 1 needs both in its first TODO cell. The request holds a list of dictionaries under one key. MODEL_EXTRAS is folded into it. The answer is then read back through a key, a list position and two more keys. Week 3 TODO 1 reads nested values the same way. Both additions are declared in the assumptions below.

Section 4: JSON as a dictionary in text form

A bus arrival board sends one line as text. It looks like a dictionary and is not one. json.loads() turns it into one, and then it can be read by key. json.dumps() goes the other way.

The section closes by breaking it on purpose. Text that is not valid JSON raises an error. The section names that error and points forward to section 9, which catches it.

Section 5: f-strings

The example builds a workshop name badge from a name, a role and a table number. The same line is then built with + and quotes, right beside it. Seeing both makes the case for the f-string without argument.

Week 7 is where this pays off. write_note() fills six fixed lines from one result. That cell is short only because f-strings exist.

Section 6: Defining a function with parameters

The example is a room painting calculator. paint_needed(width, height, coats=2) returns how much paint a wall takes. It is called with two arguments and then with three, so the default is visible.

This section also settles the most common beginner confusion in the course: return hands a value back to the caller, print puts text on screen, and they are not the same thing. Week 1 breaks immediately for a student who mixes them.

Section 7: if, elif and else

A thermostat reads one temperature and picks heat, cool or nothing. Exactly one branch runs. The section then shows in, testing whether a name is on a list.

Week 4 TODO 1 is a chain of if and elif that picks one of three functions by name. That cell is the single closest match to this section anywhere in the course.

Section 8: Loops, in two parts

This is the longest section, because week 5 is the hardest week.

Part one is the for loop. The example walks a class roster of quiz scores, building one line per student with .append(), then joining the lines into one block of text. That is exactly the shape of week 3 TODO 2.

Part two is the while loop with a counter. The example keeps reading scores until it finds one below passing, and stops anyway after three passes. It shows a loop with no cap that would never end, and then the counter that ends it.

Part two is an addition to the build prompt's scope list. It is declared in the assumptions below. Week 5's lab and sidebar both need while, and week-05-spec.md records the same dependency.

Section 9: try and except

The example splits a bill. One entry arrives as text that is not a number, so the conversion fails. try runs the conversion and except handles the failure, and the program carries on.

The section then puts the try inside a small loop, so one bad entry is skipped rather than fatal. That is the shape of week 2's retry.

One practice note is stated here and not softened. Catch the error you named, not every error. A blanket catch hides the message section 10 teaches students to read.

Section 10 in detail: how to read an error message

This is the highest-value section in week 0. A student debugging alone at 11pm has no instructor and no classmate. They have a traceback and whatever they can make of it. Section 10 is the whole answer to that.

It ships in two numbered parts, the way section 8 does, and keeps the one section number so the sidebar links still resolve. Part one is the five step routine and the anatomy of a traceback. Part two walks the six errors. Splitting it keeps each part inside the per-section length budget, which the whole section was sitting on.

Why it is section 10 but reachable from minute one

The build prompt fixes the scope order, and reading errors comes last in it. That order is kept, because two other blueprint documents link to sections by number.

Pedagogy is handled a different way. A short If something breaks card sits in the notebook's second cell, above section 1. It fits on one screen. It carries the five step routine and the six error table, and it links to section 10.

Every section ends with the same one-line pointer to that card. The card also ships as a Canvas page, If something breaks, in the Orientation module. So a student meets it in the first two minutes and can reach it from the course navigation at any point in the term.

Linking that page from inside each weekly module is not built. The template's weekly module has no slot for a standing reference link, so the placement is open question 3 below. Until that is settled, no weekly module links the page.

The routine, five steps

The card and the section both carry these, in this order.

  1. Read the last line of the error first. Everything above it is context.
  2. Name the error type. It is the word before the colon on that last line.
  3. Find your line. Scan the trace for the last line that names your cell.
  4. Print the value on the line above the one that failed.
  5. Change one thing. Run it again.

Step 1 is the one that changes outcomes. Students read tracebacks top down, hit file paths from code they did not write, and conclude the error is unreadable. The section says plainly that Python puts the answer at the bottom.

The anatomy walk

One traceback is walked line by line, on a shopping basket example. The walk labels four parts on screen: the header line, the frames, the arrow at the failing line, and the error type with its message.

The phrase "most recent call last" is explained in plain words, because it is the reason the useful part is at the bottom.

The six errors, and where each one shows up

Each error gets a cell that breaks on purpose. The student predicts the error, runs the cell, reads the message, then fixes it. The fix is in the cell below.

Error What it usually means Where the course produces it
NameError A name is used before it exists, or the cell that makes it has not been run Week 1, running the check cell before the TODO cell. Also every sidebar pasted into a lab cell.
SyntaxError and IndentationError Python could not read the line at all. A missing colon, an unclosed bracket, uneven indenting Any week a student writes a def, an if or a loop
KeyError A dictionary was asked for a key it does not have Week 3 facts, week 4 tool results, week 7 batch rows
TypeError A value was used in a way its kind does not allow, often a None where a dictionary was expected Week 2, when extract_json() returns None and the next line reads a key from it
ValueError, including the JSON decode error The kind was right and the content was wrong. Text that is not JSON Week 2, the exact failure that try/except catches
AttributeError A method was called on a value that does not have it, such as a string method on a list Weeks 3 and 6, when a fact arrives as a list and is treated as text

Those six were chosen from the failures the week specs already predict, not from a general list of Python errors.

The notebook trap

One page of section 10 covers a failure that is specific to notebooks. The cell that raises the error is not always the cell that is wrong.

Cells run in whatever order a student clicks them. A name defined in a cell that was never run does not exist, even though it is on screen. The section says this plainly, and gives the fix: run the cells above, or restart and run all.

This is here because the hosted notebook is the whole environment. There is no command line and no other way to run the code.

What not to do

Three habits waste the most time, and the section names them.

  1. Do not retype the whole cell. The message says which line failed.
  2. Do not delete the error and try something else at random. Read it first.
  3. Do not paste the whole notebook into an AI assistant before reading the last line. The course AI policy permits AI help with disclosure. It works far better when you can say which error you got.

Time and payoff

Section 10 is planned at 30 minutes, between 26 and 36. Week 1 already holds back 10 minutes of its budget for one traceback, so the payoff starts in week 1.

The time arithmetic

Three columns. Fast is a student who has programmed before and is checking. Planned is the figure the blueprint uses. Careful is a student meeting all of it for the first time and doing every optional cell.

Section Fast Planned Careful
Front matter, the time statement, the out-of-scope list, and the self-assessment 12 15 18
1. Variables and strings 16 20 24
2. Calling functions and using return values 20 25 30
3. Lists, dictionaries and keys 34 40 48

All three totals land inside the 4 to 6 hour target. The planned figure sits 63 minutes below the ceiling. The careful figure sits 4 minutes below it, which is almost no slack.

Section 3 is what took that slack. It grew from 30 planned minutes to 40 when the write moves and the nested read were added, because week 1 cannot be written without either. If the careful column has to come back down, the place to look is section 3, not the two sections that carry week 5.

The three sections with the most spread are 3, 8 and 10. All three are deliberate. Section 3 now carries week 1's first TODO cell in full. Section 8 carries the while loop that week 5 depends on. Section 10 is the one section a student is expected to reread later in the term.

These are planned figures and not measured ones. No timing data exists for this student population. They should be re-measured after the Phase 2 build.

What week 0 leaves out

The build prompt requires the exclusions be stated so students do not go hunting. Here is the text students read, on the Overview page and in the notebook.

What this notebook leaves out

You do not need these for this course. You do not have to go find them.

  • Classes and objects.
  • Comprehensions.
  • Reading and writing files, past the cells we wrote for you.
  • Decorators.
  • Virtual environments.
  • Pip, and installing packages.
  • The command line.

Every line you write in a lab uses only what is in this notebook. Say a web search hands you one of the things on this list. You can close that tab.

The self-assessment

Ten questions, one per section. Every question uses everyday subject matter. None mentions email or security.

Delivery. The self-assessment ships as a Canvas ungraded practice quiz, which scores itself and shows the answers. That follows template conflict 27, which puts it in Module 0's quiz slot. The notebook's first cell links to it and reprints the scoring rule, so the two never drift.

Feedback per question names the section the question came from. That is what makes the middle scoring band usable. The name used is the notebook's own heading, word for word, because that is the heading a student scrolls to look for.

The questions, as students see them

Question 1. Section 1, variables and strings.

You run these three lines. What prints?

color = "blue"
color = "green"
print(color)
  • A. blue
  • B. green
  • C. blue green
  • D. An error

Answer: B.

Question 2. Section 2, calling functions and using return values.

You run these four lines. What prints?

def add_fee(price):
    return price + 1

total = add_fee(4)
print(total)
  • A. 5
  • B. 4
  • C. Nothing. The function returns, so nothing reaches the screen.
  • D. An error, because print cannot take a number.

Answer: A.

Question 3. Section 3, lists, dictionaries and keys.

You run these two lines. What prints?

sizes = {"small": 8, "medium": 12, "large": 16}
print(sizes["medium"])
  • A. medium
  • B. 12
  • C. 1
  • D. An error

Answer: B.

Question 4. Section 4, JSON, a dictionary in text form.

A program hands you this, and it is text.

line = '{"city": "Austin", "high": 96}'

You want to read the high by name. What do you do first?

  • A. Nothing. You can read it by name already.
  • B. Turn the text into a dictionary with json.loads().
  • C. Turn it into text with json.dumps().
  • D. Count the characters in it.

Answer: B.

Question 5. Section 5, f-strings.

You run these three lines. What prints?

name = "Sam"
cups = 3
print(f"{name} drank {cups} cups.")
  • A. {name} drank {cups} cups.
  • B. Sam drank 3 cups.
  • C. f Sam drank 3 cups.
  • D. An error

Answer: B.

Question 6. Section 6, writing your own function.

Which line starts a function that takes two values?

  • A. def area(width, height):
  • B. area(width, height)
  • C. def area = width, height
  • D. function area(width, height):

Answer: A.

Question 7. Section 7, if, elif and else.

A parcel desk picks a shipping class from a weight in pounds. You run these lines. What prints?

weight = 3
if weight > 20:
    print("freight")
elif weight > 10:
    print("ground")
else:
    print("letter")
  • A. freight
  • B. ground
  • C. letter
  • D. All three

Answer: C.

The domain is deliberately not a thermostat. Section 7's own worked example is a thermostat with a temp of 41, and it attaches different words to the branches. A question that looked like that cell would punish a student for remembering it.

Question 8. Section 8, loops, in two parts.

You run these lines. What prints?

counts = [2, 5, 1]
total = 0
for c in counts:
    total = total + c
print(total)
  • A. 3
  • B. 8
  • C. 2
  • D. 251

Answer: B.

Question 9. Section 9, try and except.

What does a try and except block do?

  • A. It stops the program the moment a line fails.
  • B. It runs some code, and runs a backup plan if that code fails.
  • C. It makes the code run faster.
  • D. It repeats the code until the code works.

Answer: B.

Question 10. Section 10, how to read an error message.

Your code failed and printed this.

Traceback (most recent call last):
  File "shopping.py", line 7, in <module>
    print(basket["pears"])
KeyError: 'pears'

Which line failed, and why?

  • A. Line 7. The basket has no key named pears.
  • B. Line 1. The file could not be opened.
  • C. Line 7. The word print is spelled wrong.
  • D. There is no way to tell from this message.

Answer: A.

The scoring rule, as students read it

What your score means

This quiz is not graded. It does not count toward anything. Take it as many times as you like.

Each answer names the week 0 section it came from. Use those names.

9 or 10 right. Skip week 0 and start week 1. Keep the notebook handy. Open a section when you want it.

6, 7 or 8 right. Work through the sections your missed questions name. Skip the rest. That is about one to two hours.

5 or fewer right. Work through the whole notebook, in order. Plan about five hours. Most students in this course have never written Python. That is what this notebook is for, and week 1 reads much better after it.

You do not have to finish week 0 before week 1 starts. Week 0 stays open all term.

Look and feel

The build prompt requires the same look and feel as the lab notebooks. The on-ramp follows the standing cell order in code-ledger.md section 2.3, with the parts week 0 does not have removed.

What is the same. Markdown cell, then a code cell that runs, then a plain walkthrough, then a check. Headings in the same style. A "try changing this" prompt at the end of each part, as the sidebars have.

What is different, and why. There is no configuration cell, no token counter, no send_request() and no export cell. Week 0 talks to no network and submits nothing. json is the only import, so nothing needs installing.

Each of the ten sections runs the same seven cells.

  1. Markdown: what this is, and where it shows up in the course.
  2. Code: the worked example, running end to end.
  3. Markdown: the walkthrough, one plain sentence per line of code.
  4. Code: try changing this.
  5. Code: you try. One small task.
  6. Code: the answer to step 5.
  7. Markdown: the link line, naming the week and lab cell this prepares, and the pointer to the error card.

Step 6 ships revealed rather than hidden. Week 0 is ungraded, so a hidden answer costs a stuck student time and protects nothing.

Measured with tools/readability.py. Code blocks, tables and block quotes are excluded by the tool, which is why the student-facing blocks were extracted and measured on their own.
