Assumptions

  1. Section numbers follow the build prompt's scope order. Calling functions is section 2 and f-strings is section 5, which is what week-01-spec.md assumes. Loops are section 8, which is what the week 5 sidebar links to. A renumbering breaks four sidebar links.
  2. while with a counter is added to section 8. The build prompt's scope names for loops only. Week 5 TODO 1 is a while loop with a step cap, and week-05-spec.md records this as a blocking dependency. Declared here rather than resolved quietly.
  3. Lists are added to section 3. The scope list names dictionaries and not lists. A for loop needs something to walk, FACT_ORDER is a list, and the indicators key holds one. .append() comes with them.
  4. A safe key lookup with a default is added to section 3. Two sender reputation records in the corpus match on an address and carry no domain key, so week 4 code that assumes the key fails on those two.
  5. elif and in are added to section 7. Week 4 TODO 1 is an elif chain, and week 6 TODO 2 tests membership.
  6. Joining a list into text is added to section 8. Week 3 TODO 2 and week 7 TODO 1 both build one block of text out of several lines.
  7. Time figures are planned, not measured. They should be re-measured after the Phase 2 build, alongside the week 1 and week 2 time check the build prompt already asks for.
  8. The self-assessment is a Canvas ungraded practice quiz. It is set to unlimited attempts with the answers shown after each submission. That matches the judgment made for the weekly self-checks in week-01-spec.md.
  9. Week 0 gets no CLO mapping and no Canvas Outcome. This follows the build prompt and alignment-matrix.md.
  10. The on-ramp ships as one notebook, not ten. One link, one place to come back to, and a section index at the top. Ten files would be easier to reopen at one section and harder to find.
  11. Writing to a dictionary is added to section 3. The scope list names key access and does not say whether that means reading or writing. Week 1 TODO 1 folds MODEL_EXTRAS into the request dictionary, which needs .update(), and code-ledger.md section 3 records that merge. Assigning a key is taught beside it because it is the same move.
  12. Nested access is added to section 3. The scope list does not name it. Week 1 TODO 1 builds {"messages": [{"role": ..., "content": ...}]}, a list of dictionaries held under a key, and reads the answer back with four chained lookups and a list position in the middle. serving-stack-shape.md fixes both shapes. Week 3 TODO 1 reads two values out of nested dictionaries. Without this part a student meets chained access for the first time in the first cell they are asked to write.

Open questions

  1. Should the instructor see who took the self-assessment, and what they scored? A Canvas practice quiz records the attempt either way. That data would support early outreach, and it could also feel like surveillance to a student told the quiz does not count. The scoring text currently says no one sees the score but the student. That sentence has to change or stay based on your answer.
  2. Is the notebook server available to students on day one? Week 0 must be open from day one. If the hosted notebooks are not provisioned until week 1 opens, week 0 cannot be run when it is needed, and the front-loading promise fails. A downloadable copy is a poor substitute, because students have a browser and nothing else.
  3. How does each weekly module point at the error card? The card now ships two ways: as the notebook's second cell, and as a Canvas page called If something breaks in the Orientation module. What is still undecided is the weekly link. The template's weekly module has no slot for a standing reference link. The two candidates are one line in each week's Overview page, or the same page added as a repeated module item in all seven weeks. This needs a decision before week 3 is built.
  4. How is week 0 attrition tracked? The risk register commits to tracking attrition among students who skip week 0. That measurement needs the quiz attempt data from question 1.
  5. Do students get access before the term opens? Four to six hours of front-loaded work is easier in the week before the term than in the first week of it. This is a college scheduling question, not a build question.
Week 1, call the model from code4,910 words · week-01-spec.md

Course: Agentic AI and Security Operations (ITSY 1377), Northeast Lakeview College, Cyber Defense program Phase 1 blueprint, item 3, week 1 of 7. Prepared 2026-09-03. Audience: the program manager. Nothing here assumes you are building the course.

Field Value
Week title Call the model from code
Supports CLO2
Primary evidence None. Week 1 adds supporting CLO2 evidence that week 2 replaces.
Breadth topics carried B1 and B3, in the reading, the discussion and the self-check
Emails used 001 and 002
Student time, total 4 hours 32 minutes
Estimated tokens, one full lab run about 2,530

A note on terms. A model is the AI service the course sends text to. A notebook is the web page where students run Python. A cell is one block in that notebook. A TODO cell is a cell marked for the student to fill in. Pre-written means the cell arrives finished and working.

A token is a chunk of text, roughly four characters. It is the unit the model is billed and capped in. Temperature is a setting on the model call. A low temperature makes answers steadier. A high one makes them vary more.

A traceback is the block of text Python prints when a line of code fails. The serving stack is the software that runs the model and answers requests.

1. The capability added, and why it is exactly one

Week 1 adds one thing. The student's Python code can send text to the model and read the answer back.

That is the whole capability. Nothing is classified. Nothing is checked. Nothing is stored in a fixed shape. The student writes one function, ask_model(), and uses it twice.

Why this counts as one capability and not several. Students observe three things this week. Answers vary. Temperature changes how much they vary. Longer mail costs more tokens.

All three are properties of the same single call, and none of them needs new code. The pre-written cells run the same ask_model() the student just wrote, six times and then twice more. So the student writes one mechanism, then watches it behave.

Why this is the right first capability. Every later week is this call plus one addition. Week 2 fixes the shape of the answer. Week 3 changes what goes in. Week 4 lets the model ask for a tool. Week 5 puts the call in a loop.

A student who never sees the bare call first will read the week 5 loop as magic. The course's whole claim is that an agent is a loop around a call, not magic. So the call has to come first, and it has to come alone.

Week 1 also carries the continuity promise. The first markdown cell tells students two things in full. Their notebook work is saved for the term. Every later starter notebook already contains a working version of everything through the prior week.

This is said in week 1 so that nobody believes a bad week ends the course.

2. The lab deliverable

One text file, week1_first_calls.txt, written by a single pre-written export cell.

The file holds, under plain headings: the two email ids used, the exact prompt text sent, the six repeated answers for email 001 with the temperature beside each one, the answer for email 002, and the session token totals.

A grader reads that file and can see whether the student's ask_model() worked, whether the six answers actually differ, and what the run cost. No one has to open or run the student's notebook.

3. What the student writes, and what arrives pre-written

Two TODO cells. Eleven to fourteen written lines in total, inside the course target of 5 to 15 lines per lab. Blank lines and comments are not counted.

TODO cell What the student writes Lines
TODO 1, notebook cell 7 ask_model(prompt, max_tokens=512, temperature=0.2). Builds the request dictionary, merges in the extra request fields, calls the pre-written send_request(), returns the answer text. 8 to 10
TODO 2, notebook cell 10 No new function. Parses email 001, sends its body with a one-sentence instruction, prints the answer. 3 to 4

Everything else in the notebook arrives finished. That is thirteen of fifteen cells.

Pre-written cell What it gives the student
Configuration The endpoint, the model name, the extra request fields, and the API key read from the environment
Token counter A running count printed after every call
send_request() and save_artifact() The one network call, and the export writer
.eml parser and email_path() A raw mail file turned into a dictionary of facts
show_email() Subject, sender and the first 400 characters printed
Check cell Tests the student's ask_model() on one plain question
Temperature comparison Six runs of the same prompt, three at 0.2 and three at 1.0
Second email Email 002 sent, with its token line
Export cell Writes week1_first_calls.txt and prints its path

The parser is worth naming for the program manager. Reading a raw mail file into headers, authentication results, links and body text is not a 5 to 15 line job. It is also not what week 1 teaches. So it ships as a module the student imports and never edits.

The same is true of send_request(), which is the only place in the whole course that touches the network. Students call it. They do not write it.

The configuration cell reads the API key from the student's environment. It prints one line saying whether a key was found. It never prints the key, and no cell in any week does.

4. Weekly objectives

Three objectives. All three parent to CLO2. Each names one observable thing the student does in this week's lab.

On the page they sit under the template's lead sentence, "By the end of this module, you will be able to:". Each one ends with its parent in brackets. That is the resolution recorded as conflict 22 in the template conflicts document, applied here rather than left open.

# Objective, as students see it Verb Words Parent
1.1 Classify one email using a model call from your own code. (CLO2) Classify 11 CLO2
1.2 Record how the six answers to one email differ at two temperatures. (CLO2) Record 12 CLO2
1.3 Name one thing to check before trusting the model's answer. (CLO2) Name 10 CLO2

Each is one sentence, under 20 words, with one measurable verb. None joins two verbs with "and". None uses a banned verb. The bracketed parent is a tag rather than part of the sentence, so the word column does not count it.

Why all three parent to CLO2. CLO2 asks a student to classify an email with a model, then check the model's output before trusting it.

Objective 1.1 is that first act at its plainest depth. One email goes to the model from the student's own code, and a phishing judgment comes back. Objective 1.2 produces the reason checking is needed, because one input gives six different answers. Objective 1.3 is the first act of checking, done in plain language before any code does it in week 2.

One act in this lab carries no parent, and this spec says so rather than inventing one. The student writes ask_model(). Writing a Python function is not named by any of the six course objectives.

The build prompt calls an unparented weekly objective an error to report, not a reason to add a course objective. So the function is the means here, not an objective in its own right. What the week measures is the classification the function produces.

Depth is the other thing to notice. Week 1 collects supporting evidence for CLO2. The primary evidence is the week 2 lab. Section 9 states how the two scores interact.

5. The sidebars

The week 1 target names three things: calling a function, using what it returns, and f-strings. An f-string is a Python string that drops values into a sentence.

Two sidebars cover the three, and the merge is worth arguing rather than assuming. Calling a function and using what it returns are one construct, not two. A return value only exists because a call was made, so a sidebar that showed one without the other would show nothing.

Sidebar 1A carries that single construct. F-strings are a separate construct, so they get sidebar 1B. Together the two are 25 minutes.

Line counts below are taken the same way for both sidebars: every line in the code block, including the title comment and any blank line. Both sit well under the 20 line ceiling.

Chosen everyday domain: a grocery stockroom. Never email. Never security.

Sidebar 1A: call a function and keep what it hands back

Eleven lines. Runnable and complete.

# Sidebar 1A: call a function and keep what it hands back
def boxes_in_cases(cases):
    return cases * 6

morning_boxes = boxes_in_cases(4)
afternoon_boxes = boxes_in_cases(9)
day_total = morning_boxes + afternoon_boxes

print(morning_boxes)
print(afternoon_boxes)
print(day_total)

The walkthrough explains it line by line at the course reading level. boxes_in_cases() takes a number of cases. It hands back a number of boxes.

The three lines below it do the part the lab needs. Each call hands a value back. Each value is kept in a name, so it can be used again. day_total is built from two kept values. That works only because nothing was thrown away.

The lab does the same thing one layer down. The student's code calls a function someone else wrote. It keeps what comes back and passes it on.

Nothing here needs a dictionary, a default value, or a comparison. Those belong to later weeks. This sidebar stays inside what week 0 section 2 already taught.

Try changing this. Change the 9 to a 2. Predict all three printed lines before you run it.

Week 0 link. Section 2, calling functions and using return values.

Sidebar 1B: build a sentence from values with an f-string

Seven lines. Runnable and complete.

# Sidebar 1B: build a sentence from values with an f-string
shelf_name = "rice"
on_hand = 4
low_mark = 5
note = f"{shelf_name}: {on_hand} boxes on hand, reorder at {low_mark}."
print(note)
print(f"Short by {low_mark - on_hand} box.")

The walkthrough shows that the f before the quote is what makes the braces work, and that anything inside braces is worked out first and then dropped into the sentence.

Try changing this. Add the day of the week to the note.

Week 0 link. Section 5, f-strings.

Why pasting a sidebar into the lab fails

Neither sidebar shares a single name with the lab. The lab's check cell calls ask_model(), which no sidebar defines, so a student who pastes sidebar code into the TODO cell and runs the check cell gets NameError: name 'ask_model' is not defined rather than a working lab.

6. The reading

One authored page, about 1,350 words, inside the 1,400 word ceiling the course writing standard sets for a reading. It carries breadth topics B1 and B3, and it is the only place either is taught before the final exam.

Worth naming for the program manager: 1,350 words is above the 900 to 1,050 word target the same standard sets, though below its ceiling. This page carries two breadth topics rather than one, which is why it runs long.

What it covers, in two parts.

Part one is B3, the parts of a security operations center. It defines ten things in plain words: assets, users, networks, endpoints, logs, alerts, tickets, incidents, escalation, and documentation.

A labeled diagram then shows how a reported email becomes an alert, then a ticket, then either a closed ticket or an incident. The last paragraph puts a pin on that diagram where this course's own agent sits, between the alert and the ticket. This part doubles as the orientation the prerequisite course does not supply.

Part two is B1, AI in IT and security operations broadly. It separates three things students will otherwise blur together. Scoring AI sorts or rates something, such as a spam filter. Generative AI writes new text or code, such as a chat assistant. An agentic workflow decides its own next step and then acts.

Each of the three gets one plain definition and two examples from IT or security work outside phishing. Students see the breadth before the course narrows.

Citations. The reading cites four authoritative sources and every one is checked to resolve before publishing. Any claim an expert review cannot ground is removed or flagged rather than kept quietly.

Estimated time: 40 minutes, which is the floor of the 40 to 50 minute reading budget. It breaks down in three parts.

Part of the reading Words Minutes
Part one, B3, the ten parts of a security operations center 780 20
Part two, B1, the three kinds of AI 570 10
Opening and skimming one of the four cited sources 10
Total 1,350 40

The course prices one reading pass at 200 words a minute, which is section 2 of the survey content plan. At that rate this page reads once in 6.8 minutes. The other 33 minutes are study time on ten first-time definitions, a labeled diagram and one cited source. The same three-part split appears in sections 3.3 and 5.3 of the survey content plan, so the two documents now agree.

Week 1 is the week the build prompt names for verifying the time budget against a real build. This figure should be re-measured after Phase 2 rather than trusted.

Week 1 also carries the course's heaviest first-time friction. Students open the notebook server for the first time, meet a traceback for the first time, and find out what a token counter is.

That friction is why the week wants slack, and section 11 shows where the slack sits. It is not a reason to price the reading below its band, so the reading is priced at the band.

7. The discussion prompt

Written out below exactly as students will see it. It carries B1 and B3. It requires the student's own exported results, so a general opinion cannot answer it. Estimated time 20 minutes, including one substantive reply.

This text is word for word the same as section 7 of the survey content plan. An earlier draft here asked for about 200 words and set no reply length. Weeks 6 and 7 both ask for about 150 words and about 60, so 150 and 60 is the course figure and this week now matches it.

Where you have already met AI at work

Post by Thursday. Reply to one classmate by Sunday at 11:59 PM Central.

Write about 150 words in your post. Write about 60 words in your reply.

Part 1. Your own results.

Open your file week1_first_calls.txt. Find the six answers your notebook gave to the same email. Quote one short line from one answer. Say which temperature produced it. Then name one thing that answer got right. Name one thing it got wrong or left out. Use your own file. Do not use a classmate's.

Part 2. Where you have met this before.

Name one place you have already met AI in IT work or security work. It can be from a job, from school, from home, or from the news. Then say which of these three kinds it was.

  • Scoring AI, which sorts or rates something. A spam filter is one.
  • Generative AI, which writes new text or code. A chat assistant is one.
  • An agentic workflow, where a tool picks its own next step and then acts.

Then name one part of a security operations center that your example touched. Pick from this week's reading: assets, users, networks, endpoints, logs, alerts, tickets, incidents, escalation, or documentation.

Your reply.

Reply to one classmate who named a different kind than you did. In two or three sentences, say what would change in their example if the tool were agentic. Point to a line in their post. A reply that only agrees does not count.

You may use AI help on this post. If you do, add one line saying what you asked it.

8. The submission artifacts

Three items. Nothing else. No zip files, no folders, no links.

1. The screenshot. One image of the output of notebook cell 11, the temperature comparison cell. It must show all six answers. Cell 11 prints no key material, and the lab page says so.

That cell prints no token line, so the screenshot requirement asks for none. The code ledger sets cell 11 as six answers and nothing else. Token evidence reaches the grader through the exported file instead, which section 2 lists as holding the session token totals.

2. The file. week1_first_calls.txt, attached as a file. The pre-written export cell in notebook cell 14 writes it and prints its full path, so the student can see exactly what to attach.

3. The written answer. Three to five sentences, typed into the submission box. The prompt reads:

Answer all three questions in 3 to 5 sentences. (1) Which of your six answers differed most, and at which temperature? (2) What happened to the token count on email 002, and why? (3) What would you check before trusting this answer in a real ticket?

All three items are gradeable without running the student's notebook.

9. Rubric sketch

Four criteria, 100 points, four performance levels each. The four levels match the mastery scale used across the course, so a rating maps straight across with no translation step.

Every lab in the course carries 100 points, which is what makes the seven labs weigh equally inside the 60 percent Labs group. The grade distribution and token totals document holds the whole table.

# Criterion Points Canvas Outcome
1 Submission completeness. Screenshot, file and written answer are all present and readable. 25 None. Non-CLO expectation.
2 The call works. The file shows six answers for email 001. It shows one answer for email 002. Both show the prompt text you used. 25 CLO2, supporting evidence
3 Variation reported. The written answer names which answer differed most and at which temperature. 25 CLO2, supporting evidence
4 First judgment. The written answer names one specific thing to check before trusting the answer. 25 CLO2, supporting evidence

Why three of the four link to CLO2. Each of the three measures an act named in a week 1 objective, and all three objectives parent to CLO2. Section 4 sets that out. The build prompt lets a criterion go unlinked only when it measures a non-CLO expectation such as submission completeness. Criterion 1 is that case. The other three are not, so they link.

This is a settled design, not an exception request. No exception to the Outcome-link rule is being asked for in week 1.

What the link costs, stated plainly. The six Outcomes report the most recent score, so a week 2 result replaces a week 1 result. CLO2's primary evidence is the week 2 lab, which comes after this one. Nothing recorded here survives in the reported number.

That replacement is the intended behavior. The week 1 score is not for the roll-up. It gives the instructor a reading on every student one week before the score that counts. A student whose own code cannot get an answer out of the model in week 1 will struggle in week 2, and this criterion shows it a week early.

The alignment matrix states the same three links in its own table of rubric criteria, so the two documents agree.

10. The self-check

Six multiple choice items in a Classic Quiz. Unlimited attempts, highest score kept, no time limit. Low stakes by design, and part of the 8 percent that self-checks carry across the course.

Items What they cover
3 B3, the parts of a security operations center. One asks which part a given line belongs to. One asks where this course's agent sits. One asks who receives an escalated ticket.
2 B1, the three kinds of AI. Each describes a tool in plain words and asks which kind it is.
1 The token counter. Asks what the running count is read from, and why it is not the length of the answer text.

Every stem is scenario based and written at the same reading level as the rest of the course. The last item matters more than it looks: on one of the two candidate models the tokens spent thinking are billed but never shown, so a student who measures the visible text would under-report by roughly ten times.

The week finishes 88 minutes under the 6 hour ceiling. Every item sits inside its own band, and the slack is what is left over once each one is priced honestly.

That slack is not headroom to fill. It is what absorbs the first-run friction described in section 6. The build prompt allows reading to be cut only when a week runs past 6 hours, and this week does not, so no cut is taken.

12. Estimated tokens per full lab run

Week 1 sends no system prompt, no facts block, no tool results and no schema. Those arrive in later weeks. The only prompt parts this week are a one-sentence instruction and an email body.

The instruction the lab supplies is one sentence, 94 characters, plus a blank line:

Read the email below. In two sentences, say whether it looks like a phishing attempt, and why.

The arithmetic, in three lines.

  1. One email call sends 96 characters of instruction plus about 800 characters of body. That is 896 characters, and 896 divided by 4 is 224 tokens exactly. A full run makes 9 calls. One is a plain check question of about 60 characters, or 15 tokens. Eight carry an email body. The prompt side is 15 plus 8 times 224, which is 1,807 tokens.
  2. The serving stack measurements recorded 46 to 79 completion tokens for a short answer with reasoning switched off. Taking the top of that range and rounding up to 80 gives 9 times 80, which is 720 completion tokens.
  3. One full run is 1,807 plus 720, which is about 2,530 tokens.

Two caveats the program manager should see.

The 800 character body figure is a stand-in. Emails 001 and 002 are specified in the corpus but not yet generated, so this uses the measured mean body length of the eleven teaching emails already built, which is 772 characters, rounded up.

The reasoning switch is what keeps this number small. One of the two candidate models thinks before it answers by default, and those thinking tokens are billed. The same short call measured 819 to 1,173 completion tokens in that mode, a mean of 951. Nine calls at that mean would put a full run near 10,400 tokens, about four times the 2,530 above.

The gap is wider on completion tokens alone, which is the side the switch acts on: about 720 with reasoning off against about 8,600 with it on.

The notebook configuration cell turns that mode off. It sends reasoning_effort set to "none", and the Phase 2 author should use that field rather than re-derive it. Two request forms turn reasoning fully off on this endpoint; the other is reasoning set to an effort of none.

Five further ways of asking for the same thing were tested. Every one was accepted and then ignored, with no warning and no error. A sixth was rejected outright with an HTTP 400 error, so that one at least fails loudly. The five ignored fields and the rejected one are listed in the serving stack document.

Practical budgeting figure: a student who debugs and reruns the lab once spends about 5,050 tokens in week 1.

This week's figure and the six others sit in one table in the grade distribution and token totals document, together with the course total.

13. Assumptions and open questions

  1. Emails 001 and 002 exist as manifest records but not yet as files. Their body lengths, and therefore the token estimate, use the measured mean of the teaching emails already built.
  2. Week 0 section numbers follow the order the build prompt lists them in. Calling functions and using return values is section 2. F-strings is section
  3. If the week 0 outline renumbers them, both sidebar links change.
  4. The week 1 lab is worth 100 points, the same as every other lab. The Labs group is weighted 60 percent, and a percent-weighted group scores as points earned over points possible inside it. Equal points across the seven labs are therefore what makes the seven weigh equally. The grade distribution and token totals document sets the points for every graded item in the course.
  5. Two videos are counted as 10 minutes together. The build prompt sets a 2 to 4 minute overview video and a separate walkthrough video, with a combined ceiling of 12 minutes.
  6. The self-check is set to unlimited attempts with the highest score kept. This is a judgment call about what "low stakes" means and it is easy to change before Phase 2.
  7. The reading is priced at one pass of 200 words a minute plus study time, the one rate the course uses. No reading-rate measurement exists for this student population. Section 2 of the survey content plan states the rate and section 6 above shows this page's split. The figure should be checked against a real week 1 build.

Measured readability

Measured with the course readability gate on 2026-09-04. This is a program manager document, so the whole page is held to grade 9.5, an average sentence of 18 words and no sentence over 45. Student-facing text drafted inside it is held to grade 7 and an average sentence of 12 words.

Text measured Flesch-Kincaid grade Average sentence Longest sentence
This whole page 5.61 12.4 words 39 words
The three objectives, section 4 5.81 11.0 words 12 words
The discussion prompt, section 7 3.09 8.8 words 17 words
The written answer prompt, section 8 4.54 11.5 words 13 words
The rubric criterion column, section 9 4.75 8.3 words 13 words

No prose paragraph on this page runs over 70 words.

How the short blocks were scored. The readability tool declines to score anything under 40 words. A block under that floor was repeated until it cleared it and then scored, which is the method week 6 uses. Repeating text does not move a Flesch-Kincaid grade, because the formula is a ratio of words to sentences and syllables to words. A rubric row is the criterion column, each cell read as one line, with the header row left out.

Two numbers that moved. Objective 1.1 read "Classify one suspicious email using a model call from your own code." Scored on its own that sentence reads grade 7.77, over the grade 7 ceiling. Dropping the word "suspicious" brings it to 5.86 and loses nothing, because week 1 sends one phishing message and one ordinary internal notice.

The rubric criterion column read grade 7.14, driven by an 18 word criterion 2. Criterion 2 is now three short sentences and the column reads 4.75.

Week 2, structured output4,359 words · week-02-spec.md

Course: Agentic AI and Security Operations (ITSY 1377), Northeast Lakeview College, Cyber Defense program Phase 1 blueprint, item 3, week 2 of 7. Prepared 2026-09-03. Audience: the program manager. Nothing here assumes you are building the course.

Week 2 carries the primary evidence for course learning objective 2 (CLO2). The build prompt fixes the wording of that objective, so it is quoted here word for word.

CLO2 (Analyze): Classify a suspicious email as malicious or benign using an AI model, then validate the model's output before trusting it.

The week 8 final exam adds recognition items on the same objective. The graded proof lives here.

Eight terms are used below.

  • A cell is one block in a notebook that a student runs on its own.
  • A TODO cell is a cell with a comment marking the lines the student writes.
  • Pre-written means the cell arrives complete and working.
  • JSON is a plain text format for writing a set of named values.
  • A schema is the written rule that says which names an answer must carry and which values are allowed.
  • A sidebar is a short, ungraded practice notebook on an everyday topic.
  • The serving stack is the software that runs the model and answers requests. The course tested one of these and wrote down what it returned.
  • A reasoning block is text a model writes to itself before its answer. One of the two course models writes one. The course strips it off.

Capability added

The agent answers in a fixed JSON shape, and the student's code checks that shape before anything uses the answer.

This is exactly one capability, for three reasons.

  1. The model call itself does not change. Week 1 built ask_model(), and week 2 calls it with a different instruction. No new network code appears.
  2. Nothing new enters the prompt. The agent still sends the same email body it sent in week 1, plus a fixed instruction naming the four keys. Headers, authentication results and tool output all stay out until later weeks.
  3. The retry is part of the capability, not a second one. A shape check that catches a bad answer and then stops is not usable. The retry is the handler for the condition the check finds, so the two ship together.

The four keys are verdict, category, confidence and indicators. The allowed values for verdict and category are rendered from corpus/spec/enums.json, which is the same file that renders the week 7 scoring list. So the model is asked for the categories it will later be scored against.

One idea drives the week: a badly shaped answer is a normal condition to handle, not a failure. The serving stack tests recorded a model that ran out of tokens mid-thought and returned an empty answer in 4 of 6 runs at one setting. Students meet that condition on purpose, in a pre-written cell, before they write anything.

Lab deliverable

One file, week2_classification.json, written by a single pre-written export cell.

For each of emails 001, 002, 003 and 004 it holds the email id, the checked answer, schema_ok, problems, and how many tries it took. Every try is recorded, and schema_ok and problems are written on the accepted try as well as on the failed ones. problems is an empty list when the check found nothing wrong.

That last point is what makes the file gradeable. A student who wired in schema_errors() and one who skipped the check and kept the first parse would otherwise look identical on an email that passed on the first try.

The file also holds the session token totals, the model name, and a sample_checks block. That block is the four saved replies the check cell already feeds in, one clean and three broken, each with what the check reported.

So three recorded failures and their problem text sit in every submitted file, whatever the model did on the day. The samples are saved text, so the block costs no extra model calls.

The four emails are set by the corpus design. 001 is a credential phishing message, 002 is a real internal notice, 003 is marketing spam, and 004 is a gift-card style business email compromise attempt. Together they hit four of the seven categories and both verdicts. 003 is the case that proves the two fields are independent: its category is spam and its verdict is benign.

What the student writes, what arrives pre-written

Two TODO cells. Thirteen written lines in the reference solution, up to seventeen in the one-condition-per-line style week 0 teaches. Blank lines and comments are not counted.

Cell What the student writes Lines
TODO 1 extract_json(text). Calls strip_reasoning(), tries json.loads(), returns the dictionary or None. 5 to 7
TODO 2 classify_email(parsed, max_tries=3). Builds the prompt, calls ask_model(), extracts, checks with schema_errors(), logs the try, asks again with the problems attached, returns the checked answer or None. 8 to 10

Seven items arrive pre-written and working this week, on top of everything week 1 established.

Name What it does
OUTPUT_SCHEMA The required answer shape and the allowed values, rendered from corpus/spec/enums.json
CLASSIFY_INSTRUCTIONS The fixed instruction text naming the four keys
build_classify_prompt(parsed) Joins the instructions and the email body into one prompt
strip_reasoning(text) Removes a leading reasoning block, a stray closing tag, and code fences
schema_errors(data, schema) Returns a list of plain-language problems, empty when the answer is good
ATTEMPT_LOG A list holding every try, good or bad
log_attempt(...) Adds one try to that list

Two design notes the program manager should see.

First, schema_errors() is pre-written on purpose. Walking a schema by hand is not a 5 to 15 line job. The student wires it in and decides what to do with what it returns, which is the part that teaches the objective. The retry loop is theirs.

Second, strip_reasoning() is pre-written because the serving stack tests found three places a model's private reasoning text can appear, and one of the two course models produces none at all. Handing students that mess would teach nothing about triage.

Flag: the line count runs slightly long. The build prompt targets 5 to 15 written lines per lab. Week 2 lands at 13 to 17. The extra lines are the retry loop, which is the heart of the objective and cannot be cut. The check cells accept both the compact style and the longer one, and the rubric never rewards shorter code.

Weekly objectives

Three objectives. All three carry CLO2, because week 2 is that objective's primary evidence and adds no other.

# Objective, as students see it Parent Verb Words
2.1 Turn the model's reply into a dictionary your code can read. CLO2 Turn 11
2.2 Check each model answer against the course output schema. CLO2 Check 9
2.3 Retry a failed answer with the schema problems added to the new prompt. CLO2 Retry 13

Each one names something a grader can see in week2_classification.json without opening a student's notebook.

  • 2.1 shows up as a parsed answer under each email id.
  • 2.2 shows up as the schema_ok and problems fields. They are written on every try, including the ones that passed first time.
  • 2.3 shows up as the try count and the problems recorded against each failed try.

Objective 2.3 needs one extra note, because a live retry depends on the model. The serving stack tests produced valid four-key JSON in 3 of 3 runs at the setting week 2 recommends, so a clean four-email run is the likely case rather than the edge case.

The file therefore also carries the sample_checks block described above: three broken saved replies, each with the problems the check reported. A grader always has a recorded failure and its problem text to read. A live try count above one is the stronger evidence when the model supplies it.

Sidebar

Construct: dictionaries and key access, JSON as text, and try/except with a retry.

Domain: weather readings from a rooftop station. No email, no alerts, no verdicts.

The build prompt allows one construct per sidebar, so this week ships three short ones in sequence. All three share the weather domain, so students carry one mental model through them.

Sidebar Construct What it shows Links back to
2A Dictionaries and key access A reading held as {"station": ..., "temp_f": ..., "sky": ...}, read by key, then read with a default when a key is missing Week 0, dictionaries and key access
2B JSON as text The same reading arriving as a line of text, and json.loads() turning it into something you can read by key Week 0, JSON as a dictionary in text form
2C try/except with a retry A loop that asks a fake gauge up to three times, catches a bad line, and stops on the first good reading Week 0, try/except

Each sidebar is under 20 lines, runs end to end, and ends with one small "try changing this" prompt. Sidebar 2C's prompt asks the student to set the gauge to fail every time and watch what the loop does when the tries run out.

Why pasting the sidebar into the lab fails. Sidebar 2C defines read_gauge(line) and returns a reading keyed on station and temp_f, so a student who pastes it where the lab calls extract_json(text) gets a NameError and never reaches the schema check at all.

Reading

Week 2 ships two reading pages rather than one. The reason is set out at the end of this section.

Page 1: Why a fixed answer shape matters. About 900 words. 20 minutes.

What it covers, in order.

  1. What structured output is, and why free text cannot be acted on by code.
  2. JSON as text: a set of named values written as a line of characters. Text that looks like a dictionary is not one until your code loads it.
  3. What a schema is, in plain words, and the four keys this course uses.
  4. Why valid JSON can still be a wrong answer. A reply can load cleanly and still carry a category the course does not use.

Two short JSON blocks are embedded: one good answer, and one that loads cleanly but names a category the course does not use. One stop-and-think question sits inside the page and is ungraded.

Page 2: When the answer comes back wrong. About 950 words. 22 minutes.

What it covers, in order.

  1. Malformed output as a normal condition. The three shapes seen in testing: an answer wrapped in extra text, an answer cut off partway, and an empty answer with a stop reason of length.
  2. The retry, and the one rule that makes it work: send the problem back with the request, not just the request again.
  3. What a student's code should do when the tries run out, so no loop ships with nothing under it.
  4. A short closing on cost. Every retry is another paid call.

Three short JSON blocks are embedded, one per failure shape. One stop-and-think question sits inside the page and is ungraded.

Three citations are planned. Page 1 cites RFC 8259 for the JSON format. Page 2 cites the OWASP Top 10 for Large Language Model Applications entry on improper output handling, and the model vendor's own documentation for the reasoning block behavior.

Every citation is checked for licence and for whether the link resolves before the page ships.

Reading time: 42 minutes, inside the 40 to 50 minute band. The course prices one reading pass at 200 words a minute, which is section 2 of the survey content plan. The rest of each page's minutes are study time on the JSON blocks, the stop-and-think question and one cited source.

Page Prose words One pass at 200 words a minute Blocks, question and a second pass One cited source Page total
1. Why a fixed answer shape matters 900 5 11 4 20
2. When the answer comes back wrong 950 5 14 3 22
Both pages 1,850 10 25 7 42

Why two pages. The build prompt budgets 40 to 50 minutes of reading a week. The writing standard caps one reading page at 1,400 words and targets 900 to 1,050. No realistic pace turns one page into 40 minutes.

Splitting the week's reading settles it without bending either rule. Each page is measured against the 1,400 word cap on its own. Each sits inside the 900 to 1,050 word target. The two together reach the reading band.

This is the rule the build follows wherever the two collide: split the reading, not the standard. It is stated here so it is not decided again week by week.

Discussion prompt

Written out below exactly as students will see it.

Post by Thursday. Reply to one classmate by Sunday at 11:59 PM Central. Write about 150 words in your post. Write about 60 words in your reply.

Open your week2_classification.json file. Find one email where your agent needed more than one try, or where the first answer was wrong.

In your post, give all four of these.

  1. The email id, plus the verdict and category your agent ended up with.
  2. The exact problem your code reported on the failed try. Copy the line.
  3. Your best guess at why the model answered that way.
  4. One change you would make to the instructions to stop it happening again.

If every email passed on the first try, say so. Then post the email your agent was least sure about. Give its confidence number. Say whether you agree with it.

Then reply to one classmate. Compare their failed try to your own. Name one thing that was the same and one thing that was different. A reply that only agrees does not count.

You may use AI help on this post. If you do, add one line saying what you asked it.

Why this cannot be answered without doing the lab. Every part asks for the student's own file: their email id, their problem text, their try count, their confidence number. There is no general opinion to give.

Time: 20 minutes, including the reply.

Submission artifacts

Three items. Nothing else. No zip file, no folder, no link.

Screenshot. One image of the output of the results cell, the one that runs all four emails. It must show all four rows with the email id, verdict, category, confidence and tries used, plus the token line printed under the table. One screenshot, whole output visible. No cell in the notebook prints key material, so no screenshot can capture it.

File. week2_classification.json, attached as itself. The export cell prints the full path and the first few lines, so the student can see what they are attaching.

Written answer, 3 to 5 sentences. Shown to students as written here.

In 3 to 5 sentences, answer this. Which of your four emails took more than one try? Copy the exact problem your code reported on that failed try. If all four passed on try one, say so instead. Then give the lowest confidence number you saw. Either way, name one change you would make to the instructions.

Both paths through that prompt reach full credit on rubric criterion 4. A student whose four emails all passed on the first try is never asked for problem text they do not have.

All three items are gradeable without running the student's notebook.

Rubric sketch

Four criteria, 100 points. Criterion 2 is the only one that carries a Canvas Outcome, which matches the alignment matrix. The other three are declared non-CLO expectations, as the build prompt requires.

Every lab in the course carries 100 points. That is what makes the seven labs weigh equally inside the 60 percent Labs group, and the grade distribution and token totals document holds the whole table.

# Criterion Points Levels Canvas Outcome What earns full credit
1 Submission completeness 15 15 / 10 / 0 None. Non-CLO expectation. All three items are present and readable.
2 Output validation 50 50 / 35 / 20 / 0 CLO2: Classify and validate an AI verdict. Primary evidence. The file carries a checked answer for all four emails. It carries schema_ok and problems on every try, the accepted try included. Every failed try is recorded with the problems that caused it.
3 Results screenshot 15 15 / 10 / 0 None. Non-CLO expectation. The screenshot shows all four emails and the token line, and is readable.
4 Written answer 20 20 / 10 / 0 None. Non-CLO expectation. Three to five sentences carrying the student's own numbers. It names the email that needed more than one try. It copies the problem text their code reported. For a clean run it says so and gives the lowest confidence number in their own file. Either path also names one change to the instructions.

Every level is named and priced. Criterion 2 uses the four labels from the alignment matrix mastery scale. Criteria 1, 3 and 4 use three labels each.

Criterion Level names and points, highest first
2, Output validation Exceeds standard 50, Meets standard 35, Approaching standard 20, No evidence 0
1, Submission completeness Full 15, Partial 10, Missing 0
3, Results screenshot Full 15, Partial 10, Missing 0
4, Written answer Full 20, Partial 10, Missing 0

Two scales on one row, kept apart on purpose. Criterion 2 reports into CLO2, so its row carries two numbers that do different jobs. The rating label the grader picks is what Canvas records against the Outcome, on the shared scale of 4, 3, 2 and 0.

The criterion points on the same row are what the grade uses, on 50, 35, 20 and 0. The four labels line up one to one, so the grader picks once and both scales follow. The criterion points are not the outcome result and are never reported as one.

One build detail to confirm in Canvas before Phase 2. An Outcome-linked criterion may keep its own point values, or it may inherit the Outcome's 4, 3, 2, 0.

If Canvas forces the inherited values, criterion 2 is worth 4 points and the rubric totals 54. The other three criteria are then rescaled to bring the lab back to 100. Week 5 raises the same question about its own scaled criterion.

Nothing in this rubric needs the instructor to run a student's code.

Self-check

Six items, multiple choice, low stakes. Unlimited attempts, highest score kept. Self-checks are 8 percent of the course grade in total.

The six cover:

  1. Which four key names the course output schema requires.
  2. What a stop reason of length means, and what your code should do next.
  3. Why the model's private reasoning text has to come off before the answer is loaded.
  4. Which line of an error message names the thing that failed.
  5. What a retry must add to the second request to be worth making.
  6. Why an answer can load cleanly and still fail the schema check.

Items 1, 5 and 6 sit on this week's objectives. Items 2, 3 and 4 are the three failures students are most likely to meet alone at 11pm.

Time estimate

Activity Minutes

Total 310 minutes, about 5.2 hours

The lab totals 180 minutes, at the top of its 2.5 to 3 hour band. Every other line sits inside its own band too, reading included. Nothing in this week now runs short of a stated band.

The week comes in 50 minutes under the 6 hour ceiling. That margin is the slack week 2 needs, because it is the first week a student writes a loop.

Token estimate per full lab run

Prompt parts measured from the real corpus files. Week 2 sends no facts block and no tool results; those start in weeks 3 and 4.

Line 1. Fixed parts: the instruction text is 603 characters and OUTPUT_SCHEMA rendered from corpus/spec/enums.json is 347 characters, so 950 characters go out on every call. The rendered schema text and its measurement sit in section 2 of the grade distribution and token totals document, and weeks 3, 4, 5 and 6 send the same 347 characters.

Line 2. Email bodies, plain text part: 003 measures 1,118 characters and 004 measures 482 in the rendered files. 001 and 002 are not rendered yet, so each is counted at 772, the mean of the eleven rendered teaching bodies. The four average 786. Prompt per call is (950 + 786) / 4 = 434 tokens.

Line 3. Completion per call is counted at 90 tokens, just above the highest reasoning-off measurement of 79 for a four-key JSON answer. The extra allows for the longer category names and up to six indicators.

So 434 + 90 = 524 tokens per call. A typical run makes 5 calls: 524 x 5 = 2,620 tokens, call it 2,600 per run. A worst case of 12 calls is 524 x 12 = 6,288, call it 6,300.

One warning for the budget. If the reasoning model runs with reasoning left on, measured completion rises to between 819 and 1,173 tokens per call. A typical run then costs about (434 + 1,000) x 5 = 7,170 tokens, nearly three times as much. The configuration cell turns reasoning off for that model, and this is why.

Assumptions and gaps

  1. Emails 001 and 002 are not yet rendered as files. Their body sizes are estimated at 772 characters each, the measured mean of the eleven rendered teaching bodies. The token figure is refreshed once they exist.
  2. The call count of 5 typical and 12 worst case comes from the code ledger and includes the pre-written cell that shows a failing reply.
  3. The lab is scored out of 100 points, the same as every other lab. The seven labs are 60 percent of the course grade in total, and a percent-weighted group scores as points earned over points possible inside it. Equal points across the seven labs are therefore what makes the seven weigh equally. The grade distribution and token totals document sets the points for every graded item in the course.
  4. Criterion 2 is the only Outcome-linked criterion this week, which keeps the alignment matrix exact. If the program wants a second criterion reporting into CLO2, say so before Phase 2.
  5. The 42 minute reading figure is one pass at 200 words a minute plus study time on the five JSON blocks, the two stop-and-think questions, and one cited source per page. It is a working-pace figure, not a comfortable-reading one. It is re-measured after Phase 2, as week 1 is.
  6. Late work and resubmission policy is still unset, so the rubric says nothing about a resubmitted file.

Measured readability

Measured with the course readability gate on 2026-09-04. This is a program manager document, so the whole page is held to grade 9.5, an average sentence of 18 words and no sentence over 45. Student-facing text drafted inside it is held to grade 7 and an average sentence of 12 words.

Text measured Flesch-Kincaid grade Average sentence Longest sentence
This whole page 5.79 13.2 words 34 words
The three objectives 4.79 11.0 words 13 words
The discussion prompt 2.87 8.5 words 18 words
The written answer prompt 3.43 9.5 words 11 words
The rubric criterion and full-credit columns 6.17 10.8 words 17 words

No prose paragraph on this page runs over 70 words.

How the short blocks were scored. The readability tool declines to score anything under 40 words. A block under that floor was repeated until it cleared it and then scored, which is the method week 6 uses. Repeating text does not move a Flesch-Kincaid grade, because the formula is a ratio of words to sentences and syllables to words. A rubric row is the criterion column, each cell read as one line, with the header row left out.

One number that moved. The rubric's criterion and full-credit text read an average sentence of 15.1 words, over the 12 word limit for text a student reads to find out what earns the points. The driver was a 37 word either-or sentence in criterion 4. That sentence is now three short ones, criterion 2's opening sentence was split as well, and the column reads 10.8.

Week 3, grounding3,523 words · week-03-spec.md

Course: Agentic AI and Security Operations (ITSY 1377), Northeast Lakeview College, Cyber Defense program Phase 1 blueprint, item 3, week 3 of 7. Prepared 2026-09-03. Audience: the program manager. Nothing here assumes you are building the course.

Week 3 carries the primary evidence for course learning objective 1 (CLO1): Extract the header, authentication, sender, and URL facts an AI model needs to judge a suspicious email.

Five terms are used below.

Term What it means
Prompt The text sent to the AI model.
Facts block A short labeled list of facts about one email, written into that prompt.
Grounding Sending those facts instead of the raw message text alone.
SPF, DKIM and DMARC Three checks the mail system runs on an arriving message. They test whether it really came from the domain it names.
TODO cell A block in the student's notebook, marked with a comment, where the student writes the code.

The capability added

Students stop sending the raw email body on its own. They build a facts block from what the shipped parser already returns, and they send that instead.

The facts block holds:

  • The sender name, the sender address and the sending domain.
  • The reply address and the subject.
  • The three authentication results.
  • Whether the displayed name matches the sending domain.
  • The link hosts found in the message.
  • The gateway's outside-mail flag and its scan result.
  • Any attachment filename.

The prompt also carries an eleven rule phishing playbook and the list of the organization's own domains and hosts. Both ship as data files.

This is exactly one capability because nothing else in the agent moves. The model call, the strict JSON output, the schema check and the retry all stay as week 2 left them. There is no tool, no loop, and no new output format.

The only change is what goes into the prompt. That is what makes the week's lesson visible. The same code and the same schema produce a different verdict once the facts arrive.

The lab deliverable

Six emails run twice each. First the ungrounded way, body alone, which is week 2's path. Then the grounded way, with the facts block. The notebook prints one comparison table and writes one file, week3_grounding.json.

Per email that file holds:

  • The email id.
  • The ungrounded verdict and its confidence.
  • The grounded verdict and its confidence.
  • Whether the verdict changed.
  • The exact facts block that was sent.

It also holds the session token totals.

The six emails are 001, 004, 005, 006, 017 and 025. Emails 005 and 025 are the matched pair the corpus was built around. Read on the body alone, 005 looks malicious and 025 looks benign. The truth is the reverse.

Grounding has to correct both, in opposite directions, on the same extractor and the same prompt. That symmetry is the point of the week. Grounding is not a way to add suspicion. It is a way to add facts.

What the student writes, and what arrives written

Two TODO cells. Everything else in the notebook is pre-written and working.

Cell What the student writes Lines
TODO 1 build_facts(parsed). Picks the facts worth sending out of the parsed email and returns them as one flat dictionary. 8 to 10
TODO 2 format_facts(facts). Walks a fixed order and builds one labeled text block. 4 to 5

TODO 1 is where a student decides which facts a sound judgment needs. That decision is the week's reading put into practice. TODO 2 is the exact construct the sidebar prepares.

These parts arrive pre-written and working:

  • The configuration cell, the token counter, the request helper and the export helper.
  • All working code from weeks 1 and 2.
  • The data file loader, the known-good domain list and the playbook text.
  • The known-name list that lets the display-name check fire.
  • The fixed fact order and its labels, named FACT_ORDER and FACT_LABELS.
  • The prompt builder and the grounded classifier.
  • The routine that runs both paths over a list of emails.

Two check cells also arrive written. One runs the student's build_facts() on 005 and 025 and prints both results side by side. The other prints the finished facts block for 025 and counts its characters, so students see the size of what they are sending.

One gap, now closed. The code ledger set TODO 1 at 8 to 10 lines and TODO 2 at 5 to 7. Together that is 13 to 17 lines, and the build prompt's ceiling is 15 lines per lab. This spec closes the gap by shipping the fixed fact order and its labels pre-written, as FACT_ORDER and FACT_LABELS. That takes two lines out of TODO 2 and lands the pair at 12 to 15.

The code ledger now carries the same two names and the same line counts, in its week 3 section and in its student line count table.

Weekly objectives

Shown as students see them, under the template's lead sentence, with the parent course objective in brackets.

By the end of this module, you will be able to do these three things.

# Objective Verb Words Parent
3.1 Extract the sender, header, link and mail-check facts into one facts block. (CLO1) Extract 12 CLO1
3.2 Report which verdicts changed after you added the facts block. (CLO1) Report 10 CLO1
3.3 Justify which facts you left out of the prompt. (CLO1) Justify 9 CLO1

Two notes on the wording. "Mail-check facts" is the plain-language name for the SPF, DKIM and DMARC results, so objective 3.1 still covers all four fact groups CLO1 names. Objective 3.2 names what the student does, which is reading the comparison table the notebook prints and reporting what moved. The notebook, not the student, builds that table.

All three sit under CLO1, which is correct for this week. CLO1 is the only objective week 3 carries, and it is carried here as primary evidence. No objective in this week lacks a parent.

Each objective is measured by a rubric criterion that links to the CLO1 Canvas Outcome. Objective 3.1 is measured by criterion 2, objective 3.2 by criterion 3, and objective 3.3 by criterion 4. Criterion 2 carries the primary link. The other two carry supporting links, and the alignment matrix crosswalk lists all three.

The sidebar

Construct. Building a longer string from several parts, in a controlled order. One construct only.

Domain. A class roster. The sidebar prints one line per student for a sign-in sheet. No other sidebar in the course uses this domain. It has nothing to do with email, alerts, headers or verdicts.

# Build one roster line out of several fields, in an order you choose.
roster = {
    "surname": "Okonkwo",
    "given": "Amara",
    "seat": "B4",
}
headings = {"surname": "Last", "given": "First", "seat": "Seat"}
line_order = ["given", "surname", "seat"]

roster_lines = []
for field in line_order:
    roster_lines.append(headings[field] + ": " + roster[field])

roster_block = "\n".join(roster_lines)
print(roster_block)

Fifteen lines, runs as written, one construct. The walkthrough explains each line in plain words. The first dictionary holds the student's details. The second holds the words printed in front of them. The list sets the order. The loop makes one line at a time. The join glues those lines into one block of text.

Try changing this. Move "seat" to the front of line_order. Run it again. The block comes out in a new order. You changed no detail to do it.

Link back. Week 0, the section on variables and strings, and the section on for loops.

Why pasting it into the lab fails. The lab hands format_facts() a dictionary named facts, and it ships its own order and labels as FACT_ORDER and FACT_LABELS. Those four names are the lab's whole vocabulary for this step, and the code ledger fixes them. The sidebar uses roster, headings, line_order, roster_lines and roster_block instead. None of those five exists in the lab, so a student who pastes the sidebar into the TODO cell gets a NameError.

None of the five appears in any other weekly sidebar either. Each of the seven weeks uses a different everyday domain and a different set of names, so no sidebar reads as revision of one a student has already worked through.

The reading

Title. What you put in the prompt is what you expose.

Length. About 1,050 words, plus two short header excerpts and one table. That sits inside the 1,400 word page budget, at the top of the 900 to 1,050 word target.

Estimated time. 45 minutes, inside the 40 to 50 minute weekly budget. It is built the way every week is built: one pass at the course rate of 200 words a minute, then a named line for everything the page asks beyond reading the words once. The rate is stated once for the whole course, in section 2 of the survey content plan.

Part of the reading Minutes
One pass of 1,050 words at 200 words a minute 5
Reading the two header excerpts and the table 15
A second pass over the data minimization section 15
Opening and skimming one cited source 10
Total 45

The reading covers four things.

  • Why a model is a judgment layer over facts you pull out yourself, not a detector.
  • The facts worth pulling: who sent the message, whether the three checks passed, whether the displayed name matches the sending domain, and where the links point.
  • Data minimization, which means sending only what a decision needs. Every line you add to a prompt is a line you have handed to an outside service.
  • The same extraction pattern applied to logs, alerts, tickets and system messages, so students see that the skill is not about email.

Citations come from NIST, CISA and the published mail authentication standards. Every one is checked to resolve before the reading ships.

The discussion prompt

Written below word for word, as students will see it.

Grounding changed a verdict. Which one, and on what fact?

Post by Thursday. Reply to one classmate by Sunday at 11:59 PM Central.

Your notebook ran six emails twice. Once on the body alone. Once with your facts block.

In your post, do three things.

  1. Name one email whose verdict changed. Give the email id. Give the verdict before and the verdict after.
  2. Paste the one line from your facts block that you think changed the answer. One line only.
  3. Say what your model listed as its indicators for that email. Did it name the fact you pasted?

Then reply to one classmate. Look at the line they pasted. Say what your own run on that same email did. Name a number or a line from your own output. A reply that only agrees does not count.

Write about 150 words in your post. Write about 60 words in your reply. Use your own results only.

You may use AI help on this post. If you do, add one line saying what you asked it.

Time. 20 minutes. That covers reading two or three posts, writing one, and writing one reply. It sits inside the 15 to 20 minute budget.

The prompt cannot be answered from general opinion. Every part of it asks for a value the student's own run produced.

What students submit

Three items, and nothing else. No zip file, no repository link, and no cell that shows key material.

  1. Screenshot. The printed comparison table from cell 14. It shows all six email ids with the ungrounded and grounded verdicts side by side, and the token line under it. One image.
  2. File. week3_grounding.json, written by the pre-written export cell. The cell prints the full path, so students see exactly which file to upload. Attach the file itself.
  3. Written answer, 3 to 5 sentences. The prompt students see: Name one fact you chose not to send to the model. Say why a sound verdict does not need it. Then name the one fact that changed a verdict in your run. Give the email id.

The rubric is gradeable from those three items alone. Nobody has to run a student's notebook.

Rubric sketch

Four criteria, twenty-five points each, one hundred points. Four rating levels, matching the mastery scale in the alignment matrix: exceeds standard, meets standard, approaching standard, no evidence.

Every lab in the course carries 100 points. That is what makes the seven labs weigh equally inside the 60 percent Labs group, and the grade distribution and token totals document holds the whole table.

Two scales sit on each Outcome-linked row, as they do in week 2. The rating the grader picks is what Canvas records against the Outcome, on the shared scale of 4, 3, 2 and 0. The criterion points are what the grade uses: 25 for exceeds standard, 19 for meets, 13 for approaching, 0 for no evidence. Those are the mastery ratings scaled by 6.25 and rounded to whole points.

# Criterion Points Judged from Canvas Outcome
1 Submission completeness 25 All three items present and readable None. Non-CLO expectation
2 Fact extraction 25 The facts block in the exported file, per email, plus both verdicts recorded side by side CLO1: Extract email triage facts (primary)
3 Verdict comparison 25 All six emails are in the file, with both paths and the changed flag correct. The same six rows are readable in the screenshot. CLO1: Extract email triage facts (supporting)
4 Written answer 25 Name one fact you left out. Say why it was not needed. Name the fact that moved a verdict, with its email id. CLO1: Extract email triage facts (supporting)

Criterion 2 carries the primary link for CLO1. That matches the alignment matrix, which names week 3 criterion 2 as the primary link.

Criteria 3 and 4 carry supporting links to the same Outcome. Three of the four criteria therefore reach the program's outcome report. That is deliberate. All three weekly objectives sit under CLO1, and an objective with no Outcome-linked criterion produces no outcome data.

Criterion 3 measures objective 3.2, which is the verdict comparison. Criterion 4 measures objective 3.3, which is the data minimization judgment the week's reading teaches. Both are CLO1 work, so both link.

Criterion 1 is the only non-CLO expectation in this rubric. It measures submission completeness and nothing else, which is the one exception the matrix allows.

The self-check

Six items, multiple choice. Practice attempts are unlimited until the due date. The score goes into the self-check group.

The six items cover:

  • Which facts belong in a prompt, and which are exposure with no payoff.
  • What a failed DMARC check on the organization's own domain means.
  • Why a link host on neither list is unknown rather than clean.
  • Why internal mail carries no authentication results at all.
  • What changed between the two verdicts for email 005.
  • How the same extraction step applies to a log line rather than an email.

Tokens per lab run

Character counts are measured from the real corpus files. The average email body is 706 characters, which is the mean over 004, 005 and 006, the three week 3 emails already rendered in Phase 0. The average facts block is 344 characters, taken from the same three. The playbook file is 1,366 characters.

The rendered known-good domain list is 668 characters and the rendered output schema is 347. Both are measured once for the whole blueprint, in section 2 of the grade distribution and token totals document. The system prompt is 184 characters, so the system prompt with the output schema is 531.

  • Ungrounded prompt = 531 + 706 = 1,237 characters, divided by 4, is 309 tokens. Grounded prompt = 531 + 344 + 1,366 + 668 + 706 = 3,615 characters, divided by 4, is 904 tokens.
  • The full run sends six emails both ways, so twelve calls: (309 + 904) times 6 = 7,278 prompt tokens.
  • Completion runs 65 to 79 tokens per JSON answer with reasoning switched off, from the serving stack record. Round to 100 for this week's longer indicator list: 12 times 100 = 1,200. Add one retry, about 1,000. Total is about 9,500 tokens per full lab run.

One caveat belongs beside that number. The serving stack record shows the reasoning model spending 819 to 1,173 completion tokens on the same kind of call when reasoning runs at its default setting. At that rate the same lab costs about 20,500 tokens, more than double. Switching reasoning off is therefore a cost decision as well as a reliability one.

Assumptions and gaps

  1. Emails 001, 017 and 025 are not yet rendered as files. The character averages above come from 004, 005 and 006, the three week 3 emails that exist today. Their bodies measure 482, 1,032 and 603 characters, so the mean is 706. Recompute the token estimate once all six are generated.
  2. The line count fix above assumes FACT_ORDER and FACT_LABELS ship pre-written. The code ledger now names both and sets TODO 2 at 4 to 5 lines. Anyone who changes that ships a lab over the 15 line ceiling.
  3. The lab carries 100 rubric points, the same as every other lab. All seven labs sit in one assignment group weighted at 60 percent. A percent-weighted group scores as points earned over points possible inside it, so the point total on one lab does set that lab's weight against the other six. Equal points are what make them equal. The grade distribution and token totals document sets the points for every graded item in the course.
  4. The token estimate counts one full pass. Students who rerun cells while debugging will spend more. The running token counter in the notebook shows them their own total.
  5. Week 3 sends no tool results, because tools arrive in week 4. The token arithmetic reflects that.
  6. Due dates follow the template: Sunday at 11:59 PM Central. Term dates are still needed before any due date can be set.
