Week 4, tools4,723 words · week-04-spec.md

Course: Agentic AI and Security Operations (ITSY 1377), Northeast Lakeview College, Cyber Defense program Phase 1 blueprint, item 3, week 4 of 7. Prepared 2026-09-03. Audience: the program manager. Nothing here assumes you are building the course. Evidence role: supporting evidence for course learning objective 3 (CLO3). Week 5 carries the primary evidence.

Four terms used throughout. A tool here is an ordinary Python function that the course ships, such as one that looks up a sender's history. A tool call is the model asking, in a structured reply, for one of those functions to run. The model never runs anything. The student's code does.

The capability added, and why it is exactly one

Week 3 left the agent sending a block of extracted facts and getting a verdict back. Week 4 adds one thing: the model may ask for one of three named tools, and the student's code runs it and hands the result back. Nothing else moves. The same facts block, the same answer keys, and the same retry from week 2 all stay as they were.

It is one capability because the agent still asks once and answers once. There is no loop, no choosing a second tool from what the first returned, and no cap on steps. That is week 5. A student who finishes week 4 has an agent that works end to end, and it is one round longer than last week's.

The three tools

They ship in lab/tools/triage_tools.py and are imported, never written by students. Each returns exactly the record the corpus holds for that email, so the answer key and the tool can never disagree.

Two of the three read a list. An allowlist is a list of link hosts the organization has already cleared. A blocklist is a list of link hosts it has already judged bad. The course ships both as small data files.

Tool What it answers
check_sender_reputation(sender) Has this sender or its domain been seen before, and how did those reports close
check_urls(hosts) Is each link host on the allowlist, on the blocklist, internal, or on no list
lookup_recipient(address) Who is this recipient, are they an executive, and what have they reported before

The six emails, and why each one

The lab runs six emails from the corpus. Four of them turn on one tool alone, so a student can see exactly which tool did the work. A fifth, 019, turns on one tool plus the internal delivery path. The sixth, email 003, does not change verdict at all. It is there to show what a recipient's report history looks like when the answer was already right.

Email Tool the week uses What the student sees
008 check_sender_reputation A calm, well-written quote from a vendor. Every header check passes. The domain has three prior reports for the same trick, so the verdict flips to malicious.
018 check_urls A real customer's short note. The sender is a known-good customer, but the link host is on the blocklist, so the verdict flips to malicious.
017 check_urls A payroll notice that reads like a phishing message. The link host is on the allowlist, so the verdict flips to benign.
009 lookup_recipient Three sentences in the chief executive's name from a free webmail account. The recipient record carries the executive flag and prior reports of the same scam.
019 lookup_recipient The chief financial officer asks accounts payable to move a payment. The message never left the company. The verdict is benign, and it still escalates for a phone callback.
003 lookup_recipient (no verdict change) A marketing mailing, benign before and after. The recipient has reported eleven messages in ninety days, and none was a real threat.

Email 019 is the one that teaches the hardest point: a verdict of benign and a decision to escalate are two separate answers, not one.

The lab deliverable

One exported file, week4_tool_calls.json, written by a single pre-written cell. It holds one record per email. Each record carries six things.

  • The email id.
  • The tool the model asked for, and the arguments it sent.
  • The result the student's code returned.
  • The call id the model sent, and the call id the student's tool result message carried back.
  • The verdict without tools, and the verdict with tools.
  • Whether the fallback path ran.

The pair of call ids is there so a grader can read the second TODO cell from the file. Matching ids mean the student's message carried the model's own id back. That is the only evidence in the submission for weekly objective 4.2, so it has to be in the file.

The file also holds the session token totals. A grader reads that file and the screenshot. A grader never opens the student's notebook.

What the student writes, and what arrives pre-written

The student fills two clearly marked TODO cells. A TODO cell is a notebook cell with a comment marking the lines the student writes; every other cell arrives complete and working.

Cell Function Lines the student writes What it does
TODO 1 run_tool(name, arguments, parsed) 8 to 10 Picks which of the three functions to run from the name the model sent, takes the argument from the model's request or from the parsed email, and returns the result. An unknown name returns a plain sentence, not an error.
TODO 2 tool_result_message(tool_call, result) 3 to 4 Builds the message that carries the tool result back to the model, copying the call id across

That is 11 to 14 written lines, inside the course target of 5 to 15. Everything else in the seventeen-cell notebook arrives pre-written and working.

  • The configuration cell, the token counter, and the network helper.
  • All working code from weeks 1 to 3.
  • The three tool definitions, in the format the model expects.
  • The helper that sends a message list with the tools attached.
  • The helper that runs one full round of ask, run, and answer.
  • The comparison run over all six emails.
  • The forced fallback demonstration.
  • The export cell that writes the submission file.

run_tool() is the cell sidebar 4b prepares. Criterion 2 of the rubric scores both TODO cells.

One pre-written helper deserves a note. The serving endpoint has no way to force a tool call. When the model answers in prose instead of asking for a tool, the helper falls back to week 2's manual parsing and records that it did. That fallback fired in none of nine recorded tests, but nothing prevents it, so students meet it as a normal path rather than as a bug.

Weekly objectives

By the end of week 4 you will be able to:

# Objective Parent CLO Words Verb Reported by
4.1 Write one function that runs the tool the model names. CLO3 10 Write Rubric criterion 2
4.2 Build the message that carries a tool result back to the model. CLO3 12 Build Rubric criterion 2
4.3 Compare each email's verdict with tools against its verdict without tools. CLO3 11 Compare Rubric criterion 3

All three name something the student does in this week's lab. All three carry a parent. None joins two verbs with "and", and none uses a banned verb.

Every objective is reported. Criterion 2 reads 4.1 and 4.2 from the exported file. Criterion 3 reads 4.3 from the screenshot and the written answer. Both criteria link to the CLO3 Outcome, so all three objectives produce outcome data for the program.

All three sit under CLO3 rather than under CLO1, and that is deliberate. Week 3 carries the primary evidence for CLO1. The outcome calculation is most recent score, so a week 4 result linked to CLO1 would replace the week 3 primary result in the program's report. Section "Rubric sketch" keeps week 4 off CLO1 for the same reason.

The sidebar, in two parts

The build prompt sets one construct per sidebar, and says that two constructs mean two short sidebars. Its week 4 target names two: defining a function with parameters and a return value, and choosing which function to run from a name. Week 4 therefore ships two short sidebars rather than one. That follows the binding rule and still teaches both constructs.

Domain for both: a music playlist. Never email, never security. Time: 25 minutes for the pair. That is the single sidebar line in the time table below, so the week does not get longer.

Sidebar 4a: write a function that hands an answer back

Construct: defining a function with parameters and a return value. Length: 12 lines, runnable.

The sidebar opens with a dictionary holding two playlists. It then defines two small functions. One counts the songs in a named playlist. The other turns that count into playing minutes. Each takes the playlist name and the dictionary, and each returns a number instead of printing one.

The walkthrough explains every line in ordinary words, at the course reading level. It ends with one ungraded prompt: add a third playlist, then call the counting function on it. It links back to the week 0 section "Defining a function with parameters".

Sidebar 4b: pick the function from a name

Construct: choosing which function to run based on a name. Length: 8 new lines, runnable.

Sidebar 4b opens with the two functions from 4a already written. The only new idea is the choosing. It defines a third function that takes a report name as text and runs one of the two. When the name matches neither, it returns a plain sentence instead of crashing.

The walkthrough explains every line the same way. It ends with one ungraded prompt: ask for a report name that does not exist, and say why a sentence beats a crash. It links back to the week 0 section "if/else".

The names the two sidebars use

They are listed here so the no-collision rule can be checked from this page.

Kind Names
Functions count_songs, playing_minutes, run_report
Parameters playlist_title, shelf, report_title
Dictionary music_shelf, with the keys "road trip" and "study"

None of those names appears anywhere in section 6 of the code ledger, which is the week 4 contract the notebook author builds from. That section holds run_tool, tool_result_message, TOOL_SPECS, ask_with_tools, start_messages, triage_with_tools, check_sender_reputation, check_urls and lookup_recipient, plus the parameters name, arguments, parsed, tool_call and result.

Pasting either sidebar into the lab fails immediately. Sidebar 4b's chooser takes two plain strings. The lab's run_tool() takes three values, one of them a dictionary of arguments the model wrote, so the lab's check cell raises a missing-argument error before any tool runs.

The reading

Title: "The model asks, your code decides." Length: 1,150 words. Budget: 40 minutes.

The page runs 100 words above the 900 to 1,050 word target the course writing standard sets, though well inside its 1,400 word ceiling. The overshoot is point 4 below, the trust boundary. Week 6's lab turns on that distinction, so week 4 is where it has to be taught properly rather than named.

The reading covers six things.

  1. What a tool call is. It is a structured request from the model, not an action.
  2. Why the three tools are ordinary functions rather than questions put to the model. A lookup that must be right every time does not belong to a judgment layer.
  3. The shape of the request. The model's arguments arrive as text and have to be read before use.
  4. The trust boundary. A tool result is data your own code produced. Text inside an email that looks like a tool result is not.
  5. What to do when the model answers in prose instead of asking for a tool.
  6. Cost. Every tool result is sent back to the model, so tools are not free.

Point 4 is placed here on purpose. Week 6 uses an email whose body contains a fake tool result. This reading is where students first meet the distinction that defeats it.

Citations will be drawn from published, openly readable sources on tool use and agent risk, including the OWASP Top 10 for Large Language Model Applications and NIST guidance on AI risk. Every citation is checked by expert subagent review before the reading ships, per the build prompt. Any claim that review cannot ground is flagged rather than kept.

The discussion prompt

Written below exactly as students will see it.

Week 4 discussion: call it before you read it

This post has two halves. Do the first half before you open your file.

Half one. Your prediction.

Take email 018. Read it on the lab page. Do not open week4_tool_calls.json yet.

Name the one tool you would have asked for on that email. Say in one sentence what you expected it to come back with.

Half two. What your agent did.

Now open your file. Name the tool your agent actually asked for. Paste the one fact it got back.

Then say who was right, you or your agent, and how you know. Two or three sentences.

Then reply to one classmate whose prediction missed. Say which fact in their own pasted result settles it. Use one line from your own file too. A reply that only agrees does not count.

Post by Thursday. Reply to one classmate by Sunday at 11:59 PM Central.

Keep your post near 150 words. Keep your reply near 60 words.

You may use AI help on this post. If you do, add one line saying what you asked it.

Why this prompt and not a before-and-after comparison. Week 3 already asks students to quote a pair of their own results and explain the difference. That shape is week 3's whole lesson, so week 3 keeps it and week 4 does something else.

The prediction is what week 4 adds. A student who commits to a tool before reading the file finds out whether they can pick one, which is the judgment the week is really about. The check half still requires their own exported result, so the post cannot be answered from opinion.

Time on task, including the reply, is 18 minutes.

What students submit

Three items, and nothing else. No folders, no archives, no links.

  1. One screenshot. The output of the cell titled "All six emails, with tools and without". That cell prints one row per email with four columns: the email id, the tool asked for, the verdict without tools, and the verdict with tools. It closes with the running token line, which must be in the shot. Section 6 of the code ledger now names the same five printed items for that cell, so the spec and the ledger describe one screenshot. Students are told not to screenshot the configuration cell. That cell prints only whether a key was found, and no cell in any week prints key material.
  2. One file. week4_tool_calls.json, written by the export cell near the end of the notebook. The cell prints the full path and the first few lines, so the student can see what they are attaching.
  3. A written answer of 3 to 5 sentences, typed into the submission box. The prompt reads: "Pick the email where the tool changed your agent's verdict. Name the email and the tool. Give the one fact the tool returned. Then say what your agent decided before it had that fact. If no verdict changed on your run, say which email came closest and why."

Rubric sketch

One hundred points. Every weekly lab in this course is worth 100 points, so the seven labs carry equal weight inside the Labs group. Every criterion is gradeable from the screenshot, the file, and the written answer alone.

# Criterion Points Levels Canvas Outcome
1 Submission completeness. All three items present and readable. 32 32 / 21 / 0 None. Non-CLO expectation: submission completeness.
2 Tool call handled end to end. For each of the six emails the file records one of two things. Either the tool the model asked for, its arguments, the result your code returned, and a tool result message carrying the model's call id. Or the fallback record, showing the prose path was handled. Recorded results match the shipped tool records. 40 40 / 30 / 20 / 0 CLO3: Build a bounded triage agent (supporting evidence)
3 Reading your own results. The written answer names one email, one tool and one returned fact, then says what that fact changed. The screenshot shows the same email's two verdicts. 28 28 / 21 / 14 / 0 CLO3: Build a bounded triage agent (supporting evidence)

Criteria 2 and 3 use the mastery scale set in the alignment matrix: 4 exceeds standard, 3 meets standard, 2 approaching standard, 0 no evidence. Criterion 2 is that scale multiplied by ten and criterion 3 is the same scale multiplied by seven, so each one can actually award its top level and a rating maps across with no translation step.

An earlier draft priced criterion 3 at 3 points on a four-level scale, which made its top level unreachable. Both Outcome-linked criteria are now exact multiples of four.

Both are declared supporting evidence. Week 4 stays a supporting result under CLO3, week 5 stays the primary one, and week 4 stays clear of CLO1, whose primary result is week 3's.

Criterion 2 scores the student's code, not the model's choice. The endpoint cannot force a tool call. When the model answers in prose instead, a correctly written fallback record earns the top level, exactly as a recorded tool call does. The rating text says that in those words, so no student loses points for a path the model picked.

Criterion 2 also carries objectives 4.1 and 4.2. The tool, the arguments and the result report 4.1. The returned call id reports 4.2: when it matches the id the model sent, the student's tool result message was built correctly.

Criterion 3 carries objective 4.3. It links to the CLO3 Outcome because it reports a stated objective with a parent CLO. It does not score the writing itself. Criterion 1 is the only week 4 criterion with no Outcome link, and it measures submission completeness alone.

The self-check

Five multiple choice items, auto-graded, low stakes, unlimited attempts. Here is what they cover.

  1. Who asks for a tool, and who runs it.
  2. Why the arguments the model sends have to be read as text first.
  3. Which of the three tools answers a given question.
  4. What should happen when the model names a tool that does not exist.
  5. What the code does when the model answers in prose instead.

Stems are written at the course reading level.

That is inside the 6 hour ceiling with 75 minutes of slack, and every line sits inside its own budget. Video is 7 minutes against a ceiling of 12. The lab is 2 hours 45 minutes against a range of 2.5 to 3 hours. If week 4 runs long in the first offering, the reading is the first thing cut, and the sidebars and the lab scaffolding are never cut.

Token estimate per full lab run

Measured from the corpus files and the recorded serving stack behavior. A full lab run means every cell in the notebook run once, top to bottom.

One email's first request is 4,653 characters. The four parts marked shared are measured once for the whole blueprint, in section 2 of the grade distribution and token totals document, and weeks 5, 6 and 7 send the same figures.

Part Characters Source
System prompt and the rendered answer schema 531 Shared: 184 plus 347
Facts block from the parsed email 352 Measured on this week's emails
Playbook snippet 1,366 Shared
Known-good domain list 668 Shared
Email body 772 Measured mean of the eleven rendered teaching bodies
The three tool definitions 964 Shared

Divided by 4, that is 1,163 tokens. The second request repeats all of it. It adds the model's tool request and the tool result, about 500 characters more, so 1,288 tokens. The no-tools comparison call drops the tool definitions. That leaves 3,689 characters, or 922 tokens.

A full run makes 18 calls. Six emails run with tools at two calls each. Six run without tools at one call each.

Prompt side: (6 x 1,163) + (6 x 1,288) + (6 x 922) = 20,238 tokens. Completion side, using the measured reasoning-off figures of 34 tokens for a tool request and about 75 for a JSON verdict: (6 x 34) + (6 x 75) + (6 x 75) = 1,104 tokens.

Week 4 total: about 21,300 tokens per full run.

Two figures belong beside that number. First, if the reasoning model is left in its default mode, measured completions rise to roughly 155 tokens for a tool request and roughly 950 for a JSON verdict. The same run then costs about 32,600 tokens.

Second, character count understates the tool block. On the recorded stack, one small tool definition of about 250 characters added 263 prompt tokens, because the chat template wraps tool definitions in extra text.

Twelve of this week's eighteen calls carry the tool block. Treat 21,300 as a floor and roughly 23,700 as the working figure for the reasoning-off path. Section 3.2 of the grade distribution and token totals document carries the same allowance for weeks 5, 6 and 7.

Assumptions and things to confirm

  1. Per-lab points. Every weekly lab in this course is worth 100 points. All seven weekly specs now carry that total, and the Labs group weight of 60 percent divides equally across the seven.
  2. Reading time is built from the one course rate. The course prices one reading pass at 200 words a minute, which is section 2 of the survey content plan, and prices anything a page asks beyond that as its own named line. Week 4 reads 1,150 words in 6 minutes at that rate, then adds 18 for the worked example of a tool request, 6 for the two tables, and 10 for opening one cited source. That is 40 minutes, the floor of the reading band, and it is how the reading line is reported.
  3. Four of the six emails are not yet rendered. Emails 003 and 019 exist as finished files. Emails 008, 009, 017, and 018 are fully specified in the corpus spec, with their tool records written, but have not been generated. Every fact quoted above comes from that spec.
  4. One figure in the token table is projected. The 500 characters added by the model's tool request and the tool result is an estimate, because that text only exists at run time. The system prompt, the answer schema, the facts block, the playbook, the known-good list, the tool definitions and the email bodies are all measured.
  5. Body length. The 772 character body figure is the mean of the eleven rendered teaching emails, which run from 396 to 1,129 characters. It was measured with the course's own corpus/tools/parse_eml.py on the body text, with line endings normalised. Of the two week 4 emails that exist, 019 at 396 characters is the shortest of the eleven, and 003 at 1,118 is close to the longest, which is 013 at 1,129. Individual emails will land above and below the mean. Rerun the figure when the remaining teaching emails are generated.
  6. Model not yet chosen. The estimate assumes the reasoning-off path. The model recommendation is blueprint item 5 and depends on week 5 testing.
  7. Template conflicts that touch this week. Conflict 1, the prompt's eight weekly sections do not map onto the template's four pages. Conflict 15, the template's discussion rubric expects two to three posts per week and this course expects one post plus one reply. Conflict 20, the template makes everything due Sunday, and this discussion asks for a first post by Thursday so that a peer has something to reply to. Conflict 22, the template's objectives table has no column for a parent CLO, and this week's three objectives all carry one. None is resolved here.
  8. No week 4 criterion links to CLO1. Stated above, and repeated here because it is a reporting decision, not a content one. If the program wants week 4 to feed CLO1, the outcome calculation method has to change first.
  9. Three edits made in the code ledger. Section 6 of docs/phase1-blueprint/code-ledger.md is the week 4 contract the notebook author builds from, so it was brought into line with this spec. Its cell 14 now names the five items that cell prints, which is what the graded screenshot shows. Its submission artifact now carries the two call ids. Its sidebar note now points at 4b rather than at a single sidebar. Nothing else in the ledger moved.
  10. The alignment matrix now carries both week 4 criteria. Section 11 of docs/phase1-blueprint/alignment-matrix.md lists every rubric criterion that links to an Outcome. It names week 4 criterion 2, "Tool call handled end to end", and week 4 criterion 3, "Reading your own results", both as supporting evidence for CLO3. The CLO3 row of the main matrix names the same two. The criterion names in both documents match this spec word for word.

Measured readability

Measured with the course readability gate on 2026-09-04. This is a program manager document, so the whole page is held to grade 9.5, an average sentence of 18 words and no sentence over 45. Student-facing text drafted inside it is held to grade 7 and an average sentence of 12 words.

Text measured Flesch-Kincaid grade Average sentence Longest sentence

The three objectives 5.31 11.5 words 12 words

How the short blocks were scored. The readability tool declines to score anything under 40 words. A block under that floor was repeated until it cleared it and then scored, which is the method week 6 uses. Repeating text does not move a Flesch-Kincaid grade, because the formula is a ratio of words to sentences and syllables to words. A rubric row is the criterion column, each cell read as one line, with the header row left out.

Nothing on this page had to be reworded to reach these numbers. The one change made here was to the terms note, which now defines allowlist and blocklist before the tool table uses them.

Week 5, the bounded loop9,209 words · week-05-spec.md

Course: Agentic AI and Security Operations (ITSY 1377), Northeast Lakeview College, Cyber Defense program Phase 1 blueprint, item 3, week 5 of 7. Prepared 2026-09-03. Audience: the program manager. Nothing here assumes you are building the course. Primary evidence for: CLO3, Build an AI agent that selects and runs triage tools in a bounded loop to reach a verdict.

This is the hardest week in the course for students and the least certain week for a small model. It therefore carries the most pre-written scaffolding and only one TODO cell.

Three terms are used throughout. A loop here means the agent repeats three actions until a rule stops it. It looks at what it has, decides what to ask for next, and runs that request. A step is one pass through those three actions.

A small model is one of the two the course runs on. Each holds about three billion parameters, which are the numbers a model learns while it is trained. Three billion is small as these models go. It is small enough that choosing a tool in a loop is the least reliable thing this course asks of one.

1. The capability added

Week 5 adds the loop and nothing else.

By the end of week 4 the agent can ask for one tool, get one result back, and answer. That is one round. Week 5 changes how many rounds happen and who ends them.

The agent may take up to four steps. After each tool result it either answers or asks for a different tool, chosen from the result it just read. An explicit rule ends the run early when one result settles the case.

Why this is exactly one capability

Nothing else moves this week. The prompt parts are week 3's. The three tools are week 4's. The answer keys are week 2's. No new tool is added, no new field appears in the output, and no new data file ships.

The one new thing is control flow, which is the order in which code runs. The student writes one function that counts steps, asks, checks and stops. Every line inside it calls a helper that already exists. So the week adds a shape, not a feature.

2. The lab deliverable

The student runs the finished agent over six emails and exports one record of what happened.

The six emails are chosen so each produces a different loop shape.

Email Steps Why it is here
011 1 The sender is inside the organization, so the first result settles it.
007 1 The sending domain is already known bad.
005 2 A trusted sender and a trusted link host agree.
010 2 The link host is on the block list.
004 2 There is no link at all, so only two tools have anything to look at.
012 3 No tool returns anything decisive, so the run ends with no tool left.

Four of the five stop reasons appear in these six runs. The fifth, step_cap, appears only when the model asks for a tool it has already run. Section 3 says what the code does then.

The exported file holds, for each email: the ordered steps with the tool name, the arguments and a one-line result, the stop reason, the number of steps used, the final verdict, and the tokens that email spent. It also holds the session token totals and the token count of a deliberately uncapped run.

3. What the student writes, and what arrives written

The one TODO cell

Cell What the student writes Lines
TODO 1 run_loop(parsed, prompt_text, max_steps=MAX_STEPS, step_guard=None) 13 to 15

That is the whole of the student's writing this week. The target for a lab is 5 to 15 written lines, and 13 to 15 sits inside it. Blank lines and comments do not count.

The function counts steps, asks the model, stops when the model answers, runs the tool the model asked for, checks for a decisive stop, asks the supplied guard whether another step is allowed, and stops when no tool is left or a cap is reached. It uses only a counter, a while, an if and a return. Every action inside it is one call to a helper that arrives working.

The fourth parameter is a growth hook, and week 5 leaves it empty. step_guard is how week 6 adds a per-run token cap without any student reopening this cell. The student writes one if that calls guard_says_stop(step_guard).

In week 5 nothing is passed in, that helper always answers no, and the line never fires. In week 6 guarded_triage() passes over_token_cap in, and the same line starts firing between steps. The week 5 cell is never edited.

What arrives pre-written

Everything from weeks 1 through 4 arrives as working cells, so a student who never finished week 4 can still build the loop. Eleven new items arrive this week:

Name What it does
MAX_STEPS The hard cap, set to 4
LOOP_INSTRUCTIONS Text telling the model it may ask for one tool per step and must answer when it has enough
loop_prompt(parsed) Joins the loop instructions to week 3's grounded prompt for one email
new_trace(parsed) Makes an empty record of the run, and notes which tools this email can answer
first_call(calls, answer, trace) Picks the one tool call the loop acts on, and covers the model answering with no tool at all
append_turn(messages, turn, tool_call, result, trace) Adds the model's turn and the tool result, and writes the step into the record
decisive_stop(tool_name, result, parsed) Returns a stop reason when one result settles the case, otherwise nothing
tools_left(trace) Names which of the three tools have not run yet and still have something to look at
guard_says_stop(step_guard) Answers whether a supplied guard says the run has spent its budget. Answers no when no guard was supplied
out_of_room(trace) Names why a loop ended when nothing settled the case: tools_exhausted or step_cap
show_trace(trace) Prints one run as a readable table, one row per step

Three of those eleven and one changed signature came out of writing the lab in full. Section 15.1 says which, and why each one is needed.

decisive_stop() is what makes the stop condition a rule rather than a guess. It returns internal_sender when the mail never left the organization. It returns decisive_malicious when a link host is blocked or a sender is known bad. It returns decisive_benign when a trusted sender and a trusted link host agree. The five stop reasons the corpus scores against are decisive_malicious, decisive_benign, internal_sender, tools_exhausted and step_cap.

The other two reasons end a run that nothing settled, and tools_left(trace) is the test for both. The loop keeps going only while a step is left and a usable tool is left.

A tool is usable only when it still has something to look at. check_urls has nothing to check on an email with no link host in it, so it is not left on those emails. That matches the corpus, where fifteen records end on tools_exhausted after two tools rather than three.

A run that spends every usable tool without settling the case ends as tools_exhausted. That is step 3 at the latest, and step 2 on an email with no link. Email 004 is the case students see: two tools run, neither settles it, and the run ends.

A model that asks for check_urls on 004 anyway is answered with an empty result. The step still counts, so the run reaches three steps instead of two. That is the cost of a wrong choice, and the week 5 written answer asks students which second tool their own agent picked.

step_cap ends a run that reaches four steps with a tool still unused. That happens when the model asks for a tool it has already run. The code runs the repeat and the step still counts, which is what the cap is there to catch. The uncapped demonstration cell drops both the cap and the no-tool-left stop, and holds a hard stop at twenty steps.

The starter notebook holds eighteen cells. Two of them exist only to slow the week down. One shows a single pass of the loop written out flat, with every value printed, so a student sees one pass before writing many. The other calls each new helper on its own and prints what it returns.

Section 15 writes all eighteen out. It holds the TODO cell as the student opens it, the reference solution, every markdown cell's text, and every pre-written cell's code.

4. Weekly objectives

Each is one sentence, holds one measurable verb, and names something the student does in this week's lab.

# Objective Parent Words Verb
5.1 Build a triage loop with a four-step cap and a stop condition. CLO3 12 Build
5.2 Record the tool order and stop reason for six triage runs. CLO3 11 Record
5.3 Compare the token cost of a capped run and an uncapped run. CLO3 12 Compare

All three carry CLO3, which is correct for the week that holds its primary evidence. No objective this week is left without a parent.

5. The sidebar

Construct and domain

The construct is a loop that carries information between passes, with a counter cap and a stop condition. The build prompt asks for the longest and most careful sidebar in the course here, and this is it.

The domain is a bus timetable. No other sidebar in the course uses it.

The sidebar walks a route of four stops and reads how many minutes late the bus is at each one. It carries three things forward: how many legs it has ridden, which stops it has already passed, and which stops are still ahead. It stops early when the bus is back on time. It stops anyway after three legs.

The sidebar is 19 lines, runs on its own, and prints a line per leg plus three closing lines. It is written out below in full.

Then it links back to week 0, and that link is a blocking dependency. The build prompt's week 0 scope names for loops and does not name while, so the passage this sidebar needs does not exist yet. The week 0 outline, blueprint item 6, has to add a short while, counter and break passage to its loops section.

The sidebar is written as though that passage is new to the student. It walks the counter, the test and the stop line by line, because for most students this is the first place they meet while. Section 14 item 1 carries the same dependency.

The sidebar code

# Sidebar 5: ride one leg at a time, and stop when a rule says stop.
timetable = {"Oak St": 4, "Vine St": 0, "Rail Yard": 2, "Depot": 3}
stops_ahead = ["Oak St", "Vine St", "Rail Yard", "Depot"]
called_out = []
legs = 0
MAX_LEGS = 3
finish_note = "rode every leg the cap allowed"
while legs < MAX_LEGS and stops_ahead:
    stop_name = stops_ahead.pop(0)
    minutes_late = timetable[stop_name]
    called_out.append(stop_name)
    legs = legs + 1
    print(f"leg {legs}: {stop_name}, {minutes_late} minutes late")
    if minutes_late == 0:
        finish_note = f"stopped at {stop_name}, the bus was back on time"
        break
print(f"legs ridden: {legs}")
print(f"stops called out: {called_out}")
print(f"why it ended: {finish_note}")

Nineteen lines, counted the way week 1 counts them: every line in the block, including the title comment. The ceiling is 20. What it prints:

leg 1: Oak St, 4 minutes late
leg 2: Vine St, 0 minutes late
legs ridden: 2
stops called out: ['Oak St', 'Vine St']
why it ended: stopped at Vine St, the bus was back on time

The walkthrough, as students read it

The first line is a comment. Python ignores it. It is there for you.

timetable holds one number for each stop. The number is how many minutes late the bus is there. stops_ahead is the route, in order. called_out starts empty. It fills up as the ride goes on.

legs is the counter. It starts at zero. MAX_LEGS is the cap. It never changes.

finish_note holds the reason the ride ended. It is set before the loop runs, so there is always a note to print.

Now the loop. while means keep going while both tests are true. The first test is legs < MAX_LEGS. The second test is stops_ahead, which is true while the list still holds a stop. Both have to be true. When either one fails, the loop ends.

Inside the loop, stops_ahead.pop(0) takes the first stop off the front of the list and hands it back. The list is one shorter after that. This is how the loop carries what it learned into the next pass.

timetable[stop_name] reads the minutes for that stop. called_out.append adds the stop to the list of stops already passed.

legs = legs + 1 moves the counter. Without this line the first test never fails, and the loop never stops on the cap.

The print line shows one leg per pass.

The if is the stop condition. When the bus is on time, minutes_late is zero. The note is rewritten and break ends the loop at once. Nothing later in that pass runs.

The last three lines run once, after the loop. They print the counter, the stops called out, and the note.

Try changing this

The one ungraded prompt the sidebar ends with, word for word:

Change MAX_LEGS from 3 to 2. Run it again. Say which of the three closing lines changes.

The answer is none of them. The bus was on time at leg 2, so the stop condition ended the ride before the cap could. That is the lesson the lab needs: a good run ends on its stop condition, and the cap is the guard for the run that does not.

One correction the program manager should see. An earlier draft of this section said the prompt would ask which single closing line changes when the cap is lowered. Writing the sidebar out showed that no arrangement of a three-line closing block changes exactly one line. Either the stop condition fires first, in which case nothing changes, or it never fires, in which case two of the three change. The prompt above takes the first case, because it is the one that teaches the distinction week 5 rests on.

Link back

Week 0, the loops section, specifically the while, counter and break passage that section 14 item 1 asks that section to add.

Why pasting it into the lab fails

Pasted into the lab, the sidebar raises NameError: name 'timetable' is not defined, because the lab has no timetable dictionary, no stops_ahead list and no MAX_LEGS value, and its loop asks a model rather than reading a number from a table.

The names are deliberately disjoint. The sidebar uses timetable, stops_ahead, called_out, legs, MAX_LEGS, finish_note, stop_name and minutes_late. The lab uses parsed, messages, trace, step, MAX_STEPS, tools_left, guard_says_stop and decisive_stop. The shapes differ too: the sidebar carries a list of short strings, the lab carries a record of steps holding a tool name, arguments and a result.

None of those eight names appears in any other sidebar in the course. Each of the seven weeks uses a different everyday domain, and section 5 of every weekly spec lists its own names so the rule can be checked from the page.

6. The reading

Word count: about 1,250 words. Page budget is 1,400 weighted words, so this fits. Estimated time is 40 minutes, itemized in section 11.

The page runs 200 words above the 900 to 1,050 word target the course writing standard sets. The overshoot is the two worked runs printed as tables at the end. This is the hardest week in the course, and those two runs are what a student checks their own trace against.

What it covers, in order:

  1. What the loop is here, drawn as observe, decide, act, with a cap.
  2. What makes a stop condition explicit, and the five stop reasons by name.
  3. How the agent picks its next tool from the result it just read.
  4. Why a wrong second choice costs a step, using the case of an email with no link in it.
  5. What a runaway loop is, and why every pass costs more than the one before it. Each pass carries the whole history, so cost grows faster than the step count.
  6. Why the cap is a control rather than a convenience. It catches the run that spends a step on a tool it already ran, and the page names who owns that control in a security operations team.

The page ends with two worked runs printed as tables, one that stops after one step and one that uses all three tools and then stops with none left. Four ungraded questions ask the student to name the stop reason for each and say which step added nothing.

Citations planned: the NIST AI Risk Management Framework on bounding autonomous behavior, and the OWASP Top 10 for Large Language Model Applications entry on excessive agency. Both are openly readable. Both go to expert review with the draft, and any claim that review cannot ground is removed rather than kept.

7. The discussion prompt

Written below as students will see it. Estimated 20 minutes including one reply.

Open the file your notebook wrote, week5_loop_trace.json. Find the run for email 012. No tool result settled that case. Your agent kept going until no tool was left to try.

Post one message with three parts.

  1. Name the tools your agent chose for 012, in the order it chose them.
  2. Give the stop reason and the step count your own file shows.
  3. In two or three sentences, say what the last step added. Was it worth its tokens?

If your run for 012 stopped for another reason, say which stop reason your file shows and answer for that run.

Then reply to one classmate. Look at the tool order in their post. Say whether your agent chose the same second tool. If it chose a different one, name what your agent had already found out by then.

Post by Thursday. Reply to one classmate by Sunday at 11:59 PM Central.

You may use AI help on this post. If you do, add one line saying what you asked it.

The prompt cannot be answered from opinion. Every part asks for a value out of the student's own exported file, and the reply asks the student to compare that value with a peer's.

8. What students submit

Three items. Nothing is zipped and no link is submitted.

The screenshot. One image of the printed output of the cell named "Run all six emails". All six trace tables must be visible, with the step count and stop reason columns readable. Do not crop off the token total printed under the last table. No cell in the notebook prints key material, so no screenshot can expose it.

The file. week5_loop_trace.json, written by the export cell near the end of the notebook. The cell prints the full path of the file and its first few lines, so the student can see exactly which file to attach. Attach that one file.

The written answer, 3 to 5 sentences. Shown to students as:

Answer in 3 to 5 sentences. Use numbers from your own run.

  1. Which email used the most steps? Give that step count and its stop reason from your file.
  2. Email 004 has no link to check. Name the second tool your agent chose. Say what the first result had told it.
  3. Give both token totals your notebook printed. One is the capped run, one is the uncapped run. Say what the cap saved.

9. Rubric sketch

One hundred points, four criteria, four performance levels each. Every weekly lab in this course is worth 100 points, so the seven labs carry equal weight inside the Labs group. Every criterion is gradeable from the screenshot, the file and the written answer alone. No instructor runs a student's notebook.

# Criterion Points Levels Canvas Outcome
1 Submission completeness. Screenshot, file and written answer are all present and readable. 20 20 / 15 / 10 / 0 None. Declared a non-CLO expectation.
2 Bounded tool loop. The file holds six runs. Each shows an ordered tool list, a step count of four or fewer, and one of the five stop reasons. 40 40 / 30 / 20 / 0 CLO3: Build a bounded triage agent. Primary evidence.
3 Reading your own trace. The written answer names the correct step count, stop reason and second tool from the student's own file. 20 20 / 15 / 10 / 0 CLO3: Build a bounded triage agent. Supporting evidence for objective 5.2.
4 Cost of an uncapped run. The written answer gives both token totals and says what the cap saved. 20 20 / 15 / 10 / 0 CLO3: Build a bounded triage agent. Supporting evidence for objective 5.3.

Three of the four criteria post to the CLO3 Outcome, and all three appear in the alignment matrix crosswalk. Criterion 2 carries the primary evidence. It reads objectives 5.1 and 5.2 out of the exported file.

Criterion 3 is supporting evidence for 5.2, taken from the written answer rather than the file. Criterion 4 is the only measure of objective 5.3, so it is supporting evidence for that one. Criterion 1 is the only criterion with no Outcome link, and it measures submission completeness alone.

Every criterion uses the shared mastery scale of 4, 3, 2 and 0. Criterion 2 scales it by ten and the other three scale it by five, so every rubric rating maps onto an outcome rating with no translation step.

Two build details need confirming in Canvas. First, that a scaled outcome criterion still posts the unscaled rating into the outcome report. Second, which result the CLO3 roll-up keeps when one rubric posts three results to one Outcome under the most recent score method. If that order is not fixed, criterion 2 is the result of record and the other two stay in the outcome detail view.

10. The self-check

Six items, multiple choice, auto-graded, low stakes. Self-checks are 8 percent of the course grade spread across seven weeks.

What the six items cover:

  1. Which of the five stop reasons fits the trace shown.
  2. What makes a stop condition explicit.
  3. Why the cap is needed when the model repeats a tool it already ran.
  4. Which tool to choose second when an email has no link.
  5. What the token counter reads, and what it never measures.
  6. What the code does when the model answers instead of asking.

Every stem is drawn from this week's own agent and corpus. None can be answered from general phishing knowledge.

The lab takes the top of its band and the sidebar takes the top of its band. Both are deliberate. This is the week where a student with no programming background is most likely to stall.

12. Estimated tokens per full lab run

Character counts below come from the corpus files and from the rendered emails for 004, 005, 007, 010, 011 and 012. Completion figures come from the measured serving stack document, using the reasoning-off setting it leans toward.

The four shared parts below are measured once for the whole blueprint, in section 2 of the grade distribution and token totals document. Weeks 4, 6 and 7 send the same figures.

Prompt, fixed per call:  loop instructions 900 + answer schema 347
  + three tool definitions 964 + playbook 1,366 + known-good list 668
  = 4,245 chars; plus this email's facts block and body, 347 + 749 on average
  = 5,341 chars, about 1,335 tokens. Each finished step adds about 430 chars
  of history, about 108 tokens.
Calls in a full run: 16 across the six emails, 3 in the check and demo cells,
  20 in the uncapped demonstration = 39 calls.
  Six-email run:   16 x 1,335 + 15 history increments x 108 = 23,000 tokens.
  Check and demo:   3 x 1,335                               =  4,000 tokens.
  Uncapped run:    20 x 1,335 + 190 increments x 108        = 47,200 tokens.
  Prompt total = 23,000 + 4,000 + 47,200 = about 74,000 tokens.
Completion, measured: 34 tokens per tool request, about 75 per JSON verdict.
  30 tool requests + 9 verdicts = about 1,700 tokens.
Total per full lab run = about 76,000 tokens.

The 190 increments in the uncapped run are the sum of 0 through 19, because call twenty carries nineteen earlier turns. That growth is the week's lesson about cost, written as arithmetic.

Two notes the program manager should have. First, the uncapped demonstration is roughly 47,000 of those 76,000 tokens. That the demonstration costs more than the lab is itself the week's lesson, and the file records both numbers so the student sees it. Second, if the reasoning model runs in its documented default mode instead, completion rises to roughly 13,000 tokens and the run total reaches roughly 87,000.

13. What the measurement found, and what ships

The reliability measurement is done. It ran 294 complete agent runs across both course models and four lab designs, on exactly these six emails. The full report is builder/docs/week5-model-test.md and the raw record is builder/docs/week5-model-test.json.

The design in this spec is the one that works. The shape above gives the model one job: choose the next tool. decisive_stop() in the student's code ends the run. On Granite 4.2 with reasoning off, that shape produced the exact expected tool path in 29 of 30 runs. It ended the run for the right reason in 30 of 30, and reached the right verdict in 28 of 30. The fallback below is not needed. It stays written down as the contingency for a future model version.

Why the stop condition belongs in code, now measured. When the model was left to decide for itself when to stop, it chose the correct first tool almost every time and still produced the correct whole path only 25 of 60 times. The gap was the model not stopping once it had enough. On email 007, whose sender is already known bad, it ran the link check anyway, reached the right verdict, and spent an extra step doing it. Moving the stop into decisive_stop() closed that gap completely: across every model and every email in that design, the run ended for the right reason 88 of 90 times.

The model to standardise on. Granite 4.2 3B with reasoning switched off. The configuration cell sets reasoning_effort to none, gated by model name, because Granite 4.1 rejects that field with an HTTP 400. In its default reasoning mode Granite 4.2 spent an entire 512-token answer budget thinking and returned nothing 18 times out of 18. Raised to 4,096 tokens it works, and still should not ship: 83 seconds and about 7,900 tokens for one triage, against 4.1 seconds and 3,813 tokens for the recommended setting.

Granite 4.1 3B is the documented fallback, with one caveat. In this same design it ends the run for the right reason in 30 of 30 runs, but it picks a different tool order, matching the expected path 0 of 30 times while still reaching the right verdict 20 of 30. The order is what varies, not the termination. This is why the rubric below scores the stop reason and the trace rather than the tool order. A rubric that graded order would fail students for the model's behaviour, and would fail them on one model and not the other.

One thing the measurement changed in this spec. Email 012 was recorded as ending at the step cap. Three tools exist under a cap of four, so a well-behaved agent runs out of tools first, and every run confirmed it. The corpus record, its ground truth entry and section 2 of this spec now all read tools_exhausted. The cap is still taught, through the deliberately uncapped run in section 15.

One thing the measurement says about grading. Email 012 splits the configurations. It is a real vendor on a new domain, with a link host on neither list, asking for names, and most designs read that as an attack. The recommended configuration answered it correctly in 4 runs of 5. Every other configuration together managed 9 of 44, and Granite 4.1 called it malicious in all 20 of its runs.

So the verdict on 012 is not stable across models, and the week 5 rubric awards no points for it. The discussion prompt in section 8 uses it as the example of a wrong answer worth reporting, which holds whichever model the college serves.

The fallback, kept for a future model version

A fixed two-step sequence. Step one is not a choice: the code always calls check_sender_reputation first, with no model involved. decisive_stop() runs on that result exactly as it does now.

If it fires, the run ends and the model is asked once for the verdict. If it does not, the model is handed the first result and picks the second tool from the two that are left, by name. The code runs it, decisive_stop() runs again, and the run ends.

The same six emails, the same five stop reasons, the same decisive_stop(), the same trace record, the same export file, the same rubric and the same submission. MAX_STEPS stays at 4 and still guards the code.

Under the fallback the TODO cell becomes run_sequence(parsed, prompt_text) at 8 to 10 lines, with two written-out passes instead of a while. Lab time drops to about 150 minutes and the estimate drops to roughly 23,000 tokens per lab run.

The agent then chooses one tool instead of up to three, so the trace shows one free choice rather than a sequence of them. CLO3 still reads true. The evidence is thinner, and the alignment matrix would need a one-line note saying so.

Objective 5.1 does not survive that change. It would have to read: Build a two-step triage sequence with an explicit stop condition. That is ten words, one verb, parent CLO3. Objectives 5.2 and 5.3 hold word for word.

14. Assumptions and gaps

  1. Week 0 must cover while loops. The build prompt's week 0 scope names for loops and does not name while. The week 5 lab and sidebar both need a while with a counter. The week 0 outline therefore has to add a short passage on it. That passage is the target of the sidebar's week 0 link in section 5, so it blocks the sidebar. The sidebar teaches the construct from scratch in either case.
  2. LOOP_INSTRUCTIONS is budgeted at 900 characters. The text is not written yet. Every other character count in section 12 is measured, either from a real corpus file, a rendered email, or the rendered prompt parts in section 2 of the grade distribution and token totals document.
  3. The known-good domain list is 668 characters as rendered into a prompt. The shipped data file is 892 characters of JSON, which is not the form the prompt carries. The rendered form is printed and measured once for the whole blueprint, in section 2 of the grade distribution and token totals document, and weeks 3, 4, 6 and 7 use the same figure.
  4. A full lab run is defined as the six-email run, the check cell on 011, the single unrolled pass, and the uncapped demonstration. A student who reruns cells while debugging will spend more.
  5. The scaled outcome criterion in section 9 assumes Canvas posts the unscaled mastery rating into the outcome report. Confirm before the build.
  6. The reading's two citations are planned, not verified. Both go to expert review with the draft.
  7. Email 012 now stops on tools_exhausted, not step_cap. Three tools exist, so a run that repeats none of them ends by step 3. The build prompt fixes the cap at four, so the cap cannot be lowered to three to fit the old figure. The corpus manifest entry for 012 now reads tools_exhausted, and so does its record in the rendered ground truth file. Three other lines changed with them: both notes fields and the phase 0 week-by-week table, plus the week 5 entry in the code ledger. No other record in the corpus used step_cap, and nothing else moved. check_spec.py still reports all rules holding on all 56 records. This is a change to an approved phase 0 artifact and the program manager should see it as one.
  8. Three rubric criteria post to one Outcome. Section 9 links criteria 2, 3 and 4 to CLO3. Confirm which result the most recent score method keeps when three arrive from one rubric at once. The fallback is stated in section 9.
  9. Writing the lab out added three pre-written helpers and changed one signature. loop_prompt(), first_call() and out_of_room() are new, and new_trace(email_id) becomes new_trace(parsed). Section 15.1 gives the reason for each. The code ledger's week 5 section now carries the same eleven names. Nothing the student writes changed: the TODO cell is still one function of 13 to 15 lines.
  10. Two small helpers ship inside the pre-written cells and are not counted in the eleven. usable_tools(parsed) is called once by new_trace(), and one_line(result) is called by append_turn() to shorten a tool result for the trace table. Both are printed in section 15.4. Neither is ever called from the TODO cell.
  11. parse_eml() is assumed to return an email_id key. Section 15's code reads parsed["email_id"]. The code ledger's week 3 section lists the parser keys a correct build_facts() uses and does not list this one, because build_facts() does not send it to the model. Confirm the parser returns it, or run_loop() needs the id passed in as a fourth argument.
  12. The sidebar's "try changing this" prompt changed shape. An earlier draft said one closing line would change when the cap is lowered. No three-line closing block can change exactly one line. Section 5 states the prompt that ships and the reason.
  13. The sidebar is 19 lines, not 18. Counted the way week 1 counts, which includes the title comment. The binding ceiling is 20.

15. The week 5 lab, written out

The build prompt asks for this week to be written in full before the other six weeks depend on its pattern. This section is that. It holds the one TODO cell as the student meets it, the reference solution, every markdown cell's text, and every pre-written cell's code.

15.1 What writing it out changed

Writing the loop out found three gaps in the eight helpers section 3 lists. All three are named here rather than resolved quietly. Section 3's table and the code ledger now carry them.

Name Why the loop needs it
loop_prompt(parsed) Four cells build the same prompt: LOOP_INSTRUCTIONS, then week 3's grounded prompt. Without one helper, those two lines are copied into four pre-written cells.
first_call(calls, answer, trace) Week 4's ask_with_tools() hands back a list of tool calls, because the wire format allows more than one. The loop needs exactly one. This helper takes the first, reads the arguments out of the JSON string the model sends, and covers the case where the model answers in prose and asks for no tool.
out_of_room(trace) Names why a loop ended when nothing settled the case. It returns tools_exhausted when no usable tool is left, and step_cap when one is still unused. Without it the student writes a four line if and else, and the TODO cell goes over its line budget.

One signature changed with them. new_trace(email_id) becomes new_trace(parsed). Section 3 says tools_left(trace) names the tools that have not run yet and still have something to look at. A trace built from an id alone cannot know whether the email has a link host in it, so it cannot answer the second half. Built from the parsed email, it can, and tools_left(trace) keeps its one argument.

That makes eleven pre-written items this week rather than eight. Week 5 is the week the build prompt tells us to pre-write more scaffolding than anywhere else, so the count moving up is the intended direction.

Two more small helpers ship inside the pre-written cells and are never called from the TODO cell: usable_tools(parsed), which new_trace() calls once, and one_line(result), which append_turn() calls to shorten a tool result for the trace table. They are printed below with the cells that hold them.

15.2 The TODO cell, as the student opens it

This is notebook cell 12, and it is the only cell in the week with anything missing.

# TODO 1. Write run_loop().
#
# The six sentences in the cell above are the six things this function does.
# Use only helpers already defined in this notebook. Write no new helper.
#
#   trace     the record of this run. new_trace() makes an empty one.
#   messages  the message list. start_messages() builds the first one.
#   step      your counter. It starts at 0 and goes up by 1 each pass.
#
# The loop keeps going while a step is left and a usable tool is left.
# Stop as soon as trace["stop_reason"] is set. Hand back the trace either way.

def run_loop(parsed, prompt_text, max_steps=MAX_STEPS, step_guard=None):
    # your code here
    return trace

15.3 The reference solution

Fifteen written lines, inside the 13 to 15 this week commits to. Every line calls something that already exists.

def run_loop(parsed, prompt_text, max_steps=MAX_STEPS, step_guard=None):
    trace = new_trace(parsed)
    messages = start_messages(parsed, prompt_text)
    step = 0
    while step < max_steps and tools_left(trace):
        step = step + 1
        answer, finish, turn, calls = ask_with_tools(messages)
        tool_call = first_call(calls, answer, trace)
        result = run_tool(tool_call["name"], tool_call["arguments"], parsed)
        append_turn(messages, turn, tool_call, result, trace)
        trace["stop_reason"] = decisive_stop(tool_call["name"], result, parsed)
        if trace["stop_reason"] or guard_says_stop(step_guard):
            return trace
    trace["stop_reason"] = out_of_room(trace)
    return trace

Four notes on it. The while line carries the two tests section 3 names and no third one. guard_says_stop() always answers no in week 5, because nothing is passed in, so that half of the if cannot fire until week 6.

The last two lines run only when the loop ran out of steps or ran out of tools. tools_left(trace) is never empty at the top of a pass, because the while line just tested it, so first_call() can always name a tool to fall back to.

15.4 The eighteen cells, written out

Cells 2, 3, 4 and 5 carry code the student already has. They arrive from the week 4 notebook unchanged, and the code ledger names every function in them. They are listed here by what they define rather than reprinted, because reprinting four weeks of working code inside a week 5 spec would bury the week 5 content this section exists to show.

Cell 1. Markdown.

Week 5: the bounded loop

This week your agent takes more than one turn. It asks for a tool, reads the result, and decides what to ask for next. A rule stops it. A cap stops it too, when the rule does not.

Everything you built in weeks 1 to 4 is already in this notebook and working. If your own week 4 code never finished, that is fine. Start here and build the loop.

Cell 2. Code, pre-written. Imports and the configuration cell from code ledger section 2.1: BASE_URL, MODEL, MODEL_EXTRAS, API_KEY, EMAIL_DIR and DATA_DIR. It prints one line saying whether a key was found. It never prints the key.

Cell 3. Code, pre-written. The token counter from code ledger section 2.2: USAGE, RUN_USAGE, LAST_CALL, record_usage(), show_tokens(), reset_tokens() and start_run().

Cell 4. Code, pre-written. send_request(payload) and save_artifact(filename, content).

Cell 5. Code, pre-written. Working code from weeks 1 to 4: ask_model(), parse_eml(), email_path(), show_email(), OUTPUT_SCHEMA, strip_reasoning(), schema_errors(), extract_json(), classify_email(), KNOWN_GOOD, PLAYBOOK, KNOWN_NAMES, FACT_ORDER, FACT_LABELS, build_facts(), format_facts(), build_grounded_prompt(), classify_grounded(), the three imported tools, TOOL_SPECS, run_tool(), tool_result_message(), ask_with_tools() and start_messages().

Cell 6. Markdown.

What a loop is here

One step is three actions. The agent looks at what it has. It picks one tool. Your code runs that tool and hands back the result.

The agent may take four steps at most. That number is MAX_STEPS, and it is the cap.

Most runs end sooner. One tool result can settle the case on its own. When it does, decisive_stop() names the reason and the run ends.

There are five stop reasons. Three come from a settled case: decisive_malicious, decisive_benign and internal_sender. Two come from a case nothing settled. tools_exhausted means no usable tool was left. step_cap means the run hit four steps with a tool still unused.

Cell 7. Code, pre-written. The cap, the instructions the model gets, the prompt builder, and an empty run record.

MAX_STEPS = 4
TOOL_NAMES = ["check_sender_reputation", "check_urls", "lookup_recipient"]

LOOP_INSTRUCTIONS = (
    "You are triaging one reported email. You may ask for one tool per step. "
    "Ask for the tool that answers the question you still have. Do not ask "
    "for a tool you have already run. When you have enough to judge the "
    "email, answer with the JSON keys you were given and ask for no more "
    "tools."
)

def loop_prompt(parsed):
    facts_text = format_facts(build_facts(parsed))
    return LOOP_INSTRUCTIONS + "\n\n" + build_grounded_prompt(facts_text,
                                                              parsed["body"])

def usable_tools(parsed):
    # check_urls has nothing to look at on an email with no link host in it.
    if parsed["url_hosts"]:
        return list(TOOL_NAMES)
    return ["check_sender_reputation", "lookup_recipient"]

def new_trace(parsed):
    return {
        "email_id": parsed["email_id"],
        "usable_tools": usable_tools(parsed),
        "steps": [],
        "stop_reason": None,
        "final_answer": None,
        "tokens": 0,
    }

Cell 8. Code, pre-written. One pass of the loop, written out flat, with every value printed. Students see one pass before they write many.

# One pass, unrolled. Email 011 is the shortest run in this lab.
start_run()
parsed = parse_eml(email_path("011"), known_names=KNOWN_NAMES)
prompt_text = loop_prompt(parsed)

trace = new_trace(parsed)
messages = start_messages(parsed, prompt_text)
print("tools usable on this email:", trace["usable_tools"])
print("tools left at the start:", tools_left(trace))

answer, finish, turn, calls = ask_with_tools(messages)
print("finish reason:", finish)

tool_call = first_call(calls, answer, trace)
print("tool the model asked for:", tool_call["name"])
print("arguments it sent:", tool_call["arguments"])

result = run_tool(tool_call["name"], tool_call["arguments"], parsed)
print("what the tool returned:", result)

append_turn(messages, turn, tool_call, result, trace)
print("steps in the record now:", len(trace["steps"]))
print("tools left now:", tools_left(trace))
print("stop reason:", decisive_stop(tool_call["name"], result, parsed))
show_tokens()

Cell 9. Code, pre-written. The five helpers the loop calls, and the two small ones they call.

def one_line(result):
    return json.dumps(result)[:80]

def first_call(calls, answer, trace):
    # The wire format allows more than one call. The loop wants exactly one.
    if calls:
        return {"id": calls[0]["id"],
                "name": calls[0]["function"]["name"],
                "arguments": json.loads(calls[0]["function"]["arguments"])}
    # The model answered instead of asking for a tool. Keep what it said, then
    # keep the run moving with the first tool it has not used yet.
    trace["final_answer"] = extract_json(answer)
    return {"id": "no-call", "name": tools_left(trace)[0], "arguments": {}}

def append_turn(messages, turn, tool_call, result, trace):
    messages.append(turn)
    messages.append(tool_result_message(tool_call, result))
    trace["steps"].append({
        "step": len(trace["steps"]) + 1,
        "tool": tool_call["name"],
        "arguments": tool_call["arguments"],
        "result": one_line(result),
    })
    trace["tokens"] = RUN_USAGE["total_tokens"]

def decisive_stop(tool_name, result, parsed):
    if tool_name == "check_sender_reputation":
        if result.get("status") == "internal":
            return "internal_sender"
        if result.get("status") == "known_bad":
            return "decisive_malicious"
    if tool_name == "check_urls":
        if "blocklisted" in result.values():
            return "decisive_malicious"
        sender = check_sender_reputation(parsed["from_address"])
        if "allowlisted" in result.values() and sender["status"] == "trusted":
            return "decisive_benign"
    return None

def tools_left(trace):
    used = [step["tool"] for step in trace["steps"]]
    return [name for name in trace["usable_tools"] if name not in used]

def guard_says_stop(step_guard):
    # Week 5 passes nothing in, so this always answers no. Week 6 passes a
    # token guard in, and the same line in run_loop() starts firing.
    if step_guard is None:
        return False
    return step_guard()

def out_of_room(trace):
    if tools_left(trace):
        return "step_cap"
    return "tools_exhausted"

def show_trace(trace):
    print(f"{trace['email_id']}  steps: {len(trace['steps'])}  "
          f"stop: {trace['stop_reason']}  tokens: {trace['tokens']}")
    for step in trace["steps"]:
        print(f"   {step['step']}. {step['tool']}({step['arguments']}) "
              f"-> {step['result']}")

decisive_stop() calls check_sender_reputation() a second time on the check_urls branch. That is a local file lookup and costs no model call, and it is what lets the function keep the three arguments the code ledger fixes for it. The alternative was to hand it the whole trace.

Cell 10. Code, pre-written. Each helper called once on its own, so students see what each one hands back before meeting it inside a loop.

demo = new_trace(parsed)
print("empty trace:", demo)
print("tools left on an empty trace:", tools_left(demo))
print("out_of_room says:", out_of_room(demo))
print("guard with nothing passed in says:", guard_says_stop(None))

rep = run_tool("check_sender_reputation", {"sender": parsed["from_address"]},
               parsed)
print("reputation result:", rep)
print("decisive_stop on it:", decisive_stop("check_sender_reputation", rep,
                                            parsed))

append_turn(messages, turn, tool_call, rep, demo)
show_trace(demo)
print("tools left now:", tools_left(demo))

Cell 11. Markdown.

The loop in six sentences

Write these six sentences as code in the next cell.

  1. Start an empty record of the run, and build the first message list.
  2. Keep going while a step is left and a usable tool is left.
  3. Count the step, then ask the model which tool it wants.
  4. Run that tool and add the result to the record.
  5. If the result settles the case, stop and hand back the record.
  6. If nothing settled it, name why the loop ran out and hand back the record.

Cell 12. Code, TODO 1. Printed in section 15.2. The reference solution is in section 15.3.

Cell 13. Code, pre-written. The check cell. It runs the loop on 011 alone, the shortest case in the lab, so a student sees one working run before running six.

start_run()
parsed = parse_eml(email_path("011"), known_names=KNOWN_NAMES)
trace = run_loop(parsed, loop_prompt(parsed))
show_trace(trace)

if trace["stop_reason"] == "internal_sender" and len(trace["steps"]) == 1:
    print("Good. One step, and the stop reason is internal_sender.")
else:
    print("Not yet. Expected one step and internal_sender.")
    print("Read the table above and check your while line.")

Cell 14. Code, pre-written. Named "Run all six emails". Its printed output is the graded screenshot.

LAB_EMAILS = ["011", "007", "005", "010", "004", "012"]
runs = []

for email_id in LAB_EMAILS:
    start_run()
    parsed = parse_eml(email_path(email_id), known_names=KNOWN_NAMES)
    trace = run_loop(parsed, loop_prompt(parsed))
    runs.append(trace)
    show_trace(trace)
    print()

show_tokens()

Cell 15. Code, pre-written. The runaway demonstration. The cap is gone and the no-tool-left test is gone, so only a hard stop at twenty ends it.

start_run()
parsed = parse_eml(email_path("012"), known_names=KNOWN_NAMES)
messages = start_messages(parsed, loop_prompt(parsed))
runaway = new_trace(parsed)

step = 0
while step < 20:
    step = step + 1
    answer, finish, turn, calls = ask_with_tools(messages)
    tool_call = first_call(calls, answer, runaway)
    result = run_tool(tool_call["name"], tool_call["arguments"], parsed)
    append_turn(messages, turn, tool_call, result, runaway)

runaway["stop_reason"] = "hard_stop_20"
show_trace(runaway)
print("capped run on 012 spent:", runs[5]["tokens"], "tokens")
print("uncapped run on 012 spent:", runaway["tokens"], "tokens")

That cell drops both stops on purpose. With no cap and no tools-left test, the model is asked twenty times and keeps asking for tools it has already run. The trace shows the repeats, one row per step, and that is exactly the behaviour the cap exists to catch.

The cell is the only place in the course where a loop is allowed to run past its guard, and it is pre-written so no student writes one by accident.

Cell 16. Markdown.

What the runaway run cost

Look at the two token totals the last cell printed. One is your capped run on email 012. The other is the uncapped run on the same email.

The uncapped run costs many times more, and nothing about it is smarter. It asks for tools it has already run. Every request carries the whole history with it, so each step costs more than the one before.

The cap is not a convenience. It is the control that stops one bad run from spending a week of budget.

Cell 17. Code, pre-written. The export cell.

report = {
    "week": 5,
    "model": MODEL,
    "runs": runs,
    "uncapped_run": {"email_id": runaway["email_id"],
                     "steps": len(runaway["steps"]),
                     "tokens": runaway["tokens"]},
    "session_tokens": USAGE,
}
save_artifact("week5_loop_trace.json", report)

15.5 What the six runs produce

Each run is checked against the corpus record for that email. The table below is the expected shape, taken from the six manifest entries, not from a model run. Section 13 says what happens if a measurement shows the models cannot hold this shape.

Email Tools, in order Steps Stop reason
011 check_sender_reputation 1 internal_sender
007 check_sender_reputation 1 decisive_malicious
005 check_sender_reputation, check_urls 2 decisive_benign
010 check_sender_reputation, check_urls 2 decisive_malicious
004 check_sender_reputation, lookup_recipient 2 tools_exhausted
012 check_sender_reputation, check_urls, lookup_recipient 3 tools_exhausted

Email 004 has no link host, so usable_tools() leaves check_urls out and the run ends after two steps. A model that asks for check_urls on 004 anyway is answered with an empty result, the step still counts, and the run reaches three steps instead of two. That is the cost section 3 describes, and the written answer asks each student which second tool their own agent picked.
