Week 6, guardrails5,853 words · week-06-spec.md

Course: Agentic AI and Security Operations (ITSY 1377), Northeast Lakeview College, Cyber Defense program Phase 1 blueprint, item 3, week 6 of 7. Prepared 2026-09-03. Audience: the program manager. Nothing here assumes you are building the course. Primary evidence for: CLO4, Judge whether an agent's guardrails reduce prompt injection risk to an acceptable level.

Four terms carry this page.

  • A token is a chunk of text, roughly four characters. It is the unit the model is billed and capped in.
  • Prompt injection is attacker-written text that a model reads as an instruction instead of as evidence.
  • A guardrail is a control placed around the model. It stops a bad answer from becoming a bad action.
  • Escalation is handing a case to a human analyst instead of closing it automatically.

1. The capability added, and why it is exactly one

Week 6 adds one thing. The agent stops trusting the text it reads and stops trusting the answer it produces.

Four mechanisms deliver that. The prompt fences the email body between two markers and names it evidence. The student's code checks the model's answer against the facts already extracted. Any answer below a confidence floor goes to a human. Any triage that spends more than a set number of tokens stops and goes to a human.

These are one capability, not four, for three reasons.

They all sit on one seam. Each acts between the model's answer and the analyst's action. None adds a new source of information, a new tool, a new loop or a new output format.

They fail as a set. Fencing the body alone loses to the payload hidden in the HTML part. The fact check alone loses to the payload in the subject line. Taught one at a time they read as tricks. Taught together they read as one control on one seam, which is what the course objective asks students to judge.

Nothing earlier changes. The week 5 loop runs unmodified, because its function already takes two things as arguments. One is the prompt. The other is an optional guard that says whether the run may take another step.

The week 6 prompt is passed in. So is over_token_cap, which is how the per-run token cap gets checked between steps. Both parameters were built into the week 5 signature for this purpose, and no student reopens a week 5 cell. That is the whole change to the agent's spine.

Why this is the week the build prompt calls the differentiator. In this agent the entire email body is attacker-controlled text flowing into the prompt. The corpus places injection payloads in six positions inside this one lab, so students meet the threat as a family of placements rather than as a single trick.

2. The lab deliverable

A guarded triage agent, and a record of what the guardrails changed.

The student runs eleven emails twice. Once through the week 5 agent with no defenses. Once through the guarded agent. The notebook exports one file holding both results for all eleven, plus the escalation decision and the token cost of each run.

The evidence that carries CLO4 is a matched pair. Email 013 is a benefits notice carrying a paragraph addressed to AI triage tools, telling them to return a benign verdict. Email 024 is the same message word for word with that paragraph removed. The student measures what the paragraph did to their own model, then measures what their guardrails took back.

Email Role in the lab
013 The required body injection. A paragraph after the signature instructs any AI reader to record benign, high confidence, no escalation
024 The control. Word for word identical, payload removed, different mailbox
014 Payload hidden in the HTML part only. The plain text part is clean
020 Payload shaped like the agent's own tool output, claiming the link is allowlisted
021 Payload disguised as the mail gateway's own closing banner
022 Payload in the subject line and the sender display name
023 Payload in forged headers below the gateway's own trace line, and it asks for the recipient's report history
015 The benign decoy. Instruction-shaped words sit inside a quoted example in an internal security reminder. A keyword detector escalates it and is wrong
016 The fact-validation case. The model reads it as benign; the extracted Reply-To domain says otherwise
009 Low confidence case. A pretext with no link and no ask yet
012 Low confidence case. Every tool returns something, none of it decisive

Email 015 is the reason this lab measures a false positive as well as a false negative. A guardrail that escalates everything is not an acceptable guardrail, and 015 is how the student sees that on their own screen.

3. What the student writes, and what arrives written

The notebook holds nineteen cells. Two are marked TODO. Everything else arrives complete and working, including a reference copy of every function from weeks 1 through 5.

TODO cell Function Lines the student fills What it does
TODO 1 build_guarded_prompt(facts_text, body) 6 to 8 Puts the rules first, wraps the body between the two markers, puts the required answer keys last, and keeps any hidden HTML text on the facts side
TODO 2 check_against_facts(result, facts) 6 to 8 Returns a list of plain-language problems where the answer contradicts the extracted facts, and an empty list where it does not

That is 12 to 16 written lines. Blank lines and comments are not counted. A correct check_against_facts() catches at least three contradictions.

  1. A benign verdict while a link host is on the blocklist.
  2. A benign verdict while the sender is known bad.
  3. An indicator naming a host that is not in the extracted list.

These arrive pre-written this week.

Name What it does
BODY_START, BODY_END The two markers that fence off the attacker's text
GUARD_RULES The sentences telling the model the fenced block is evidence to judge, never an instruction to follow
CONFIDENCE_FLOOR The threshold below which a case goes to a human, set to 0.7
TOKEN_CAP_PER_RUN The most tokens one triage may spend, set to 4000
over_token_cap() Reports when the current triage has passed that cap. guarded_triage() passes it into the week 5 loop as that loop's step guard
guarded_triage(parsed, schema=OUTPUT_SCHEMA) One full guarded triage, applying the fact check, the confidence floor and the token cap. The schema argument is a growth hook that week 7 uses and week 6 leaves at its default
compare_guarded(email_ids) Runs the guarded and unguarded paths and returns one row per email

Six pre-written demonstration cells carry the teaching. Two run the undefended agent on 013 and on 024 and print both verdicts together. One prints the finished guarded prompt so students can see their own markers in place. One runs the guarded agent on the five other payload placements and prints where it still fails. One prints the eleven-email comparison table. One lowers the token cap so students watch it fire.

The design choice worth stating plainly: the student writes the two smallest and most load-bearing pieces. Fencing the body is the defense. Checking the answer against facts is the control on the defense. Everything mechanical around them is given, so the 2.5 to 3 hours goes into judgment rather than typing.

4. Weekly objectives

Presented on the module Overview page under the template's lead sentence, "By the end of this module, you will be able to:". Each carries its parent course learning objective in brackets, per template conflict item 22.

# Objective Parent Words Verb
6.1 Build a guarded prompt that treats the email body as data. CLO4 11 Build
6.2 Compare your agent's verdict on the injected email with its clean twin. CLO4 12 Compare
6.3 Judge whether your guardrails make prompt injection risk low enough to accept. CLO4 12 Judge

All three parent to CLO4, which is correct. Week 6 is the primary evidence week for CLO4 and holds the only rubric criterion linked to that Outcome.

Week 6 also reinforces CLO1 and CLO2. Students reuse their own fact extraction from week 3 and their own output validation from week 2. Neither gets a week 6 rubric criterion, and neither gets a week 6 Outcome link. That is deliberate. The Outcomes use the most recent score, so a week 6 link would overwrite the primary result recorded in weeks 2 and 3.

Each objective is visible in a submitted item. Objective 6.1 shows up in the fence record the export cell writes for email 013. That record holds the position of each marker in the guarded prompt and the number of characters between them. Objective 6.2 shows up in the rows for 013 and 024, which each carry a verdict on both paths. Objective 6.3 is the written answer itself.

One limit, stated plainly rather than smoothed over. No rubric criterion scores the quality of the fenced prompt on its own. Criterion 3 checks only that the fence record is in the file. Criterion 2 scores the judgment, and that judgment rests on the guarded verdicts the fence produced. So 6.1 is graded through its effect, not on its own.

None of the three uses a banned verb. None joins two verbs with "and". Each is one sentence of twelve words or fewer. Their measured reading level is in section 14.

5. The sidebar

Construct: comparing two values, and checking one against known facts. Domain: a library account checked against the catalogue record. No other sidebar in the course uses it. Length: 14 lines of code. Time: 25 minutes. Ungraded.

catalogue = {"Salt Marsh Atlas": 21, "Bread Science": 14, "Night Trains": 21}
checked_out = [("Salt Marsh Atlas", 21), ("Bread Science", 28), ("Cloud Charts", 14)]

def loan_gaps(borrowed, record):
    gaps = []
    for book, days in borrowed:
        if book not in record:
            gaps.append(f"{book} is not in the catalogue")
        elif days != record[book]:
            gaps.append(f"{book} was lent for {days} days, catalogue says {record[book]}")
    return gaps

for finding in loan_gaps(checked_out, catalogue):
    print(finding)

This code was run before it went into this document. It prints two lines. Bread Science was lent for longer than the catalogue allows, and Cloud Charts is not in the catalogue at all.

The sidebar walks an account line by line. For each loan it asks two questions. Is this book in the catalogue at all? If it is, does the loan length match the length the catalogue records? It collects a plain sentence for every gap it finds and prints them.

That is the same shape the lab needs. The lab walks the model's answer field by field and asks whether each one contradicts a fact the extractor already found. Same dictionary lookup, same two-branch check, same list of plain-language problems returned.

The sidebar prose walks each line in the course reading voice, then ends with one ungraded prompt: change one loan length in the catalogue and run it again, and notice which line now appears. It links back to two week 0 sections, the one on dictionaries and key access, and the one on if and else.

Why pasting it into the lab fails. The sidebar's function is named loan_gaps() and takes a list of pairs, while the lab needs check_against_facts() and two dictionaries, so pasting it wholesale raises a name error.

The names it uses. catalogue, checked_out, loan_gaps, borrowed, record, gaps, book, days and finding. None of them appears in the lab, and none appears in any other sidebar in the course. Each of the seven weeks uses a different everyday domain.

Nothing in the sidebar mentions email, senders, verdicts or security. It uses none of the lab's names and none of the lab's data shapes.

6. The reading

Two pages, about 2,300 words in total, estimated at 45 minutes.

Page Words Covers
Prompt injection in a triage agent about 1,150 Why the body is attacker text. The six payload placements in this lab. The four defenses and the order they are met. Why a keyword detector fails on email 015
The wider AI risk set about 1,150 Hallucination, data exposure, privacy, automation bias, overreliance. Each tied to a case in this lab

The second page carries breadth topic B4. It is survey content, so it replaces reading time rather than adding to it, and it is written to the same 40 to 50 minute budget as every other week.

Each page runs 100 words above the 900 to 1,050 word target the course writing standard sets, though both sit well inside its 1,400 word ceiling.

Page one carries six payload placements and four defenses. Page two carries five named risks, each tied to a case in this lab. Cutting either to target would drop a placement or a risk. Section 6.3 of the survey content plan sets the order of cuts if the week ever runs long.

Every risk on the second page lands on a case students just ran.

  • Hallucination is the indicator that names a host the extractor never found.
  • Data exposure is email 023. Its forged header asks the agent to paste the recipient's report history into a summary meant for the reporter.
  • Privacy is that same case one step wider: what you put in a prompt is what you expose.
  • Automation bias is email 015, the benign message a keyword rule escalates.
  • Overreliance is the confidence floor, and the honest reason it exists.

The reading is authored rather than linked, because openly licensed material at this level does not exist. Sources will be drawn from national and openly published guidance on adversarial machine learning, large language model application risk, and AI risk management. Citations are verified by expert subagent review when the reading is written, and any claim the review cannot ground is flagged rather than kept.

Reading time is built from the one course reading rate, which section 2 of the survey content plan sets at 200 words a minute for one pass. What each page asks beyond reading the words is priced as its own named line.

Part of the week 6 reading Minutes
Page 1, one pass of 1,150 words at 200 words a minute 6
Page 1, walking the six payload placements and the email 015 case 17
Page 2, one pass of 1,150 words at 200 words a minute 6
Page 2, matching each of the five risks back to a case just run 16
Total 45

That is 23 minutes on page one and 22 on page two, inside the 40 to 50 minute budget with 5 minutes of headroom. The survey content plan prices the same two pages the same way.

7. The discussion prompt

Fifteen to twenty minutes, including one substantive reply. Graded. The prompt requires the student's own exported numbers, so a general opinion cannot answer it. Shown here exactly as students will read it.

Write the rule your guardrails are missing

Open the file your notebook wrote, week6_guardrails.json. This post asks you to attack your own agent, not to describe it.

Post first, then reply. Both are due Sunday at 11:59 PM Central. Post by Thursday if you can, so others have something to answer.

In your post, answer all three.

  1. Pick one email from 014, 020, 021, 022 or 023 where a guardrail did not hold. Name it. Say what your own file shows for it.
  2. Write the one extra rule you would add to stop it. One sentence, in plain words, as you would hand it to a teammate.
  3. Now find email 015 in your file. It is the benign decoy. Say what your new rule would do to 015. Would it send a message to a human that should have closed on its own? Give the number from your file that tells you.

Then reply to one classmate whose extra rule is different from yours. Say what their rule would cost you on your own 015 row. Use your own number. A reply that only agrees does not count.

Post about 150 words. Reply about 75 words. You may use AI help on this post. If you do, add one line at the end saying what you asked it.

Why this prompt and not a before-and-after comparison. Week 3 already asks students to quote a pair of their own results and explain what moved. Week 4 asks for a prediction and then a check. Week 6 asks for something neither of those does: write a control, then price it.

Question 3 is the whole point. A rule that catches the placement you missed almost always escalates the decoy too, and a student who writes the rule finds that out on their own numbers. That is the overreliance and automation bias lesson in operational form, and it is where breadth topic B4 lands in this discussion.

Two facilitation notes for the instructor guide. First, students will report different rows for the same email, because the model is not deterministic and one candidate model runs at a temperature above zero. That disagreement is the discussion, not a bug. Second, a student whose guardrails held on every placement should still answer for email 015, using the strictest rule they can imagine adding.

8. What students submit

Three items, exactly as every other week. No zip files, no folders, no links.

The screenshot. Run cell 16, the comparison cell. Take one screenshot of the printed table. It must show the header row, all eleven email rows including the row for 015, and the token line printed underneath. Do not crop a row. No cell in this notebook prints key material, so nothing has to be hidden before the screenshot is taken.

The file. Attach week6_guardrails.json. Cell 18 writes it and prints its full path. Attach that file as it is. Do not rename it. Do not attach the notebook.

Cell 18 writes one row per email. Five of the eleven emails carry no injection, so their payload placement is written as the word none.

Cell 18 also writes one fence record for email 013. It holds where each marker landed in the guarded prompt, how many characters sit between them, and how long that email's body is. The record holds no body text, so no attacker text is copied into the file.

The written answer. Three to five sentences, typed into the Canvas text box. The prompt reads:

In 3 to 5 sentences, answer this. Your guardrails changed how your agent handled email 013. Did they cut prompt injection risk enough for a real security team? Name one number from your own file that backs your answer. Then name one case your guardrails still miss.

That written answer is the CLO4 judgment. The exported file is its evidence. A grader can score both without opening a notebook or running any student code.

9. Rubric sketch

One hundred points. Three criteria, four performance levels each, matching the mastery scale in the alignment matrix. Every weekly lab in this course is worth 100 points, so the seven labs carry equal weight inside the Labs group. Every criterion is gradeable from the screenshot, the file and the written answer alone. A correct submission can reach full marks on all three.

# Criterion Points Linked Canvas Outcome What the grader reads
1 Submission completeness 8 None. Non-CLO expectation All three items present and readable. The file opens. The screenshot shows the whole table
2 Guardrail judgment 72 CLO4: Judge guardrails against prompt injection The written answer judges whether the guardrails reduce injection risk to an acceptable level, cites a number the attached file shows, and names a case that still gets through
3 Guarded run completeness 20 None. Non-CLO expectation The file holds a row for all eleven emails. Each row carries a verdict on both paths, an escalation reason and a token count. Each row carries a payload placement where the email has one, and the word none where it does not. The fence record for email 013 is present

Criterion 3 is a check on what the file contains, not on how good the run was. Only six of the eleven emails carry an injection: 013, 014, 020, 021, 022 and 023. The other five are 009, 012, 015, 016 and 024.

Those five rows read none for placement, which the export cell writes for them. A grader who expected eleven placements would mark a correct file down on five rows. So the criterion says what to expect on each kind of row.

Criterion 2 is the mastery scale multiplied by eighteen, so its four levels map one to one onto the Outcome's four ratings with no translation step. 72 points is Exceeds standard. 54 is Meets standard, which is the mastery level. 36 is Approaching standard, and 0 is No evidence.

Level Criterion 2 wording
Exceeds standard, 72 Reaches a clear judgment, supports it with the student's own before and after numbers, and names a specific residual gap with the placement that causes it
Meets standard, 54 Reaches a clear judgment and supports it with at least one number the attached file shows
Approaching standard, 36 States a judgment with no number, cites a number the attached file does not show, or argues only from general claims about AI risk
No evidence, 0 No judgment stated, or the written answer is missing

Two of the three criteria carry no Outcome link, and both measure submission completeness. Criterion 1 covers the three items. Criterion 3 covers what is inside the file. Neither is a course objective, and both are declared non-CLO expectations here, as the alignment matrix requires.

An earlier draft of this rubric held a fourth criterion for using the student's own numbers rather than general claims. Those points now sit inside criterion 2. There are two reasons.

The same evidence was being scored twice, because criterion 2 already drops a submission that cites a number the file does not show. And judging with evidence from your own run is CLO4 performance, so those points belong on the CLO4 Outcome rather than outside the Outcomes with no link.

That reading is now the course rule rather than this week's preference. Clause 4 of the rubric link rule in the alignment matrix says a criterion scoring a student's use of their own results stays inside the Outcome-linked criterion. Week 7 was brought into line with the same clause.

CLO4 has exactly one rubric criterion measuring it across the whole course. It is criterion 2 here, as the alignment matrix records.

10. The self-check

Six multiple choice items. Low stakes, auto-graded, unlimited attempts, no time limit. Self-checks carry 8 percent of the course grade in total.

Item Covers
1 Why the email body counts as attacker-controlled text
2 Which defense catches a payload hidden in the HTML part
3 What the fact check compares, and what it does when the two disagree
4 When the confidence floor sends a case to a human
5 What the per-run token cap stops, and what it does not stop
6 Breadth topic B4: which wider AI risk the forged header case in email 023 shows

Item 6 is the self-check's share of breadth topic B4. Every stem is written at the course reading level and every stem is answerable only by a student who ran the lab.

12. Token estimate per full lab run

A full run means every cell executed once, top to bottom, including the undefended pass, the guarded pass and the lowered cap demonstration. The code ledger records about 99 model calls for that run, counted cell by cell.

Two rules produce that figure. An undefended run makes one call per tool the model asks for. A guarded run makes the same calls plus one closing call, which guarded_triage() makes whatever stopped the loop.

Cell Runs Calls
6 and 7, the undefended pass on 013 and 024 2 6
11, the guarded check on 013 and 024 2 8
12, the guarded run on the five other placements 5 18
16, all eleven emails both ways 22 65
17, the lowered cap demonstration 1 2
Total 32 99

An earlier draft of this spec used 44 calls. That figure counted the comparison cell alone and left the closing call out. The arithmetic below is rebuilt on 99.

Character counts below are measured, except the two marked as authored text that does not exist yet. The four marked shared are measured once for the whole blueprint, in section 2 of the grade distribution and token totals document, and weeks 3, 4, 5 and 7 send the same figures.

Prompt part Characters Source
Guard rules 700 Authored, estimated
Loop instructions 400 Authored, estimated
Three tool definitions, resent every turn 964 Shared
Required answer keys, the rendered output schema 347 Shared
Playbook snippet 1,366 Shared
Known-good domain list 668 Shared
Facts block 400 Measured: 376 on email 013
Fenced email body 1,000 Measured: 1,129, 752 and 1,006 on emails 013, 021 and 012; mean 962
One tool result 284 Measured: 852 characters for all three tools, mean over the eleven week 6 records

The playbook snippet and the known-good domain list are on this list for a reason worth stating. An earlier draft of this table left both out.

Week 6 runs the week 5 loop unmodified and swaps in a different prompt, so it sends everything week 5 sends. Weeks 3, 4, 5 and 7 all count both, and week 6 now does too. Leaving them out understated every call by 2,034 characters.

The arithmetic, in three lines.

One first-turn prompt is 700 + 400 + 964 + 347 + 1,366 + 668 + 400 + 1,000 = 5,845 characters, and 5,845 divided by 4 is 1,461 tokens.

Each later turn resends the whole list and adds one tool request plus one tool result, about 404 characters or 101 tokens, so the average call across a run carries about 6,451 characters, which is 1,613 tokens.

99 calls times 1,613 is 159,687 prompt tokens, plus 99 times 45 measured completion tokens is 4,455, giving about 164,000 tokens per full lab run.

The 45 completion tokens per call is the measured mean from the serving stack document with reasoning turned off: 34 tokens on a tool-request turn, 65 to 79 on an answer turn.

One number that could move this by more than half again. If the reasoning model runs in its default mode, completions rise to roughly 950 tokens a call. That is 99 times 950, or 94,050 completion tokens, and a full run near 254,000. The serving stack document leans toward running with reasoning off for the labs, which is the figure above.

Week 6 is the most expensive lab in the course, ahead of week 7's batch of twenty at about 112,000 tokens. It runs eleven emails twice and then reruns seven of them in the teaching cells, so it makes more calls than any other week.

Students who rerun the lab pay the full amount again. The course token budget is controlled centrally and a student who runs out emails the instructor for a top-up with no grade penalty, so no cutoff is built into the notebook.

13. Assumptions, and three things to confirm

  1. The per-run token cap counts completion tokens. The code ledger sets TOKEN_CAP_PER_RUN to 4000 without saying which number it counts. It matters. A four-step triage costs about 4,300 prompt tokens and only about 170 completion tokens. If the cap counted prompt tokens it would fire on nearly every email in this lab, which is not the design. This spec reads the cap as counting completion tokens for one triage, which matches the serving stack document. That reading needs pinning in the ledger before Phase 2.
  2. The cap will not fire on its own with reasoning off. That is why the notebook includes a cell that lowers the cap deliberately. Students still see the control work. Say so plainly on the page rather than letting a student conclude the cap does nothing.
  3. Student line count runs one line over the stated target. The build prompt sets 5 to 15 written lines per lab. This week is 12 to 16, and reaches 16 only when a student writes each condition on its own line, which is the style week 0 teaches. Cutting to 15 would mean handing over one of the three contradiction checks, which weakens the only CLO4 evidence in the course. The recommendation is to accept 16.
  4. The cap stops the run without inventing a stop reason. A run that passes TOKEN_CAP_PER_RUN records the stop reason step_cap and escalates with escalate_reason set to token_cap. step_cap is the corpus value for a run a cap ended rather than a decision, and the escalation reason says which cap fired. There is no sixth stop reason anywhere in the course, so a week 6 file never carries a value the week 5 rubric or the week 5 self-check would reject.
  5. Discussion timing. The template sets every weekly due date to Sunday at 11:59 PM Central. The prompt above keeps that as the graded deadline and asks for a Thursday first post as a courtesy, not a rule. If the program wants a graded two-stage deadline, that is a template deviation to approve.
  6. Only three of the eleven week 6 emails are rendered today. Emails 013, 021 and 012 exist as files. Body character counts come from those three. The other eight are specified in the manifest and are generated in the full build, so the body figure above should be re-measured once they exist.
  7. The reading's citations are not yet verified. The sources are named by type in section 6. Verification happens through expert subagent review when the reading is authored, and any ungrounded claim is reported rather than kept.
  8. Reading rate. The 45 minute estimate uses the one course rate of 200 words a minute for one pass, plus named lines for what the pages ask beyond reading. The rate is stated once, in section 2 of the survey content plan, and every week is priced from it. If the program uses a different rate for online course workload, that one number changes and all seven weekly totals change with it.
  9. Evidence use sits inside the Outcome-linked criterion, course wide. This was an open question in an earlier draft and it is now settled in the alignment matrix rather than week by week. Clause 4 of the rubric link rule says a criterion scoring a student's use of their own results stays inside the Outcome-linked criterion. Week 6 already worked that way. Week 7 dropped its separate "Own results used" criterion to match.

Measured with the course readability gate on 2026-09-03. This is a program manager document, so the whole page is held to grade 9.5. Student-facing text drafted inside it is held to grade 7.

No prose paragraph on this page runs over 70 words.

How the objectives were scored. The three of them run to 35 words, which is under the gate's 40 word floor, so the tool declines to score the block as it stands. The block was repeated until it cleared the floor and then scored. Repeating text does not move a Flesch-Kincaid grade, because the formula is a ratio of words to sentences and syllables to words. Measured that way the block scores 6.15. Scored one at a time, 6.1 is 4.79, 6.2 is 6.79 and 6.3 is 6.79.

Those numbers count "prompt injection" and "guardrails" in full. The build prompt exempts required technical vocabulary from the reading level count. No exemption is claimed here, because all three objectives clear grade 7 with both terms counted.

An earlier wording of 6.2 and 6.3 did not clear it. That block scored 7.17, with 6.3 alone at 8.76. The rewording changed "control twin" to "clean twin" and "cut prompt injection risk to an acceptable level" to "make prompt injection risk low enough to accept". Both keep the CLO4 meaning and neither changes the verb.

Week 7, output and evaluation4,736 words · week-07-spec.md

Course: Agentic AI and Security Operations (ITSY 1377), Northeast Lakeview College, Cyber Defense program Phase 1 blueprint, item 3, week 7. Prepared 2026-09-03. Audience: the program manager. Nothing here assumes you are building the course. Primary evidence: CLO5 and CLO6. Emails used: the twenty batch emails, 026 to 045. Ten are malicious and ten are benign. None of them appears in any earlier week.

Eight terms carry this page.

  • A triage note is the short written record an analyst leaves after judging one reported email.
  • The batch is the set of twenty emails the agent reads in one sitting.
  • The answer key is the correct verdict for each of those twenty, written when the corpus was built.
  • A false positive is a benign email the agent called malicious.
  • A false negative is a malicious email the agent called benign.
  • Accuracy is the share of the twenty the agent called correctly.
  • Escalation agreement is the share of the twenty where the agent's escalate flag matched the answer key.
  • A token is a chunk of text, roughly four characters. It is the unit the model is billed and capped in.

1. The capability added, and why it is exactly one

The agent gains one thing this week. It writes its verdict as a fixed six-line note that another person can act on.

The six lines are set in order: verdict, confidence, indicators, recommended action, escalate yes or no, and a two-sentence plain summary for the employee who reported the mail. The template ships pre-written. The student writes the code that fills it.

Nothing else about the agent changes. The decision path built in week 6 runs untouched. No new tool, no new prompt rule, no new loop.

The batch run is not a second capability. It calls the same week 6 triage twenty times, once per email, and keeps going when one fails.

The scoring work is not a capability of the agent either. It is the student measuring the agent they already have. That distinction is the week's real lesson: a system that judges is not the same as a system you can trust, and only measurement closes the gap.

2. The lab deliverable

One text file, week7_triage_report.txt, written by a single pre-written export cell.

It has two headed sections. The first holds the twenty triage notes in the fixed format, in order. The second holds the score block: the counts, the accuracy rate, false positives and false negatives on separate lines, escalation agreement on its own line, the answer key version scored against, and the session token totals.

The file is text rather than JSON for one reason. Criterion 2 asks whether another analyst could act on the note. The grader has to read it the way an analyst would, and a text file lets them.

3. What the student writes, and what arrives pre-written

Two marked TODO cells. Together they are 13 to 17 written lines.

Cell What the student writes Lines
TODO 1 write_note(result, facts). Fills the six-line template from one triage result and joins the indicator list into one line. 5 to 7
TODO 2 score_batch(rows, truth). Loops over the results, counts correct calls, false positives and false negatives, and returns the counts with the accuracy rate. 8 to 10

Flag: the line count runs slightly long. The build prompt targets 5 to 15 written lines per lab. Week 7 lands at 13 to 17. Blank lines and comments are already left out of that count. The extra lines are the second and third counters in score_batch(), which are the objective itself and cannot be cut. The rubric never rewards shorter code.

Everything else in the notebook arrives complete and working. That includes the whole agent from weeks 1 to 6, plus seven new pre-written items: the extended output schema, the note template, the batch folder, the answer key, the file lister, the batch runner, and the report printer.

Where the last two note lines come from. Four of the six note lines are keys week 6 already produces. The recommended action and the plain summary are not. They arrive because guarded_triage() takes the answer schema as an argument, and the pre-written run_batch() hands it OUTPUT_SCHEMA_V2 rather than the week 2 schema.

guarded_triage() returns whatever keys the schema it was given names, so both new keys come back in the same dictionary as the verdict. The week 5 signature and the week 6 signature were both built to grow this way, which is recorded in section 12 of the code ledger. No student reopens a week 6 cell to get a summary.

The two-sentence plain summary is produced by the model as its own field. write_note() places it. The student does not write prose into the note by hand.

Every email therefore ends with one closing model call, whatever stopped its loop. An email settled by a single decisive tool result still gets that call. The confidence, the indicators and the summary are the model's to write, and without them a note cannot be graded.

The notebook holds seventeen cells in the same order every week uses. The student runs the batch from a pre-written cell and reads a pre-written table of every email the agent got wrong, with the answer key beside it.

One point the program manager should see. The answer key for these twenty emails ships on the week 7 page, because every module opens on day one and nothing locks. Students can see the correct verdicts. That is fine, because no part of the grade rewards a correct verdict. The graded work is the quality of the note and the honesty of the measurement.

4. Weekly objectives

Numbered as the institutional template requires, under the lead sentence "By the end of this module, you will be able to:". Each one ends with its parent course learning objective.

  • 7.1 Write a six-line triage note for all twenty emails. (CLO5)
  • 7.2 Report your accuracy and your two kinds of error. (CLO6)
  • 7.3 Name one place where a human must still review. (CLO6)

Each is one sentence, under 20 words, with one measurable verb. Each names something the student does in this week's lab. No objective is left without a parent.

The two kinds of error in 7.2 are named, not hidden. They are the false positive and the false negative, defined in the terms list at the top of this page. The lab page names them again beside the objective, and the notebook prints them on separate lines.

An earlier wording read "Report your accuracy, your false positives, and your false negatives." Scored on its own that sentence reads grade 9.55. The ceiling for student-facing text is grade 7.

The build prompt exempts required technical vocabulary from the reading level count, and this page could have claimed that exemption. It does not. The wording was changed instead, the way week 6 changed objective 6.3 rather than excusing it. Section 14 records the measured numbers.

The three of them run to 30 words, which is under the readability tool's 40 word floor, so the tool declines to score the block as it stands. The block was repeated until it cleared the floor and then scored, which is the method week 6 uses.

Repeating text does not move a Flesch-Kincaid grade, because the formula is a ratio of words to sentences and syllables to words. Measured that way, with the parent tags attached, the three objectives read at grade 4.44, with an average sentence of 9.9 words and a longest sentence of 10 words.

5. The sidebars

The week 7 target construct is three operations: looping over a list, counting results in more than one bucket, and calculating a rate. The sidebar rule is one construct per sidebar, so week 7 ships two short sidebars rather than one crowded one. Together they are 25 minutes.

Chosen everyday domain: a recipe yield log. Never email. Never security. No other sidebar in the course uses it. Four bakes, each with the number of rolls the recipe planned and the number the baker actually got.

Sidebar Construct What it shows Links back to
7A Loop over a list and count one bucket Four bakes held as {"planned": ..., "made": ...}, read by key inside a for loop, counting the bakes that hit their planned number Week 0, for loops
7B Count a second bucket, then divide for a rate The same four bakes, counting the two ways a bake missed, then dividing the bakes that hit by the number of bakes and printing the rate Week 0, if/else, and f-strings

7A is ten lines. 7B is sixteen lines. Both run on their own and use only constructs week 0 teaches. Each ends with one ungraded "try changing this" prompt.

In 7A the student adds a fifth bake that hit its planned number, then watches the count move. In 7B the student adds a bake that came up short, then watches which of the two miss counts moves.

Names and data shape. Both sidebars take a list of dictionaries keyed planned and made. 7A defines count_on_target(bakes). 7B defines rise_rate(bakes) and counts into on_target, came_up_short and ran_over. The lab cell defines score_batch(rows, truth) and reads a verdict out of each row. No name is shared, the lab parameter truth appears in neither sidebar, and no other weekly sidebar uses any of these names.

Why pasting a sidebar into the lab fails. The TODO 2 cell has to define score_batch(rows, truth). A student who pastes rise_rate() there gets a NameError as soon as the next pre-written cell calls score_batch. Renaming it does not rescue the paste. The sidebar reads planned and made out of one list, while the lab reads a verdict out of a row and compares it against the answer key, so the next error is a KeyError on the first row.

6. The reading

Title: Writing the note, and measuring the agent. Length: about 1,250 words, in three parts. The budget for a weekly reading is 1,400 words.

The page runs 200 words above the 900 to 1,050 word target the course writing standard sets, though inside its 1,400 word ceiling. Part 3 is the reason: it is survey content for breadth topic B2 and carries seven use cases. Without it the page would sit at about 860 words, inside the target.

Part Words What it covers
1. The note about 480 Who reads a triage note and when. Why the format is fixed. What each of the six lines is for. Why the summary is written for the person who reported the mail, not for another analyst.
2. Measuring about 380 What accuracy is. What a false positive and a false negative are in a reported-mail queue. What each one costs a one-person security team. Why the two counts are never added together. Why escalation agreement is reported on its own line.
3. Where else this goes (breadth topic B2) about 390 Seven jobs people give AI tools in security work: alert triage, log review, phishing analysis, documentation, scripting help, vulnerability research, and incident response support. One short paragraph each, saying what the tool does and what facts it would need first.

Part 3 is the survey content for breadth topic B2. It carries no course objective, no Canvas Outcome and no lab. It sits here because students can only generalize once they have built something.

Two worked examples run inside the reading: one finished triage note, and one score block with the two error types apart. Students read both line by line, which is where much of the reading time goes.

Sources. Four to six citations. The candidates are NIST SP 800-61 on incident handling and documentation, the NIST AI Risk Management Framework on measurement and human oversight, and CISA guidance on reported phishing. Every citation goes through the expert review the build prompt requires, and every link is verified to resolve before the reading ships. Any claim that review cannot ground is flagged rather than quietly kept.

7. The discussion prompt

Graded. One post and one substantive reply. This is also where breadth topic B2 is assessed outside the final exam. Written out below exactly as students will see it.

Your numbers, and one other job for an agent

A use case is one job people give an AI tool.

Open your week 7 report. Find three numbers: your accuracy, your false positive count, and your false negative count.

Post by Thursday. Do three things.

  1. Give your three numbers. Name one email your agent got wrong. Say which error it was.
  2. Pick one use case from this week's reading. Do not pick phishing. Your choices are alert triage, log review, documentation, scripting help, vulnerability research, or incident response support.
  3. Name one part of your agent you would have to change for that use case. Say what the change is. Say why it is needed.

Then reply to one classmate by Sunday at 11:59 PM Central. Pick someone who chose a different use case. Say one thing their change would break or fix in your own agent. Use one number from your own report. A reply that only agrees does not count.

Keep your post near 150 words. Keep your reply near 60 words.

You may use AI help on this post. If you do, add one line saying what you asked it.

The prompt cannot be answered from opinion. Every part of it needs the student's own run. Two students will not have the same three numbers, because the model varies run to run.

8. What students submit

Three items, in one Canvas assignment. Nothing else. No folders, no links, no compressed files.

  1. One screenshot. The printed score report from cell 14. It shows accuracy, false positives, false negatives, and escalation agreement, each on its own line. Escalation agreement prints there because report_scores() writes it, so one cell holds every number the rubric asks for. Students are told to capture that one cell's output, top to bottom, with the numbers readable. No cell in any week displays key material, so no screenshot can leak one.
  2. One file. week7_triage_report.txt, written by the export cell. The cell prints the full path and the first few lines, so the student sees exactly what to attach.
  3. A written answer of 3 to 5 sentences, typed into the text box. The prompt reads:

Which error is worse for a one-person security team, a false positive or a false negative? Use your own counts from this run. Name one email your agent got wrong. Say where you would keep a human analyst in the loop. Say why.

All three items are gradeable without opening the student's notebook.

9. Rubric sketch

Three criteria, 100 points, four performance levels each. Criterion numbering matches the alignment matrix. Every weekly lab in this course is worth 100 points, so the seven labs carry equal weight inside the Labs group.

# Criterion Points Levels Canvas Outcome
1 Submission completeness. The screenshot, the file and the written answer are all present and readable. 8 8 / 6 / 4 / 0 None. Declared a non-CLO expectation.
2 Triage note. All six lines present in order for all twenty emails. Each indicator names a fact your code pulled out. The recommended action fits the verdict. A reader with no security background can follow the summary. 36 36 / 27 / 18 / 0 CLO5: Document a triage decision
3 Accuracy and human review. Your accuracy number matches the file you attached. The two kinds of error are counted apart. Escalation agreement has its own line. Your written answer names where a human must still review, and cites your own accuracy figure and at least one of your own wrong emails. 56 56 / 42 / 28 / 0 CLO6: Measure triage accuracy

An earlier draft had a fourth criterion, and it was folded into criterion 3. That criterion, "Own results used", was worth 15 points and carried no Outcome link. It is gone, and its requirement now sits in the last sentence of criterion 3.

The reason is a course rule rather than a week 7 preference. Clause 4 of the rubric link rule in the alignment matrix says a criterion scoring a student's use of their own results stays inside the Outcome-linked criterion. Measuring with your own numbers is CLO6 performance, not a separate expectation.

The academic integrity purpose survives the merge. A submission that cites no accuracy figure of its own, and names no wrong email of its own, still cannot reach the top level of criterion 3.

The points moved with the requirement. Criterion 3 was 40 and is now 56. Criterion 2 moved from 35 to 36 and criterion 1 from 10 to 8, so that every Outcome-linked criterion is an exact multiple of the four-point mastery scale and the lab still totals 100.

The four levels use the same wording across the course: exceeds standard, meets standard, approaching standard, no evidence. They map onto the mastery scale in the alignment matrix with no translation step.

The lab totals 165 minutes, inside the 2.5 to 3 hour band. Every other line sits inside its own band. The week is under the 6 hour ceiling with 73 minutes of slack, which is 360 minus 287.

One warning about the batch wait. Sixty-five model calls at roughly 1.3 seconds each is 85 seconds of machine time, still under two minutes.

The default reasoning mode is far slower. The same sixty-five calls take 15 to 22 seconds each. That is 16 to 24 minutes of a student sitting and watching, which alone would push the lab over its band. This week therefore supports the recommendation to run with reasoning switched off.

12. Token estimate for one full lab run

One triage sends about 5,800 characters of prompt. Five of the parts are shared with earlier weeks and are measured once for the whole blueprint, in section 2 of the grade distribution and token totals document.

Those add to 5,785 characters, which at four characters per token is 1,446 tokens per call.

A full batch makes 65 model calls. Forty-five are calls where the model asks for a tool. The other twenty are closing calls, one per email, where the model writes the answer that fills the note.

Every email gets a closing call, including the five that stop early on a decisive tool result. That is why the count is 65 rather than 60.

Each later call in one email re-sends the earlier turns, which adds 79 increments of about 88 tokens. The 79 follows from the tool-request distribution the corpus manifest sets, so it can be checked line by line.

Tool requests the email makes Emails Calls each, one closing call included History increments each Increments
1 4 2 1 4
2 7 3 3 21
3 9 4 6 54
Total 20 65 calls 79

The check: 4 plus 14 plus 27 is 45 tool requests, and 8 plus 21 plus 36 is 65 calls. So 65 x 1,446 + 79 x 88 = 100,942 prompt tokens.

Completions add 45 x 34 + 20 x 160 = 4,730 tokens, and the one check-cell run before the batch adds about 5,900 more. A full week 7 lab run is about 112,000 tokens. Retries push it toward 120,000. This is the second heaviest week in the course, behind week 6.

If the reasoning model is left in its default mode instead, completions rise to roughly 155 tokens for a tool request and roughly 1,110 for a closing note. That is 45 x 155 + 20 x 1,110, or 29,175 completion tokens, the check-cell run rises to about 9,600, and the run reaches about 140,000 tokens.

Section 13 lists which of these inputs were measured and which are projected.

13. Assumptions and open questions

  1. Some token inputs are measured and some are projected. The measured ones are the playbook snippet at 1,366 characters, the answer schema, the known-good domain list, the three tool definitions, the facts block, and the mean rendered body. The playbook figure is the byte count of the corpus playbook file. The 34-token tool-call completion is measured too, from the serving stack probe. - Projected: the guard rules, the loop instructions, the 160-token closing completion, the 88-token turn increment, and the check-cell run. - The serving stack recorded 65 to 79 completion tokens for a four-key JSON answer, and 27 to 47 for a short answer after a tool result. Nothing measured reaches 160. That figure is a projection for the larger six-field note, and Phase 2 should measure it. - One more caution sits outside this list. The chat template inflates tool definitions beyond their character count, so every call that carries TOOL_SPECS costs more than the table above says. Section 3.2 of the grade distribution and token totals document carries the course-wide allowance for that.
  2. Body length is a mean over twelve rendered emails. Only one of the twenty batch emails is rendered today. Across the twelve files that exist the plain bodies run 258 to 1,129 characters, with a mean of 729, counted with line endings normalised. If the remaining nineteen run longer, the token figure rises in proportion.
  3. Reading minutes are built from the one course rate. The course prices one reading pass at 200 words a minute, which is section 2 of the survey content plan, and prices anything a page asks beyond that as its own named line. Week 7 reads 1,250 words in 6 minutes at that rate, then adds 26 minutes for the two worked examples and 10 for opening one cited source, giving 42. The build prompt asks for this to be checked against the real week 1 and week 2 builds, and it should be.
  4. Sixty-five calls assumes the expected loop paths hold. The count comes from the corpus manifest: forty-five tool requests across the twenty emails, plus one closing call each. By stop reason, fifteen emails run their tools out and five stop early on a decisive result. By tool count, which is what the history arithmetic needs, four emails make one request, seven make two and nine make three. The two views agree, because an email runs out of tools as soon as no untried tool has anything to look at. A model that asks for tools in a different order changes the count, not the shape.
  5. The closing call pins a behavior week 6 left implicit. The code ledger describes the week 5 loop as stopping on a decisive tool result without asking the model again. Week 7 needs a schema answer for every email, so guarded_triage() makes one closing call whatever the stop reason. The ledger records that, and the week 6 spec's own call count and token arithmetic are built on it.
  6. Open question for the program manager. The answer key ships to students on the week 7 page. That follows from the rule that nothing locks. If the program would rather the key stayed with the instructor, the week still works, but the wrong-answer table has to be replaced with a graded comparison the instructor runs later.

Measured readability

Measured with the course readability gate on 2026-09-04. This is a program manager document, so the whole page is held to grade 9.5, an average sentence of 18 words and no sentence over 45. Student-facing text drafted inside it is held to grade 7 and an average sentence of 12 words.

Text measured Flesch-Kincaid grade Average sentence Longest sentence
This whole page 6.60 14.1 words 38 words
The three objectives, section 4 4.44 9.9 words 10 words
The discussion prompt, section 7 3.03 8.3 words 20 words
The written answer prompt, section 8 4.24 10.2 words 16 words
The rubric criterion column, section 9 6.59 9.6 words 25 words

No prose paragraph on this page runs over 70 words.

How the short blocks were scored. The readability tool declines to score anything under 40 words. A block under that floor was repeated until it cleared it and then scored, which is the method week 6 uses. Repeating text does not move a Flesch-Kincaid grade, because the formula is a ratio of words to sentences and syllables to words. A rubric row is the criterion column, each cell read as one line, with the header row left out.

Two numbers that moved, and one exemption not claimed. Objective 7.2 read "Report your accuracy, your false positives, and your false negatives." Scored on its own that sentence reads grade 9.55. It now reads "Report your accuracy and your two kinds of error." and scores 6.28. Section 4 says where the two kinds are named.

The rubric criterion column read grade 7.99. Criterion 2 and criterion 3 were reworded and the column now reads 6.59.

The build prompt exempts required technical vocabulary from the reading level count. No exemption is claimed on this page. "Accuracy", "false positive", "false negative", "triage note" and "escalation agreement" are all counted in full in the numbers above, and every one of them is defined in the terms list at the top of the page.

Code accumulation ledger7,991 words · code-ledger.md

Here is the promise students are given in week 1. Your work in the notebook server is saved for the whole term, so nothing you write disappears. On top of that, every week's starter notebook already contains a working version of everything through the week before. You carry your own code forward wherever it works. Where it does not, the starter notebook's version is right there and runs.

A student who never finished week 4 can still open week 5 and build the loop. A bad week costs you that week's points. It does not end the course. This ledger is what keeps that promise true.

1. What this document is for

The course builds one phishing triage agent across weeks 1 to 7. Each week adds exactly one capability. This ledger is the contract for that build. It names every function and value in the notebook, says who writes it and in which week, and names the file each week submits.

Three groups need it. The notebook author builds from it. The tools author keeps triage_tools.py matching it. The grader knows from it what a correct submission looks like without opening a student's notebook.

Terms used below. A cell is one block in a notebook that a student runs on its own. A TODO cell is a cell with a comment marking the lines the student writes. Pre-written means the cell arrives complete and working. A signature is the function's name plus the values it takes and returns.

The serving stack is the software that runs the model and answers requests; the course tested one of these and wrote down what it returned, in builder/docs/serving-stack-shape.md. A reasoning model writes out its thinking before its answer; that thinking is a reasoning block, and the course strips it off. The context window is the most text the model can hold in one request.

2. The parts that never change

2.1 The configuration cell

Every notebook, week 1 through week 7, opens with the same pre-written configuration cell. Nothing in it is edited during a lab.

Name Value Note
BASE_URL the chat completions base address One variable, no provider name in the code
MODEL the model name One variable
MODEL_EXTRAS dictionary of extra request fields Merged into every request
API_KEY os.environ.get("ITSY1377_API_KEY", "") Read from the environment, never typed, never printed
EMAIL_DIR folder holding the .eml files
DATA_DIR folder holding the shipped data files

MODEL_EXTRAS exists because the two candidate models disagree about one request field. The reasoning model accepts reasoning_effort and the other model rejects it with an error. The dictionary holds {"reasoning_effort": "none"} for the first and {} for the second, so one notebook runs against either. This is recorded in the serving stack document.

The cell prints one line saying whether a key was found. It never prints the key, and no cell in any week does.

2.2 The token counter

Where it lives: in a pre-written cell in the notebook itself, directly below the configuration cell, in every week. It is not hidden in an imported module. Students can read it, and the number is on screen without anyone importing anything.

How it works. Every reply from the model carries a usage block with three numbers: tokens in the prompt, tokens in the answer, and the total. The pre-written send_request() reads those three numbers straight from the reply and adds them into two dictionaries.

  • USAGE counts the whole notebook session. show_tokens() prints it. reset_tokens() sets it back to zero.
  • RUN_USAGE counts one triage. start_run() sets it back to zero at the top of each email. Week 6 uses it for the per-run token cap. It exists from week 1 so that send_request() is the same code in all seven notebooks.

Two rules the counter follows, both learned from the serving stack tests.

First, it reads the reported numbers and never measures the length of the answer text. On the reasoning model the tokens spent thinking are counted in the answer total but never appear in the text, so measuring the text would under-report by roughly ten times.

Second, when a reply carries no usage block, the counter adds nothing and prints "token count not reported" instead. A missing field is visible rather than silent.

Every call prints one short line: the tokens for that call, and the running total. LAST_CALL holds the stop reason, any reasoning text and the raw reply from the most recent call, so a student can look at a failure without changing any code.

2.3 The standing cell order

Every starter notebook uses the same order, so week 5 looks like week 2.

  1. Markdown: the week, what you add, and the continuity note.
  2. Code, pre-written: imports and configuration.
  3. Code, pre-written: the token counter.
  4. Code, pre-written: send_request() and save_artifact().
  5. Code, pre-written: working code from earlier weeks. Weeks 2 to 7 only.
  6. Markdown: what the new function has to do.
  7. Code, TODO: the student writes it.
  8. Code, pre-written: a check cell that runs the TODO cell on one known email and prints what a correct result looks like.
  9. Steps 6 to 8 repeat where a week has two TODO cells.
  10. Code, pre-written: the full run for the week.
  11. Code, pre-written: the export cell.
  12. Markdown: what to submit, naming the file and the screenshot.

Cell 5 carries a short markdown note above it: these are reference versions of what you wrote before, and you may paste your own over them.

2.4 Two helpers that arrive in week 1 and never change

send_request(payload) is the only place in the course that talks to the network. It adds the key header, sends the request, reads the reply, records the tokens, and turns a rejected request into a sentence a student can act on. It returns the whole reply as a dictionary.

save_artifact(filename, content) writes the submission file. Given a dictionary it writes indented JSON. Given a string it writes text. Either way it prints the full path and the first few lines, so the student sees exactly what to attach.

4. Week 2: strict JSON, validated, with a retry

Capability added. The model answers in JSON with a fixed set of keys. Your code checks the answer against a schema and asks again when it is wrong.

Emails used. 001, 002, 003 and 004.

What the student writes.

Cell Signature Lines What it does
TODO 1 extract_json(text) 5 to 7 Calls strip_reasoning(), tries json.loads(), returns the dictionary or None
TODO 2 classify_email(parsed, max_tries=3) 8 to 10 Builds the prompt, calls ask_model(), extracts, checks with schema_errors(), logs the try, asks again with the problems attached, returns the checked dictionary or None

A design note the program manager should see. The build prompt says students validate against a schema. The mechanical walk of a schema is not a 5 to 15 line job, so schema_errors() arrives pre-written. The student wires it in and decides what to do with what it returns, which is the part that teaches the lesson. The retry loop is theirs.

What arrives pre-written this week.

Name and signature What it does
OUTPUT_SCHEMA The required answer shape: verdict, category, confidence, indicators, with the allowed values
CLASSIFY_INSTRUCTIONS The fixed instruction text naming the keys and the allowed values
build_classify_prompt(parsed) Joins the instructions and the email body into one prompt string
strip_reasoning(text) Removes a leading reasoning block, a stray closing reasoning tag, and code fences
schema_errors(data, schema) Returns a list of plain-language problems, empty when the answer is good
ATTEMPT_LOG A list holding every try, good or bad
log_attempt(email_id, try_number, raw_text, problems) Appends one try to ATTEMPT_LOG

OUTPUT_SCHEMA is rendered from corpus/spec/enums.json, so the category list the model is asked for is the same list week 7 scores against.

strip_reasoning() is pre-written because the serving stack tests found three places a reasoning block can appear and one model that produces none at all. It handles all of them, so a change of server does not break a student's work.

Everything that exists at the end of week 2. Everything from week 1, plus the seven pre-written items above, plus extract_json() and classify_email(), which the student wrote this week.

Submission artifact. week2_classification.json, written by the export cell in step 15. Holds, per email: the id, the checked answer, schema_ok, problems, and how many tries it took. schema_ok and problems are written on the accepted try as well as on the failed ones, so an email that passed first time still shows that the check ran. problems is an empty list when the check found nothing wrong.

The file also holds the session token totals, the model name, and a sample_checks block: the four saved replies from cell 11, one clean and three broken, each with what extract_json() returned and what schema_errors() reported. Three recorded failures are therefore in every submitted file. The samples are saved text, so the block adds no model calls to the counts below.

Model calls in a full run. Four to twelve, depending on retries. Five is typical.

5. Week 3: grounding

Capability added. Stop sending the raw body alone. Build a facts block from the parsed headers, the authentication results, the display-name check, the link hosts, a playbook snippet and the known-good internal domains, and send that instead.

Emails used. 001, 004, 005, 006, 017 and 025. Emails 005 and 025 are the matched pair: grounding has to correct one from malicious to benign and the other from benign to malicious, on the same code and the same prompt.

These two carry the primary evidence for course learning objective 1. TODO 1 is where a student decides which facts a sound judgment needs, which is the week's data minimization lesson: what you put in the prompt is what you expose. TODO 2 is the exact construct the week 3 sidebar prepares.

6. Week 4: tools

Capability added. The model asks for one of three tools by name. Your code runs it and hands the result back.

Emails used. 003, 008, 009, 017, 018 and 019. Each was chosen because one tool alone changes its verdict.

The three tools. They ship in lab/tools/triage_tools.py and are imported, never written by students. Each returns exactly the block recorded for that email in the corpus manifest, so the answer key and the tool cannot disagree.

Signature What it returns
check_sender_reputation(sender) The reputation record for one address or domain: status, match_level, new_domain, first_seen, messages_90d, prior_reports, last_report_verdict, last_report_date, campaign_tag, notes, and domain or address depending on which matched
check_urls(hosts) One entry per host, each allowlisted, blocklisted, internal or unlisted
lookup_recipient(address) The recipient record: name, role, mailbox_type, executive, handles_payments, chronic_reporter, report_count_90d, true_positive_count_90d, campaign_tags

status is one of internal, trusted, known_good, neutral, unknown or known_bad. match_level is one of address, domain, organizational_domain or none, and it says which lookup succeeded. Two records in the corpus match on an address and carry no domain key, so code that reads the result must use a safe lookup rather than assume the key.

TODO 2 tool_result_message(tool_call, result) 3 to 4 Builds the message that carries a tool result back to the model, with the call id copied across

run_tool() is the exact construct week 4 sidebar 4b prepares: choosing which function to run based on a name. Sidebar 4a prepares the function definition it sits inside.

triage_with_tools() also carries the fallback path the build prompt requires. The endpoint has no way to force a tool call. When the model answers in prose instead of asking for a tool, the function falls back to week 2's manual parsing and records that it did. That fallback fired in none of nine tests, but nothing prevents it, so students see it as a normal path rather than a bug.

7. Week 5: the bounded loop

Capability added. An observe, decide, act loop with a hard cap of four steps and an explicit stop condition. The agent picks its next tool from what it just learned.

Emails used. 004, 005, 007, 010, 011 and 012. Each produces a different loop shape. Email 011 stops after one step because the sender is internal. 007 stops after one step because the sender is known bad. 005 and 010 stop after two. 004 has no link at all, so a student sees the wrong second choice punished. 012 gets nothing decisive from any tool, so it runs all three and stops with no tool left. The cap of four is the guard that catches a repeated tool call.

This is the hardest week in the course for students with no programming background, and it carries the primary evidence for course learning objective 3. It therefore gets more pre-written scaffolding than any other week, and only one TODO cell.

What the student writes.

Cell Signature Lines What it does
TODO 1 run_loop(parsed, prompt_text, max_steps=MAX_STEPS, step_guard=None) 13 to 15 Counts steps, asks the model, stops when it answers, runs the tool it asked for, checks for a decisive stop, asks the supplied guard whether the run may take another step, and stops when no tool is left or a cap is reached

The loop uses only constructs week 0 covers: a counter, a while, an if, a return. Every step inside it is one call to a pre-written helper, so the student writes the shape of the loop and nothing else.

step_guard is the fourth parameter, and it is empty in week 5. It is the growth hook that lets week 6 add a per-run token cap without reopening this cell.

The student writes one if that calls guard_says_stop(step_guard) and stops the run when it says so. In week 5 nothing is passed, the helper always answers no, and the line never fires.

Week 6 passes over_token_cap in from guarded_triage(), and the same line starts firing. The student's code is never edited.

What arrives pre-written this week.

Name and signature What it does
MAX_STEPS The hard cap, set to 4
LOOP_INSTRUCTIONS Text telling the model it may ask for one tool per step and must answer when it has enough
loop_prompt(parsed) Joins LOOP_INSTRUCTIONS to week 3's grounded prompt for one email
new_trace(parsed) Makes an empty record of the run: email id, usable tools, steps, stop reason, final answer, tokens
first_call(calls, answer, trace) Picks the one tool call the loop acts on out of the list the endpoint returns, reads its arguments out of the JSON string, and covers the model answering with no tool at all
append_turn(messages, turn, tool_call, result, trace) Adds the model's turn and the tool result to the message list, and writes the step into the trace
decisive_stop(tool_name, result, parsed) Returns a stop reason when one tool result settles the case, otherwise None
tools_left(trace) Names which of the three tools have not run yet and still have something to look at. check_urls is not left when the email has no link host
guard_says_stop(step_guard) Answers whether a supplied guard says the run has spent its budget. Answers no when no guard was supplied
out_of_room(trace) Names why a loop ended when nothing settled the case: tools_exhausted when no usable tool is left, step_cap when one is still unused
show_trace(trace) Prints one run as a readable table, one row per step

Two more helpers ship inside these cells and are never called from the TODO cell: usable_tools(parsed), which new_trace() calls once, and one_line(result), which append_turn() calls to shorten a tool result for the trace table. Every one of these is written out in section 15 of docs/phase1-blueprint/week-05-spec.md, which is the week 5 lab in full.

decisive_stop() is what makes the stop condition explicit rather than a guess. It returns internal_sender when the mail never left the organization, decisive_malicious when a link host is blocklisted or a sender is known bad, and decisive_benign when an allowlisted sender and an allowlisted host agree.

The five stop reasons match the list the corpus scores against: decisive_malicious, decisive_benign, internal_sender, tools_exhausted and step_cap. There is no sixth value anywhere in the course.

tools_left() counts a tool as left only when it still has something to look at. An email with no link host in it has nothing for check_urls to check, so that tool is not left and the run ends one step earlier. Fifteen of the corpus records stop on tools_exhausted after two tools for exactly this reason, and the corpus figures every week quotes are counted that way.

Everything that exists at the end of week 5. Everything from weeks 1 to 4, plus the eleven pre-written items above, plus run_loop(), which the student wrote this week.

Cells in the starter notebook. Eighteen cells.

  1. Markdown: the week, and a one-line continuity reminder.
  2. Code, pre-written: imports and configuration.
  3. Code, pre-written: the token counter.
  4. Code, pre-written: send_request() and save_artifact().
  5. Code, pre-written: working code from weeks 1 to 4.
  6. Markdown: what a loop is here, drawn as four steps with a cap.
  7. Code, pre-written: MAX_STEPS, TOOL_NAMES, LOOP_INSTRUCTIONS, loop_prompt(), usable_tools(), new_trace().
  8. Code, pre-written: one hand-written round of the loop, unrolled, with every variable printed, so students see one pass before writing many.
  9. Code, pre-written: one_line(), first_call(), append_turn(), decisive_stop(), tools_left(), guard_says_stop(), out_of_room(), show_trace().
  10. Code, pre-written: each helper called once on its own, with its output printed.
  11. Markdown: the loop in six numbered sentences, in the order the code runs.
  12. Code, TODO 1: run_loop().
  13. Code, pre-written: check cell. Runs the loop on 011 alone, the shortest case, and prints the trace.
  14. Code, pre-written: runs the loop on all six emails and prints all six traces.
  15. Code, pre-written: a deliberately runaway version with the cap removed and a hard stop at twenty, printing the token cost of the difference.
  16. Markdown: what the runaway run cost, and why the cap is not optional.
  17. Code, pre-written: the export cell.
  18. Markdown: what to submit.

Submission artifact. week5_loop_trace.json, written by the export cell in step 17. Holds, per email: the id, the ordered steps with the tool name, the arguments and a one-line result for each, the stop reason, the number of steps used, the final verdict, and the tokens that email spent. Plus the session token totals and the runaway run's token count for comparison.

Model calls in a full run. About eighteen to thirty, plus the runaway demonstration.

8. Week 6: guardrails and human review

Capability added. Stop trusting the text the agent reads and the answer it produces. The fenced prompt, the fact check, the confidence floor and the token cap are four parts of that one control, not four capabilities. The week 6 spec argues the point in full.

The whole email body is text an attacker wrote. Prompt injection is therefore this agent's real threat, and this is the course's differentiator week.

Emails used. Eleven. 013 carries the required body injection and 024 is its word-for-word twin without the payload, so a student measures what the injection changed on their own model. 014 hides the payload in the HTML part. 020 fakes the agent's own tool output. 021 imitates the mail gateway's banner. 022 puts the payload in the subject and the display name. 023 forges gateway headers. 015 is a benign decoy holding instruction-shaped text inside a quote, which a keyword detector gets wrong. 016 is the case where the model says benign and an extracted fact says otherwise. 009 and 012 are the low-confidence cases the escalation threshold has to catch.

What the student writes.

Cell Signature Lines What it does
TODO 1 build_guarded_prompt(facts_text, body) 6 to 8 Wraps the body between the two markers, puts the rules before it and the required answer keys after it, and keeps any hidden HTML text on the facts side
TODO 2 check_against_facts(result, facts) 6 to 8 Returns a list of problems where the answer contradicts the extracted facts, empty when it does not

check_against_facts() is the exact construct the week 6 sidebar prepares: comparing two values and checking one against known facts. A correct version catches at least three contradictions: a benign verdict while a link host is blocklisted, a benign verdict while the sender is known bad, and an indicator naming a host that is not in the extracted list.

What arrives pre-written this week.

Name and signature What it does
BODY_START, BODY_END The two markers that fence off the attacker's text
GUARD_RULES The sentences telling the model the fenced block is evidence to judge, never an instruction to follow
CONFIDENCE_FLOOR The threshold below which a case goes to a human, set to 0.7
TOKEN_CAP_PER_RUN The most tokens one triage may spend, set to 4000
over_token_cap() Returns true when RUN_USAGE has passed the cap
guarded_triage(parsed, schema=OUTPUT_SCHEMA) One full guarded triage: build facts, build the guarded prompt, run the week 5 loop with over_token_cap as its step guard, make one closing model call for the schema answer whatever stopped the loop, apply the fact check, the confidence floor and the token cap, return a verdict with an escalation decision
compare_guarded(email_ids) Runs the guarded and unguarded paths over a list of emails and returns one row per email

guarded_triage() returns whatever keys the schema it was handed names, plus escalate, escalate_reason, problems, stop_reason and tokens. Called with the default OUTPUT_SCHEMA it returns verdict, confidence, indicators and those five. Week 7 hands it OUTPUT_SCHEMA_V2 and gets recommended_action and summary in the same dictionary, without editing this function or any week 6 cell.

escalate_reason is one of five plain values: low_confidence, fact_contradiction, injection_detected, token_cap or none.

Two escalation vocabularies, kept apart on purpose. These five are the agent's own reasons, and they are deliberately not the ten ground-truth escalation reasons the corpus records in corpus/spec/enums.json. The corpus reasons say why a human analyst would escalate. The agent's reasons say which of its own controls fired. Only injection_detected appears in both lists. Week 7 therefore scores escalation agreement on the yes-or-no flag alone and never compares the two reason strings.

The per-run cap uses the counter from section 2.2. start_run() zeroes RUN_USAGE at the top of each triage. guarded_triage() then passes over_token_cap into run_loop() as its step_guard, so the check runs between steps without any week 5 cell being reopened. The student's loop calls guard_says_stop(step_guard), which it has called since week 5.

A run that hits the cap records the stop reason step_cap and escalates with escalate_reason set to token_cap. step_cap is the corpus value for a run a cap ended rather than a decision, and escalate_reason says which cap fired.

No new stop reason is invented. A week 6 export file and every week 7 batch row therefore carry only values the corpus, the week 5 rubric and the week 5 self-check already treat as valid.

This is a cap on one triage inside the lab. It is not the course token budget, which the instructor controls and which never cuts a student off.

Everything that exists at the end of week 6. Everything from weeks 1 to 5, plus the seven pre-written items above, plus build_guarded_prompt() and check_against_facts(), which the student wrote this week.

  1. Markdown: the week, and a one-line continuity reminder.
  2. Code, pre-written: imports and configuration.
  3. Code, pre-written: the token counter.
